Start · README.md

Overview

Neovim, for working. Leader is a fast, opinionated Neovim config with its own GPU window (Rust + wgpu). It starts in about 20 ms, gives you Zed-like menus, projects with their sessions and an inline AI agent that runs on the backend you choose. MIT, no telemetry, no account.

Linux · WSL · macOS · Windows — the app on Linux/WSL, the config everywhere.

Contents · Install · Why Leader · What's included · Keys · Community

Install

curl -fsSL https://leader.sazardev.stream/install | bash

Windows (PowerShell): irm https://leader.sazardev.stream/install.ps1 | iex

That is the whole install: on Linux x86_64 it installs the desktop app, Neovim and the config; on macOS and Windows it installs the config. It asks one thing — which language packs to set up — and can be re-run at any time. Prefer a package? The .deb is one click; the installer details are below.

Why Leader

  • Fast by default. ~20 ms to a usable editor. Every plugin waits for an event, a filetype or a key; nothing is resident until it has a reason to be.
  • One file, one responsibility. A language is one file, a plugin is one file, an action is one file. You can read the whole thing and change one piece without a framework.
  • Everything is a key. <Space> opens the menu for where you are, <Space>Sp changes the settings without editing a file, and every key is in CHEATSHEET.md, generated from the real keymaps.
  • Local first, no telemetry. Static analysis and the completion helpers cost zero tokens. The agent runs on your CLI or a local model, only when you ask; no account, no analytics.
  • A real desktop, not a wrapper. The same Neovim in its own GPU window: menus, a command palette, projects with sessions, a toolbar and a theme inherited from your terminal.
  • Open and yours. MIT, Lua for the config and Rust for the app. Fork it, vendor it, change the opinions.

What the installer does

The short link is a tiny script served by Cloudflare Pages: it picks the desktop package on Linux x86_64 and the config installer everywhere else, so the same command is copy-paste on every machine. LEADER_CONFIG_URL and LEADER_RELEASE_URL point it elsewhere if you mirror the assets.

It asks one thing: which languages to set up, as a checklist of packs (Go, Dart / Flutter, TypeScript / JavaScript, C# / .NET, Web, Python, Rust, SQL, Swift, Java / Kotlin, Docker / Make / Prisma…) with the ones this machine already uses checked; Enter keeps them. Only the languages you keep download their LSPs, formatters and linters (the base, Lua, Markdown, JSON, YAML, TOML, is always on). Change them later in <Space>Sp → Languages. No clone needed: it fetches the repo into ~/.local/share/leader and does everything else (idempotent: run it again any time to bring it up to date):

  1. installs whatever is missing from the system (git, curl, C compiler, ripgrep, node, cmake) and Neovim ≥ 0.11 if you don't have it (in ~/.local/opt, without sudo; with brew on macOS);
  2. links nvim/ as your config (~/.config/nvim); an existing one is moved to nvim.bak-<date>, never deleted;
  3. enables the git hooks (.githooks: cheatsheet always up to date and conventional commits);
  4. writes your per-machine settings (nvim/lua/local.lua, git-ignored): the languages you chose and the options below;
  5. installs the plugins at the versions pinned in nvim/lazy-lock.json (in parallel), the LSPs/formatters/linters (Mason) and builds the treesitter parsers (tree-sitter-cli comes from Mason), reporting the time each step took;
  6. creates the leader command (~/.local/bin/leader: in a terminal it is nvim itself, same arguments and exit code);
  7. sets up the AI agent with the best backend it finds (Claude Code, OpenCode, Ollama…); --agent none skips it.

Options go after bash -s --, for example a Go laptop, fully configured:

curl -fsSL https://leader.sazardev.stream/install | bash -s -- --lang go,sql --theme gruvbox

Prefer to clone it yourself (to hack on it)? git clone https://github.com/sazardev/leader && cd leader && ./install.sh does exactly the same. Override the fetch with NVIM_CONFIG_DIR, NVIM_CONFIG_REPO and NVIM_CONFIG_BRANCH.

Then open nvim — or leader, which the installer also creates (~/.local/bin/leader): in a terminal it is nvim itself. Press <Space> and which-key shows you the menus.

There is also a landing page, docs/index.html (flat and square like the config, no build, self-hosted Geist in docs/fonts/, dark/light with t), and the documentation at docs/docs/index.html (generated from the Markdown by scripts/gen-docs.sh). Both are live at https://leader.sazardev.stream/ on Cloudflare Pages (npx wrangler pages deploy docs --project-name leader --branch=master; see AGENTS.md for the short links, headers and SEO files). To try them locally: cd docs && python3 -m http.server.

OptionEffect
--askprompts before installing system packages or Neovim (by default it asks nothing)
--lang python,sqlthose packs (go dart typescript csharp web python rust sql swift jvm infra yarnspinner) or single languages, all for every one; skips the checklist
--yesasks nothing: the packs found on this machine (also what happens with no terminal)
--theme catppuccindefault theme (local.lua)
--light / --no-lightforces light mode (no resident LSP) or turns it off; without this it is detected by RAM (≤ 2 GB)
--agent autothe AI agent (auto is the default and picks the best available), claude/haiku, ollama/qwen2.5-coder:7b@light…; none skips it
--no-depsdoesn't touch system packages or Neovim
--no-pluginsonly links; plugins and tools install themselves the first time you open nvim
--dry-runshows what it would do
./install.sh --lang go,sql --theme gruvbox --agent claude/haiku   # from a clone: a Go laptop, everything configured
./install.sh --dry-run --no-deps --lang rust                      # see what it would do

Single languages also work with --lang: lua go typescript csharp html css tailwind json yaml toml markdown docker prisma astro dart rust python java kotlin sql swift make yarnspinner. If local.lua already exists it is left untouched: the installer tells you what to add (it is yours and may hold other settings).

Native Windows (PowerShell): ./install.ps1 [-Lang go,typescript] [-Theme x] [-Light|-NoLight] [-Agent auto] [-NoPlugins] creates the junction at %LOCALAPPDATA%\nvim and does the same steps (written but not tested on real Windows; on WSL use install.sh).

Uninstall: delete the ~/.config/nvim link (and, if you want, ~/.local/share/nvim, ~/.local/state/nvim, ~/.cache/nvim).

Desktop app (Linux, WSL)

The same editor in its own window: leader, a GPU front-end for Neovim written in Rust (app/). It runs your Neovim with this config, so everything above works the same, but it draws the screen itself instead of a terminal: one draw call per frame, nothing running between keystrokes, and the flat design's half cells and borders drawn as exact rectangles. Add --app to the installer (or run app/install.sh from a clone): it installs Rust with rustup if needed, builds leader and adds Leader to the application menu (~/.local/bin/leader, ~/.local/share/applications).

From a release, in one command (Linux x86_64, glibc 2.31+: Ubuntu 20.04, Debian 11, Fedora 32 and newer; WSL with WSLg): it downloads the package, checks it, installs the app and, when they are missing, Neovim and this config; the first launch shows the welcome screen.

curl -fsSL https://github.com/sazardev/leader/releases/latest/download/leader-installer.sh | bash

From a package: Debian, Ubuntu, Mint, Pop!_OS download leader-editor-amd64.deb from the latest release and sudo apt install ./leader-editor-amd64.deb; Arch, CachyOS, Manjaro use the PKGBUILDs in packaging/arch (leader-editor-bin prebuilt, leader-editor from source; makepkg -si). The packages add the menu entry, the icons, the manual (man leader) and leader-setup, which installs the config for your user (the first launch with no Neovim config runs it in the window). Every release is signed and checksummed: how to verify it in packaging/README.md.

From a clone:

./install.sh --app          # the config + the desktop app (built from source)
app/install.sh --uninstall  # remove the app (the config stays)
  • In a terminal, leader [files…] is nvim itself (any nvim argument works); from the menu (or with leader --gui) it opens the window. Files dropped on the window open in it.
  • The system clipboard works on its own ("+y, "+p, Ctrl+Shift+V), no wl-copy/xclip/OSC 52 needed.
  • Its own title bar, painted with the editor's background so it is part of it (it steps back while a modal such as Telescope is open), in a smaller type than the code, laid out like Zed's. Leader's dotted N (it ripples on hover; also F10) opens the app menu: File, Edit, Selection, View, Go, Run, Git (inside a repo), Agent, Window and Help, each a submenu whose items do exactly what Leader's own keys do and show them, plus the command palette (Ctrl+Shift+P: every menu item and every key of the config in one search). The project's name opens the projects menu (every folder you worked in, newest first; switching saves the session and restores the other project's, unsaved files are never dropped) and Open Project… (Ctrl+Shift+O) a full-screen picker: your recent projects and every git project under your home, with the one under the cursor described on the right (branch and changes, last commit, when you opened it, whether a session brings your windows back, its stack, its README); ⏎ opens it, o in a new window, d forgets it, plus a folder by path, a new project or a clone. ⌂ goes to the start screen, ↺ opens your recent files. Center: the current file's path, every part clickable. Right, live: what is running (LSP indexing with its progress, installs, a save: a dot spinner and a thin progress line), what is left to install, a macro being recorded, the file's git changes, unusual encodings or line endings; then minimize · maximize · close as three small flat squares. Configure it in gui.appbar: controls, buttons = { mark, project, home, history }, activity, show, enabled = false for the system's title bar.
  • Full mouse: everything you can choose follows the pointer and answers a click. Menus light up the item under the pointer and open submenus as you pass over them; the project picker (and every full-screen list of the config) selects the row under the pointer with its details beside it, opens it on a click, and its key chips are buttons; modals light up their buttons; Telescope (the palette and every picker) selects on hover and opens on a click; the file tree opens a file or a folder with one click; the start screen's buttons follow the pointer. Over menus and pickers the pointer is an arrow, over text an I-beam.
  • A guided setup opens on the first launch, full screen, in seven steps (each one says what it still needs and Next waits for it; keyboard or mouse): what this machine is (distro, package manager, GPU), a theme (previewed live as you move over it), your languages (the ones this machine uses already checked), a few features, an AI agent (the ones found here with their models, or one to install), what to install (missing system packages with the exact command for pacman, apt, dnf, zypper or apk, Leader's plugins, language tools and parsers, and the agent you asked for: nothing runs until you press Install, sudo asks in a terminal, each item shows its status) and a review with the install checks. It comes back with Ctrl+Shift+W, Help › Welcome or the bar's "to finish" chip. Full-screen lists such as Open a project mark the chosen row with a bar and move with the arrows or hjkl.
  • Updates, connected: Leader looks for a newer version now and then (one tiny request, never more than every 12 hours, off with update.enabled) and shows a ↑ 1.9.0 badge in the bar and a line at the top of the logo's menu. Click it for what changed, version by version, and update on request: the config is pulled with git (never merged or discarded), the plugins restored to the lockfile, the desktop app updated through its installer, and Leader offers to restart. Skip hides a version until a newer one; Help › Check for Updates looks right now.
  • Projects first, like an IDE: started from the menu, Leader asks which project to open (or reopens the last one, or shows the start screen: your choice); leader ~/code/app opens that folder as the project. Pin the ones you use (p in the picker) to keep them at the top, or walk your folders Yazi-style (b in the picker: h up, l in, ⏎ open). Recent files (Space f o, the start screen) are the ones of the project you are in, not everything you ever opened; Ctrl+A there widens it to every project. The window comes back with its size, place and maximized state, and its frame is yours to pick (Leader's square bar or the system's).
  • Panes: Alt+V / Alt+H open a real Neovim split with the start screen in it, so ff/fo load the chosen file in that pane; the dashboard is per pane, Ctrl+h/j/k/l moves, Alt+arrows resizes, Alt+Z zooms and Alt+Q closes. <leader>V lists them and has equal sizes, rotate and swap. See panes.md.
  • A toolbar you decide: a right click on the bar (or View › Customize Toolbar) turns each tool and chip on or off: project, home, recent files, command palette, file tree, terminal, problems, agent (pick the agent and model right there), theme (light/dark, the terminal's theme, a dark and a light list previewed as you hover, the whole catalog), settings, setup. Every tool shows a tooltip with its key.
  • Window keys: F10 app menu, Ctrl+Shift+P command palette, Ctrl+Shift+O open a project, F11 fullscreen, Ctrl+Shift+Enter maximize, Ctrl+Shift+M minimize, Ctrl+Shift+N new window, Ctrl+Shift+H home, Ctrl+Shift+R recent files, Ctrl+Shift+W welcome and checks.
  • Font: gui.font in core/settings.lua (guifont syntax, "Family:h13") and gui.font_weight (400 regular, 500 medium…; the app applies it live); Ctrl+= / Ctrl+- / Ctrl+0 resize it. options.linespace (2 px) adds air between lines in the desktop app; a terminal owns its own line height and weight (font.normal.style in Alacritty).
  • WSL: it opens through WSLg on the Windows GPU (Mesa's d3d12 driver), with no setup.
  • LEADER_TIMING=1 leader prints where the startup time goes (~170 ms to the first frame on WSLg, GPU start included).

Philosophy

  • Fast: ~20 ms startup (measured headless, with --startuptime); defaults.lazy = true, plugins loaded by event/filetype/command/key, bytecode cache (vim.loader) and unneeded built-in plugins disabled.
  • Minimal: 54 entries in lazy-lock.json, counting dependencies and NvChad's own. No heavy session manager, no tab bar, no embedded AI chat, no LSP dependency tree for things you don't use.
  • Modular: the config is declared, not wired. Adding a language, a plugin, an agent action or a Go helper means creating one file; nothing else is touched.
  • Spend the minimum: whatever can be decided without a model is decided beforehand (gates, local analysis, treesitter completion); when one has to be called, only what is needed is sent and the cost is shown.
  • Light where it matters: with ≤ 2 GB of RAM the resident processes (LSP, golangci-lint) are turned off automatically; force it with NVIM_LIGHT=1|0.
  • Everything by keyboard, with keys documented and generated from the real code.

What's included

AreaContents
LanguagesGo, TypeScript/JS/TSX, HTML/CSS/Tailwind/Astro, Python, Rust, SQL (PostgreSQL, SQLite), Swift (SwiftPM), Dart/Flutter, Java/Kotlin, Lua, Markdown, JSON/YAML/TOML, Docker, Make, Prisma, YarnSpinner. Each one declares its LSP, formatter, linter, parsers and its <leader> menu (it only exists in buffers of that filetype). Python, TypeScript, Rust and Swift share tests in the editor (the test under the cursor, marks per test, failures on their line), project setup presets and debugging; SQL runs the statement under the cursor against your database
Editingundo word by word (like VS Code), U/Ctrl+Y redo, undo tree, the file at the previous save, a clipboard that x/c don't overwrite and a yank history ([y/]y after a paste, <leader>uy), blink.cmp, optional local FIM completion (Qwen2.5-Coder 1.5B), mini.pairs/surround/move, multicursor, flash (jumps), treesitter textobjects, folds (ufo) with foldable long comments, custom snippets
NavigationTelescope (+fzf), nvim-tree, harpoon, aerial (symbols), grug-far (global replace), file history
Gitgitsigns, diffview, git-conflict, git-blame (off by default) and conventional commits (see below)
Diagnosticsinline blocks under their line, line number colored by severity, unique colors across the whole UI, line popup, copy as text (line, file or project), Trouble
UIflat design (floats, menus and pickers are filled blocks, no boxes; every color derived from the theme, notifications, yank/accept flash), lualine, gruvbox ↔ light theme or live-inherited from the terminal (Alacritty), dashboard with braille art, render-markdown, inline colors
Sessionsone per folder and branch, periodic autosave, restored automatically on a bare start (terminals included)
Go without an LLMcompletion helpers that read your code and your go.mod (see below)
Dart / Flutterrun with an entry-point picker, debugging, tests inside the editor, build_runner watch, Riverpod/freezed completion (see below)
AI agent (optional)<leader>a: one-shot actions on the function under the cursor, with an inline diff (see below)
Herdr (optional)<leader>W: the workspace of agents — what every one of them is doing, live, and the setup (see below)

Commits (<leader>G)

A single panel for committing: what goes in (staged vs not staged, with +/− per file), the message with live Conventional Commits validation (✔ feat(scope) · 38/72), assisted type and scope, and a line saying what the agent will do before spending anything. Gc opens the panel, Gm opens it with the AI draft, GM commits on its own, in the background (stages what is modified except sensitive files, the AI writes ONE message in English, it is validated and committed; if anything fails, it undoes what it staged) and Ga rewrites the last one. The AI message is always in English, on one line, in ~2 s with a light model.

The remotes are there too: GP pushes (setting the upstream the first time), Gu pulls with --ff-only (a divergent branch is reported, never merged behind your back) and GF fetches every remote with --prune. They run in the integrated terminal, so credentials, SSH passphrases and hooks work, and Esc closes the window without stopping the command.

GitHub (<leader>Gh)

The gh CLI and its TUIs, one key each: d gh-dash (PRs and issues in one dashboard), o gh-observer (the current PR's checks, live), e gh-enhance (Actions runs and logs) and n gh-news (notifications). Each lives in its own terminal: the same key brings it back while it runs, Esc closes the window and it keeps running. The first launch of a missing extension shows the exact gh extension install … command and does both after you confirm; s is the checklist of all of them. p, i and r use octo.nvim for PRs, issues and reviews inside Neovim, and v/c/b/a/A cover the core commands (PR in the browser, checks, repo, auth status/login). Details in docs/cheatsheets/github.md.

AI agent (<leader>a)

It is not a plugin: it is Lua code in this config, with no dependencies, and it does nothing until you invoke it. Claude Code, OpenCode, Ollama (CPU/CUDA), LM Studio/llama.cpp/vLLM and other CLIs; it detects and configures its backends on its own (<leader>am → Setup, or ./scripts/agent-setup.sh). A short generic menu and one menu per level, each with its own folder and actions:

MenuWhat it does
<leader>aimplement, review, explain, ask, document, refactor, t convert (JSON, SQL or curl into code for the file's language), hand off to Herdr; f inline fix (the line's diagnostic, with the type of what it points at) and F block fix (the whole function)
aT testsw writes only the missing tests at the end of the test file · m mock · f fixes a test by running it first (if it already passes, 0 tokens) · g gaps (local)
aL lensessecurity, performance, concurrency, errors, API compatibility: single-focus reviews as diagnostics; each one first discards what is not its concern
aD / aP / aUhexagonal architecture: domain, repositories and services, with local 0-token analysis
aS setupthe documents agents read before they code: AGENTS.md (from the project, or a 0-token template), STACK.md (82 stacks in 10 kinds of project, games, data / AI and Neovim plugins included: Go stdlib/chi/Fiber/Huma, Bun + Hono, FastAPI, Axum, Next.js, Vite, Solid, Alpine.js, Flutter (Riverpod, Bloc, Supabase, offline-first, desktop), Bubble Tea and Ratatui TUIs, self-hosted Go + SQLite / PocketBase / Docker Compose…; then customize: Bun or npm, TypeScript 7, Biome/Oxlint, Tailwind 4, deploy (Docker self-hosted, Coolify, Fly, systemd, Kubernetes, Cloudflare, Vercel, stores, GoReleaser), CI, observability, quality; plus checked principles: SOLID, Clean Code, hexagonal, one file one responsibility…), DESIGN.md (17 design systems (Material 3, Ant, Fluent, Carbon, Atlassian, Polaris, Radix, Mantine, Geist…), 13 styles and 9 terminal designs; styles: neo-brutalism, Swiss, retro pixel, glass, mono minimal; terminal / TUI: ASCII art, Braille, brutal, classic, retro CRT, modern; then customize platforms, theme, palette, motion, effects, glyphs, density and accessibility; or described in your words) and SPEC.md (write it any way, it comes back with requirements, acceptance criteria and open questions; then add modules for what every app repeats: accounts, roles, lists, workspaces, notifications, audit log, uploads, import/export, offline, payments, subscriptions, with their edge cases and the questions only you can answer); then build from them: the whole project, one layer (foundation from STACK.md, domain from SPEC.md, design from DESIGN.md), one feature (a requirement of SPEC.md, or a new one you describe) or a recipe (prefab tasks per stack: a Flutter screen with its state, a Go endpoint, an entity with its repository…); coverage for free (which requirements have a test, what drifted from the documents), and every build leaves its decisions as ADRs and a PROGRESS.md the next agent reads
aA advancedseveral at once (a fix all, o / O fix from output, p plan; kept out of the everyday <leader>a): fix all the file's findings (one request per function), fix what a build printed (the last terminal, the quickfix or a command), a plan across files you edit before it runs; the proposals wait in a queue (] [ y l)
GRrelease: the changelog and the next version from your Conventional Commits (0 tokens), a PR description (one light request, to the clipboard), a release checklist from STACK.md's quality gates
Gvreview what is staged before committing: secrets and leftovers for free, the code once, findings as diagnostics

Around the proposals: Tab refines one instead of starting over, accepted code is verified for free (the LSP's new errors are compared with the old ones and it offers one more attempt with them), and a spend guard checks every request against your own history (it asks above a size, blocks at the caps you set). Also: clear findings one at a time, per file or all of them (<leader>ac, aC, :AgentClear), history in markdown with the code sent, filters and back navigation (<leader>ah), models per tier (cheap where it is enough). Details in docs/cheatsheets/agent.md.

Herdr (<leader>W)

Herdr is the terminal workspace that runs the coding agents. Leader reads it through its CLI: <leader>Wa opens the breakdown of the workspace — one row per agent (its state, its kind, where it is), the transcript of the one under the cursor and why Herdr thinks it is in that state, refreshed on its own. f brings the agent's pane to the front, s sends it a prompt. <leader>Ws is the setup: it detects the binary, the server and the agents' integrations, and installs what is missing (nothing without pressing the button).

It also goes the other way: with the registration on, this nvim appears in Herdr's Agents panel saying what it is doing (working while the agent runs, blocked while a proposal waits for you), and Herdr 0.10+ brings the pane back with your session after a server restart. Details in docs/cheatsheets/herdr.md.

Go without an LLM (smart completion, 0 tokens)

On top of a shared index (utils/goindex.lua: treesitter + go.mod, cached by date):

  • missing methods to satisfy an interface, with the exact signature and bodies that call your dependency (return s.repo.FindByID(ctx, id));
  • if err != nil with the zero values of the function's returns and a message taken from what is called (the menu opens by itself after x, err := …);
  • mappers between layers (NoteResponse ↔ Note), constructor wiring (NewNoteService(noteRepo) with the arguments resolved), enum helpers (IsValid, Parse, UnmarshalText…), interface from a struct and var _ I = (*T)(nil), HTTP handlers from a service (net/http, chi, gin, echo);
  • getters of the private fields (slices and maps as copies), Reconstitute (the persistence constructor), the domain's repository port and package ranked by the folder (main only where it makes sense);
  • struct tags, constructors, filled literals, sentinel errors, statement snippets. Details in docs/cheatsheets/go.md.

Dart / Flutter (<leader>d)

  • run and debug through nvim-dap, with a picker of entry points (lib/main*.dart, the last one first) and the device in the statusline; hot reload on save, the app's log in a split that keeps your focus;
  • tests inside the editor: the test under the cursor, the file or everything, a mark per test line, failures as diagnostics on the expect that failed, quickfix; <leader>dT jumps between a file and its test;
  • code generation: build_runner watch in the background with its state in the statusline and its errors on their line; saving an .arb runs gen-l10n;
  • completion for your stack, read from pubspec.yaml: Riverpod providers, freezed classes, Consumer widgets, named after the file and with their part lines; pub outdated written next to each dependency, pub add. Details in docs/cheatsheets/dart-flutter.md.

Customizing

Everything configurable is in nvim/lua/core/settings.lua with its default value and a comment. Your changes go in nvim/lua/local.lua (git-ignored, merged on top; the installer can generate it with --lang, --theme, --light):

-- nvim/lua/local.lua
return {
  theme = { name = "catppuccin" },
  languages = { disabled = { "astro", "dart", "java", "kotlin" } },
  agent = { backend = "ollama", tiers = { lens_security = "light" } },
  git = { commit = { subject_max = 60 } },
}

You do not have to edit a file for the everyday ones: <leader>Sp opens the settings panel (theme, motion, cursor, format on save, diagnostics layout, answer language, light mode, plugins, tools, update…). Enter flips a value and applies it at once when it can (the few that need a restart say so); your choices are kept apart (stdpath("state")/prefs.json), win over local.lua, and "Reset my choices" forgets them. Keys of their own: St theme, Sf format on save, Sl plugins, SM tools, Si info, Su update the config.

Files have the same treatment: <leader>F (and a / r / d inside the tree) creates, renames, moves, duplicates and deletes in flat modals that show where the file will land. Several files at once (a.go; b/c.go; d/), a name with / moves it, the move is a folder picker, and delete goes to a trash (<leader>Fu brings the last one back). Open buffers, language servers and the tree follow a rename; your unsaved changes survive it.

To add (each one is a single file and, at most, a name in its NAMES list):

  • a language: nvim/lua/languages/<name>.lua (contract at the top of languages/init.lua);
  • a plugin: a file in nvim/lua/plugins/<group>/;
  • a dashboard art: nvim/lua/ui/arts/<name>.lua (braille only);
  • an AI backend: an entry in agent.custom (no code) or nvim/lua/agent/backends/<name>.lua;
  • an agent action, lens or test: agent/actions/, agent/lenses/actions/ or agent/tests/actions/;
  • a per-language test convention: agent/tests/convention.lua;
  • a Go completion helper: a utils/go<name>.lua module on top of goindex and its call in gosmart.

How it is organized

The principles (the same across the whole repo) and where they show:

  • One responsibility per module: the pure parts (commit/conventional.lua, utils/goindex.lua, agent/protocol.lua) kept apart from what touches git, the LSP or the UI (commit/git.lua, commit/panel.lua); one presenter per output kind (agent/output/).
  • Open for extension, closed for modification: discovered registries (languages/, agent/actions/, agent/backends/, agent/lenses/); adding one doesn't force editing the others. The agent pipeline is extended with action hooks (retarget, merge, reveal, prepare), with guards (agent/guards/) and with events (agent/events.lua: verification, batches and the proposal queue subscribe; the presenter that accepts a proposal knows none of them).
  • Dependencies point to abstractions: each piece talks to a small contract (a backend's build/parser, a context provider's collect(target, done), a Go helper's declaration_items(range, frag)), not to its neighbor's implementation.
  • Configurable things in a single place: core/settings.lua; the scripts (install.sh, install.ps1) share the same settings logic (scripts/install-local.lua) instead of duplicating it.

Keys and documentation

  • The documentation site: everything below in one searchable page (/ to search). It is generated from these Markdown files (scripts/gen-docs.sh; the pre-commit hook regenerates it).
  • The offline guide: the same documentation in one HTML file with the fonts embedded, no network needed (scripts/gen-offline.sh keeps it up to date). The site also serves it at <https://leader.sazardev.stream/offline>.
  • CHEATSHEET.md: all the keys. It is generated from the real keymaps (scripts/gen-cheatsheet.sh; the pre-commit hook regenerates it). Inside nvim, the dashboard's Mappings button shows the same.
  • docs/cheatsheets/: what the code doesn't say (workflows, tools, pitfalls), one page per language and the agent's.
  • AGENTS.md / CLAUDE.md: architecture and pitfalls for whoever touches the config (people or agents).

Structure

install.sh · install.ps1      installation (Linux/WSL/macOS · Windows)
VERSION                       config version
nvim/                         the config (what gets linked)
  init.lua                    bootstrap only
  lua/core/                   settings, options, keymaps and startup autocmds
  lua/languages/              one file per language (declared; not wired)
  lua/plugins/<group>/        one lazy spec per plugin
  lua/menus/                  contextual leader menus
  lua/agent/                  AI agent: backends, actions, context, output, tests, lenses, ddd, setup
  lua/commit/                 commits: validation (pure), git, AI draft, panel
  lua/utils/                  utilities; go*.lua = Go completion helpers on top of goindex
  lua/ui/ appearance/ themes/ dashboard, theme, colors
  lua/prefs/                  settings panel (<leader>S): one file per group of toggles in items/
  lua/files/                  file operations (<leader>F): paths, file system, buffers, modals
  lua/panes/                  panes: real splits with the start screen (Alt+V / Alt+H, <leader>V)
  snippets/ ftdetect/ syntax/ custom snippets and filetypes
scripts/                      installation (install-*.lua), cheatsheet, agent setup and tests
docs/cheatsheets/             per-language and agent documentation
.githooks/                    up-to-date cheatsheet + Conventional Commits

Maintenance

  • Update: re-run the install command (or git pull && ./install.sh): it brings the plugins to the versions pinned in lazy-lock.json; the config reloads itself when nvim opens.
  • Check: scripts/test.sh runs everything (agent and commits, Go helpers, installer and up-to-date cheatsheet; no network or tokens); each suite can also be run on its own: scripts/test-agent.sh, scripts/test-go.sh, scripts/test-install.sh, scripts/gen-cheatsheet.sh --check.
  • Commits: Conventional Commits format (feat(scope): subject); the commit-msg hook validates it and <leader>Gc / Gm / GM assist with it.

Community

Leader is open source and maintained in public. There is no company behind it, no telemetry and no paid tier.

  • Discussions: questions, setup help, ideas, what you built.
  • Issues: reproducible bugs and scoped feature requests (with templates).
  • Contributing: development setup, tests, commit style and the pull request checklist.
  • Code of Conduct: the rules of the community.
  • Support: where to ask and what to include; Governance: how decisions are made.
  • Roadmap: now, next, later and what is explicitly not planned.
  • Authors: who builds it and what it stands on.

Good first issues are labelled good first issue; anything where a second pair of hands helps is help wanted.

Security

Do not open a public issue for a vulnerability. Use GitHub's private vulnerability reporting (Security → Report a vulnerability) or the contact in SECURITY.md. The policy covers the installers, the desktop app, the config and the release artifacts, and explains the security model: no telemetry, the agent only calls the backend you choose, plugins are pinned, and every release is checksummed (signed when the release key is available).

License

MIT © sazardev. Leader is an independent project, not affiliated with the Neovim project or NvChad. The plugin versions are pinned in nvim/lazy-lock.json; each plugin keeps its own license.

If Leader saves you time, a ⭐ helps other people find it.

Start · docs/cheatsheets/README.md

Workflows

Keys: CHEATSHEET.md (repo root) — the only source. It is generated by reading the real keymaps (scripts/gen-cheatsheet.sh) and regenerated on every commit; it is not edited by hand and its tables are not duplicated here. Inside nvim, the dashboard's Mappings button (NvCheatsheet) shows the same thing live. These pages hold what the code doesn't say: what each thing does, workflows, tools, pitfalls. <leader> = Space.
CheatsheetWhat it covers
go.mdGo in depth: struct tags, snippets, contextual completion, <leader>g menu
csharp.mdC# / .NET (<leader>c): Roslyn or csharp-ls, smart ctor/props/DbSet/DI completion, tests in the editor (TRX), NuGet, solutions/projects, dotnet/EF Core, netcoredbg
agent.mdInline agent (<leader>a): one-shot implement/review/explain/convert... with Claude, OpenCode or a local model (Ollama, OpenAI-compatible servers)
skills.mdAgent skills (<leader>aK): SKILL.md files the agents load on demand — marketplace, 0-config install by model/stack, and tolerance for legacy/already-worked repos
herdr.mdHerdr (<leader>W): the workspace of agents — what every one of them is doing, and the setup
editing.mdUndo, clipboard and yank history (<leader>u): undo word by word, redo keys, undo tree, the file at the previous save, yank list
gitignore.md.gitignore (<leader>Gi): what it detects, the topics, how it merges with an existing file
tasks.mdMakefile tasks (<leader>R): the picker, writing targets it can list, the preset Makefiles per language
release.mdRelease kit (<leader>GR): changelog and next version from Conventional Commits, PR description, release checklist
github.mdGitHub (<leader>Gh): gh-dash, gh-observer, gh-enhance and gh-news TUIs, octo.nvim for PRs/issues/reviews, installing the extensions
typescript.mdTypeScript / JavaScript (<leader>t): scripts, typecheck to quickfix, tests in the editor (vitest, jest, bun), biome or prettier, setup
python.mdPython (<leader>p): basedpyright + ruff, uv, tests in the editor (pytest), debugpy, setup
rust.mdRust (<leader>r): rust-analyzer with clippy, cargo, tests in the editor, macros, codelldb, setup
sql.mdSQL (<leader>Q): run the statement under the cursor against PostgreSQL or SQLite, explain, schema
swift.mdSwift (<leader>i): SwiftPM, sourcekit-lsp, tests in the editor (XCTest, Swift Testing), setup
env.mdEnv files (<leader>E): .env.example per stack, .env.dev/qa/staging/prod, check, .gitignore, AI loader
dart-flutter.mdDart / Flutter (<leader>d): run and debug, tests inside the editor, build_runner, completion for Riverpod/freezed, pub
java-kotlin.mdJava and Kotlin (Gradle / Maven)

markdown.mdMarkdown
web.mdHTML, CSS, Tailwind, Astro

terminal.mdTerminal (<leader>T): command vs named vs own shells, Esc/Shift+Esc/Ctrl+x, reopen without re-sending
panes.mdPanes (Alt+V / Alt+H, <leader>V): a real split with the start screen, per-pane dashboards, resize, zoom, list

How the leader works

Each language has its own letter under <leader> (g Go, d Dart, t TypeScript, p Python, r Rust, c C#, Q SQL, i Swift…) and only exists in buffers of that filetype; <leader>G (Git) only inside a repo, and <leader>a (agent), <leader>R (Makefile tasks), <leader>E (env files), <leader>P (sessions), <leader>u (undo and yank history), <leader>S (settings), <leader>F (files), <leader>C (comments), <leader>T (terminal), <leader>V (panes) and <leader>W (Herdr) are global. Press <leader> and which-key shows only what applies to the current file (no delay).

The language menus open an integrated terminal (toggleterm) and run the commands at the project root (they detect go.mod, package.json, Cargo.toml, pubspec.yaml, pom.xml/build.gradle...).

Workflows worth knowing

The exact keys are in CHEATSHEET.md; here, how they combine.

  • Jump and come back: after gd / grr / Ctrl+click, Esc returns to where you were (if you didn't edit there); it also closes popups and clears the highlight. Ctrl+o / Ctrl+i walk the jump history.
  • Line diagnostic (<leader>xd): opens a popup; press it again to enter it and copy the text.
  • Diagnostic blocks (utils/diagblocks.lua): <leader>xa draws all of them under their line (a bar in the severity color, source/code and a 2/5 counter); <leader>xc toggles between all expanded and one by one (each one a single line, only the one on the cursor line opens); <leader>xn/xp jump to the next/previous and turn the view on in one-by-one mode. They show up on their own in every buffer with diagnostics (the text on the right, virtual_text, is off) and <leader>xa turns them off in that buffer; then only the tinted line number remains. In insert mode the blocks are hidden (nothing appears or pushes the code while you type) and come back when you leave with Esc; diagnostics.update_in_insert = true keeps them live. Matched colors (ui/diagcolors.lua): the E2/W1 label in the status bar, the line number and each block's label are the SAME pill (background = the theme's DiagnosticError/Warn/Info/Hint, text = the theme background, bold), and the block's side bar uses that same accent; this way they change together with the theme (also with the theme inherited from the terminal and when Alacritty changes live: diagcolors.sync() detects the change on every status bar refresh and recomputes number and blocks, because base46 loads its cache without notifying through an event). Gutter: no icons either on the left or next to the code. The line number is drawn with the pill of the highest severity on the line (red error, yellow warning, blue info, cyan hint); the pill fits the digits, with a space on each side, so the number ends up centered (it is drawn by 'statuscolumn', defined in settings.options, and lines split by wrap only leave the gap) and git is a thin ┃ bar attached to the number (signcolumn=no: the statuscolumn draws the sign in ONE cell; with signcolumn=yes each sign takes two and an extra gap is left) (green added, blue changed, red deleted), so they are not confused. Your choice (<leader>xa on/off, <leader>xc expanded/collapsed) is global and saved in stdpath("state")/diagblocks.json, so it comes back the same when you reopen nvim; settings.diagnostic_blocks (auto, mode, number) only gives the initial value. <leader>xn/xp with the view off turn it on only in that buffer and don't touch what is saved. It updates on its own when diagnostics or the cursor change. The bottom panel (<leader>xx, Trouble) opens with focus: Enter jumps to the diagnostic and opens its popup, like <leader>xd.
  • Delete without clobbering the clipboard: <leader>d + motion (<leader>dd line, <leader>dw word) or in visual mode.
  • Multicursor (multicursor.nvim, real cursors like VS Code/Helix; loaded on the first key): add cursors by line (Ctrl+↑/↓), by match of the word or selection (Alt+n/Alt+p, Alt+Shift+n/p skips one) or all at once (Alt+a); Alt+click adds or removes one with the mouse. With several cursors everything (motions, operators, insert, undo) is repeated on each one; Esc clears them, Ctrl+q turns the main cursor off/on, ←/→ switch cursor and Alt+x deletes the current one. In insert mode, the secondary cursors replicate what was typed ON LEAVING insert; if the completion menu is open, Esc/jk close it first and you have to press them again. Ideas: Alt+a on a word to rename it in the file; Ctrl+↓ several times + I/A to edit columns. The native visual block (Ctrl+v + I/A/c) is still there for simple columns. Don't use Ctrl+n (it is nvim-tree).
  • Textobjects (treesitter): combine them with operators — daf deletes the function, dif the body, cif changes it, yaf copies it, vaf selects it; dac deletes the class. For braces of any block: di{ (inside), da{ (with braces), % jumps to the pair. PageDown/PageUp jump block by block (function, method, class, struct/interface in Go); they don't stop at closures inside a function, accept a count (3<PageDown>) and, with no blocks or no parser, do the normal scroll. Home/End go to the start/end of the current block; pressing them again goes up to the enclosing block (method → class → file). For start/end of line use ^/$ (or 0), and in insert they remain native.
  • Alt+hjkl move a line or block (mini.move); in Normal mode Alt+h opens a pane instead (the visual block still moves left, and Alt+v is a pane).
  • Format: <leader>fm formats (conform); it also formats on save.
  • Telescope: the preview is always visible. flex layout: on the right in windows ≥ 110 columns, at the bottom in narrow ones (lua/plugins/navigation/telescope.lua).
  • nvim-tree: opening a file closes the tree; I shows/hides what git ignores. Deleting a folder warns how many files it contains.
  • Terminal (<leader>T menu, plus Alt+i for the float, Alt+t for a quick pane below the current one, and <leader>Tv / <leader>Th for a vertical / horizontal split): t shows/hides the command terminal (where every language and task command runs — reopening after Esc sends nothing), l lists every terminal to jump to, n opens a new shell, s sends Ctrl-C. Esc (in the terminal or its Normal mode) closes the window and the shell keeps running; Shift+Esc only hands the focus back to the code and leaves the terminal open; Ctrl+x leaves terminal mode (i types again, y// copy and search the output). Shift+Esc needs a terminal that tells it apart from Esc; if yours doesn't, it behaves like Esc.
  • Completion (blink.cmp): the documentation appears instantly on selection and, for snippets, shows what will be inserted. Nothing comes preselected, except inside a Go struct tag (there Enter accepts the first suggestion). Sources: LSP, path, snippets, buffer; in Go gotags and gosmart are added; in Lua, lazydev.
  • Local AI completion (optional, <leader>SF / :Fim): Qwen2.5-Coder 1.5B Q8 through llama.cpp, ghost text while you type. Ctrl+Tab accepts the whole suggestion and Ctrl+Shift+Tab only its first line, in code, the chat and modal inputs; Tab / Shift+Tab stay blink.cmp's normal completion (they never accept the AI suggestion). It runs on 127.0.0.1:8012, starts on demand (the first start downloads ~1.6 GB), sleeps after five minutes idle, and is toggled from <leader>Sp → Editing → Local tab completion, <leader>SF, or :Fim; the statusline shows FIM when ready and FIM… while starting. Enabled per machine in lua/local.lua (fim = { enabled = true }).
    • With the menu open, Tab / Shift+Tab pick a suggestion (and Enter inserts it). Without a menu, inside a snippet (e.g. gorm.Open(dialector, opts)), they jump to the next / previous placeholder.
    • Keep what you typed and move on: Esc (closes the menu without leaving the snippet) and then Tab.
    • Esc (or jk) with the menu open only closes it and undoes the suggestion that was inserted (back to what you typed); you stay in insert mode and in your snippet. Without a menu, they leave insert as usual.
  • Sessions: one per folder (and per git branch; main/master use the base). A bare leader inside a project (or leader <folder>) brings its windows, files and terminals back automatically (session.auto_restore, <leader>Sp → Editing; the saved file is sourced directly, before the start screen opens; terminals return as fresh shells in the same place). Starting with a file (nvim x) leaves that session alone; the Restore Session button (rs) and <leader>Pr still bring any saved one on demand, <leader>Pl the last one, <leader>Pw saves now, <leader>Px does not save on exit, and <leader>Pq / <leader>Q quit everything (asking first about unsaved changes).
  • Git: git-blame is off by default (no cost); it is toggled from the Git menu.
  • Conventional commits (<leader>G, a single panel): Gc opens the panel, Gm opens it and immediately asks the AI for the draft, GM commits on its own, without a panel (see below), Ga rewrites the last commit (warns if it is already on the remote). At the top you see what gets committed: what is staged (●, with +/− per file) and, separately, what does not go in (○, modified or new); Space stages/unstages the file (or the whole group if you are on its header), a stages everything, u unstages everything, p or Enter show its diff. Below you write the message and the title is validated live (✔ feat(scope) · 38/72, or why it fails); Ctrl+t picks the type (suggested from the files) and Ctrl+o the scope (from the paths and the history). Underneath, a line says what the agent will do before spending anything (✦ AI claude/haiku · ⌃g to draft · reads 2 files, 3.1k chars: light model, only reads what is staged: its compact diff without lockfiles capped at git.commit.diff_max_chars, the branch and the list of types) and, when done, what it did (drafted by … · 2.5s · 1.2k→39 tok · $0.0001); Ctrl+g asks for the draft (never on its own, except with Gm), replaces the message and is undone with u, and repeating it gives another one. The message is always in English and on a single line (Conventional header; ~2 s with haiku). GM automatic commit: no panel and in the background; if you already staged files it commits only those, otherwise it stages everything modified except sensitive files (git.commit.sensitive: .env, keys…), the AI writes the message, it is validated and committed, and a one-line notice gives hash, message, files, +/−, time and cost. If something fails (no backend, AI, invalid header, a hook) it undoes what it staged and leaves nothing half done (Gm to do it by hand). Ctrl+s (or Enter in normal mode) commits; with nothing staged or with an invalid message it warns and does nothing; if a hook rejects the commit the panel stays open so you can fix it. Tab toggles files ↔ message, q/Esc cancel (and stop the agent). The .githooks/commit-msg hook requires the same format on any git commit (types and length in git.commit; if you change the list of types, change it in the hook too).
  • Comments (<leader>C): Cd inserts the idiomatic documentation template for the function/type under the cursor (godoc, LuaCATS, JSDoc, docstring, rustdoc) with name, parameters and return already filled in; Cm lists in quickfix the public items that have no doc comment (for other languages use the agent: <leader>ad). Snippets in any language, with the language's own comment style: todo, fixme, note, hack, warn, perf, deprecated (and with o at the end, e.g. todoo, TODO(name):); they live in nvim/snippets/all.json.
  • Theme: gruvbox ↔ gruvbox_light, or inherit the terminal's (Alacritty), which is followed live.
  • Other: :TSContext toggle pins the function/class context at the top; za/zc/zo fold (treesitter folds with nvim-ufo, everything open on start) and <leader>z folds by what the code is: <leader>zf the function you are in, zt the type, zo the object/table literal, zb the block (again = the one around it), zm the methods of the current type, zF/zT/zO every function/type/object in the file, za outline only, ze expand all, zs focus here (all closed except this), z1-z3 fold everything deeper than that level (all after <leader>); <leader>C folds long comments (blocks or consecutive // lines of comments.min_lines or more, 4 by default): Cc folds them all and remembers the preference (files you open afterwards, and future sessions, open with them already folded; it is saved in stdpath("state")/comments.json), Ce unfolds them and stops folding them automatically, Ct toggles only the one under the cursor without touching the preference; comments.auto_collapse only gives the initial value; :Lazy sync installs/updates plugins (the config auto-reload does not).

Light mode

On machines with ≤ 2 GB of RAM, LSP, go.nvim and golangci-lint are turned off (go vet remains). Treesitter, conform and nvim-lint keep running. Force it with NVIM_LIGHT=1|0. Per-machine settings in lua/local.lua (ignored by git).

Adding things

  • Language: lua/languages/<name>.lua + add it to NAMES in languages/init.lua + its page here.
  • Plugin: one file in lua/plugins/<group>/.
  • New keymap: give it a desc of 2+ words starting with its section ("Harpoon add file"); the generator and NvCheatsheet group by the first word and drop single-word ones.
  • Go snippet: nvim/snippets/go.json (VSCode format).
  • After changing keys or menus: nothing to edit by hand; scripts/gen-cheatsheet.sh regenerates CHEATSHEET.md (the pre-commit hook does it on its own).

Start · docs/FAQ.md

FAQ

Short answers to the questions that come up most often. The long version is in the documentation or in CHEATSHEET.md; the offline copy of the whole guide is docs/offline.html.

Is Leader a fork of Neovim?

No. Leader is two things: a Neovim config (the nvim/ folder, Lua, plugins, menus) and a desktop front-end (the leader binary, Rust + wgpu) that runs nvim --embed and draws the UI in its own window. The editor is stock Neovim, with your normal :commands, LSP servers and plugins.

Do I need the desktop app?

No. The config works in any terminal (Linux, macOS, Windows) and in the app. The app adds menus, a command palette, projects with their sessions, a toolbar, the terminal-inherited theme and a GPU-rendered grid.

Which platforms are supported?

Config (terminal)Desktop app
Linux x86_64✅✅ (Wayland, X11, WSLg)
Linux aarch64✅build from source
macOS✅❌ (planned)
Windows✅ PowerShell / WSLWSL only (native Windows planned)

The one-liner installs the app on Linux x86_64 and the config everywhere else.

What does the installer change on my machine?

It installs ~/.local/bin/leader, a menu entry, the docs/man pages, Neovim (if missing or too old), the config in ~/.config/nvim, plugins, language tools and parsers. An existing Neovim config is moved to nvim.bak-<date>, never deleted. Re-running it is idempotent. --dry-run shows the plan; --no-config installs only the app.

How do I update?

Re-run the one-liner. It brings the app, the config, the plugins and the tools to the current versions. If you cloned the repo, git pull && ./install.sh; for plugins only, :Lazy sync. The Settings menu also has Check for Updates and What's New.

How do I uninstall?

curl -fsSL https://leader.sazardev.stream/install | bash -s -- --uninstall

This removes the app, its menu entry and its manual pages. The config and your files stay. To remove everything, delete ~/.config/nvim, ~/.local/share/leader and ~/.local/state/nvim (back up lua/local.lua first if you customized it).

Will it overwrite my current Neovim config?

It moves it to ~/.config/nvim.bak-<date> and links Leader in its place. You can restore the backup at any time. To try Leader without touching your config, install into a throwaway XDG_CONFIG_HOME.

Why are there so many plugins and languages?

Everything is lazy: a plugin loads on an event, a filetype or a key, and a language only installs its LSP, formatters and linters if you keep its pack. --lang (or <Space>Sp → Languages) leaves out what you do not use. On a machine with ≤ 2 GB of RAM, light mode turns off the resident LSP servers and keeps the editor usable.

Does Leader phone home?

No telemetry, no analytics and no account. The config sends nothing on its own. The only network calls are the ones you trigger: the plugin/tool installer, an agent request, the optional update check (raw GitHub files, no API key) and a language server downloading its runtime. The website uses no cookies.

Which AI backends does the agent use?

Any of these, chosen in <leader>am:

  • Claude Code (claude), OpenCode (opencode), or any CLI you declare in agent.custom;
  • Ollama and LM Studio / llama.cpp / vLLM through their OpenAI-compatible HTTP APIs;
  • a local FIM completion (Qwen2.5-Coder 1.5B via llama.cpp), off by default and toggled with <leader>SF.

The agent is one-shot by default (implement, review, explain, fix…), with a persistent chat under <leader>aC.

Does the agent see my code and secrets?

It sends the target and the context Leader builds (types, diagnostics, project rules, selected files) to the backend you chose. A local backend stays local; a cloud backend receives that context. The AI commit draft excludes common secret files (git.commit.sensitive: .env, keys…) and the agent does not read outside the project. If the repository contains a secret, treat it as readable by the model you selected; use a local model for sensitive code.

Where are my settings, sessions and history?

  • ~/.config/nvim/lua/local.lua: per-machine settings (theme, languages, FIM, clipboard…), git-ignored.
  • stdpath("state"): sessions, recent files, agent history and spend, the agent choice, the settings you changed in the panel, the terminal theme marker, the task and project caches.
  • ~/.local/share/leader: the cloned config and its docs (if you used the installer).

Back up local.lua and ~/.local/state/nvim to keep your setup; the rest is regenerated.

How do I learn the keys?

Press <Space> and read the menu; it only shows what applies to the current buffer. The dashboard's Mappings button opens the whole cheat sheet, and CHEATSHEET.md is the same file (generated from the real keymaps). <Space>Sp is the settings panel. The documentation has a page per language and per feature, and the offline copy works without network.

Can I use it offline?

Yes. docs/offline.html is a single file with the whole guide, the CSS and the fonts embedded: download it and open it in any browser. The editor itself is local; only plugin/tool installation, agent requests and update checks need the network.

A plugin update broke something. How do I roll back?

The plugins are pinned in nvim/lazy-lock.json. :Lazy restore returns to the pinned versions; if you updated the lockfile, git checkout nvim/lazy-lock.json (or git revert) and :Lazy restore again. The same file is committed so a release is reproducible.

Is Leader free?

Yes. MIT license, no paid tier, no CLA. If it saves you time, the best ways to give back are a bug report, a docs fix, a patch or a star on the repository.

How do I contribute?

Read CONTRIBUTING.md, pick an issue labelled good first issue or help wanted, or open a discussion. There is no CLA; contributions are MIT, inbound = outbound.

Why is it called Leader?

The whole config hangs off one key: the leader key (<Space>). "Leader" is also the binary: the editor that leads your project, not a plugin you have to assemble.

Start · CHEATSHEET.md

Every key

Generated from the config's real keymaps (nvim_get_keymap, menus/, servers/on_attach.lua). Regenerated automatically on every commit (.githooks/pre-commit). <leader> = Space. This is the ONLY source of keys. What can't be derived from code (what each thing does, workflows, tools): docs/cheatsheets/.

Leader menus

Each menu has its own letter under <leader> and only exists where it applies: when pressing <leader> which-key only shows what belongs to the current buffer.

Start · CHANGELOG.md

Changelog

All notable changes, newest first. Generated from the Conventional Commits since each tag (<leader>GRc).

[1.18.0] - 2026-11-11

Highlights

  • Completion from a local model: FIM ghost text from Qwen2.5-Coder 1.5B Q8 served by llama.cpp on 127.0.0.1:8012, off by default. Ctrl+Tab accepts a suggestion and Ctrl+Shift+Tab its first line in any insert buffer (chat and modals included); Tab stays blink.cmp's. The server starts on demand and sleeps when idle; <leader>SF, :Fim and the settings panel toggle it, and the statusline shows FIM…/FIM.
  • Sessions come back on their own: a bare leader inside a project (or leader <folder>) restores its windows, files and terminals before the start screen would open, sourcing the file directly (no plugin load, no git subprocess). A file argument leaves that session alone, filetypes are matched again for treesitter and LSP, and open terminals return as fresh shells in the same place. terminal is in sessionoptions; <leader>Pq/<leader>Q quit everything asking first.
  • Panes you can lay out: <leader>V2/V3/Vm/V4 build two columns, three columns, a wide main pane with two stacked on the right and a 2x2 grid, keeping the file you edit in the first pane. <leader>VX closes every other pane, and Alt+T opens a quiet terminal pane below the current one whose shell survives Esc.
  • A quieter Markdown: *.md/*.mdx open as normal editable buffers (no locked reading mode, no conceal) and render-markdown is off by default; <leader>mr renders one buffer, and the agent transcript and history preview keep rendering on demand.
  • The app's type: gui.font_weight (400/500/600) and linespace give the desktop app the weight and the air between lines it was missing, both applied live; the default editor font is now h13.

Features

  • markdown: open plain and render on demand (6f8c316)
  • app: font weight and line spacing (aa50f45)
  • fim: local completion through llama.cpp (9305805)
  • panes: layout presets and a quick terminal pane (887da2b)
  • sessions: restore a bare start and keep terminals (6d09c5e)

Other changes

  • style(test): align the prefs test with the house style (4e6d21b)
  • chore(deps): add llama.vim and bump schemastore and ui (81f8843)
  • docs: FIM completion, session restore, panes and fonts (7e8b6fb)
  • chore(packaging): point the source Arch package at v1.17.0 (d375f5d)

[1.17.0] - 2026-11-10

Highlights

  • Panes, like an IDE: Alt+V / Alt+H open a real Neovim split with the start screen in it; ff/fo load the chosen file in that pane, and every pane keeps its own dashboard (selection, layout and window options). Ctrl+h/j/k/l moves, Alt+arrows resizes, Alt+Z zooms, Alt+Q closes, and <leader>V lists the panes plus equal sizes, rotate and swap. The old terminal splits moved to <leader>Tv / <leader>Th (the float stays on Alt+I), and the settings panel gained a Panes group.
  • The chat is a real editor pane: the panel is a right split that reflows the code beside it, Write answers apply their complete files, turns are animated and code blocks keep treesitter colors. The model can ask a decision with an ask block (a picker puts the answer in the composer), request missing files with @need: (injected once on the next turn), and typing while an answer streams queues the message instead of refusing it; dropped history is disclosed.

Features

  • panes: dashboard splits and pane management (bd06e48)
  • chat: ask picker, @need context, queue (180e7b0)
  • chat: split panel, apply files, animate turns (f51e6df)

Other changes

  • chore(deps): sync nvim-tree and schemastore in the lockfile (e881281)
  • chore(deps): bump neoconf and schemastore (4223338)
  • chore(packaging): point the source Arch package at v1.16.0 (ebaf9c5)

[1.16.0] - 2026-11-10

Highlights

  • GitHub lives in the editor now: <leader>Gh (inside a repo) opens gh-dash (PRs and issues), gh-observer (the current PR's checks, live), gh-enhance (Actions runs and logs) and gh-news (notifications) in their own terminals. The first launch of a missing extension shows the exact gh extension install … command and installs and starts it after you confirm; <leader>Ghs is the checklist. octo.nvim adds PRs, issues and reviews inside Neovim (Ghp/Ghi/Ghr), with the core gh commands (v, c, b, a, A) next to them.
  • Remotes from the Git menu: GP pushes (setting the upstream the first time), Gu pulls with --ff-only (a divergent branch is reported, never merged behind your back) and GF fetches every remote with --prune. They run in the integrated terminal, which keeps the focus, and Esc closes the window without stopping the command.
  • The chat has profiles and a home: Zed-style Ask (read-only), Write (the full coding partner) and Minimal (terse), saved with each session; <leader>aC on a project with conversations opens its history, newest first, instead of a blank buffer.
  • Go runs the package you are in: run/build run the current file's package in repos with several mains (root, cmd/api, cmd/worker…), and <leader>gl tests that package (go test ./folder, a main package with tests included) while m/R/c keep testing the whole module. Commands launched from the menus keep the cursor in the terminal.
  • Find: a visual selection pre-fills grug-far's search (<leader>fr), so replacing what you selected is one key away.

Features

  • chat profiles, GitHub menu and git remotes (e128fcc)
  • go: run the current file's package and test it in place (80b707a)
  • find: prefill grug-far search from visual selection (2a9616e)

Fixes

  • github: keep <leader>G intact outside repos (73a4ed9)

[1.15.0] - 2026-11-09

Highlights

  • The terminal stops surprising you: terminal.run went through :TermExec string parsing, which cut every quoted path (dotnet run --project '/a b/x.csproj' arrived as dotnet run --project). It now uses toggleterm's Lua API, and <leader>T (show / list / new / stop) reopens terminals without re-sending anything into them.
  • C# grows up: a Solution Explorer (<leader>cLo), saveable run configurations with Docker (<leader>cGr), a visual NuGet manager (<leader>cx), requests.http generated from Program.cs routes (<leader>ch) and a Noodle collection with its TUI (<leader>cNo).
  • Razor, XAML and Unity join the pack: .razor/.cshtml (html LSP), .xaml (as XML) and Unity YAML files (.unity, .prefab, .meta) with Assets/ root detection.

Features

  • terminal: <leader>T menu, named terminals, honest docs (6f9ee10)
  • csharp: explorer, run configs, nuget, http file, noodle (014f9cf)
  • csharp: razor, xaml and unity packs (88e8cff)
  • chat: persistent backend-agnostic chat under Leader (e38aa2f)
  • setup: langsetup all languages, sections, reconcile (56a0fa7)
  • find: dev-mode search in current file (8ac279f)
  • leader: leader is nvim in a terminal (d1e25bd)
  • skills: add Skills manager (<leader>aK) (705a945)
  • env: environment file kit per stack with check and AI loader (4cd29f6)
  • markdown: auto locked reading mode for md and mdx (03ea13e)
  • find: add <leader>fc for current file (35fa7fa)
  • languages: C#/.NET pack and MDX as Markdown (1ba0993)
  • pacakge: update (0b45ae3)

Fixes

  • markdown: no cursor highlight in locked reading mode (f25c6eb)
  • agent: build survives slow, rate-limited OpenCode (e2b1e54)

Other changes

  • chore(deps): update pinned plugins (634b1ef)
  • chore(packaging): point the Arch packages at v1.14.2 (a9dc10b)

[1.14.2] - 2026-10-21

Highlights

  • An empty file can be a right answer: a build used to report a failure for every file the model left empty, including the ones that are correct that way (a Python __init__.py, a .gitkeep, py.typed, the requirements file of a project with no dependencies). It now does for those what it should: nothing.

Fixes

  • build: an empty __init__.py is not a failed answer (5ecfbb0)

[1.14.1] - 2026-10-21

Highlights

  • Checked against a real build: a build of a small project with Claude Haiku (13 files, $0.03, under 3 minutes) did what the 1.14.0 notes promise: it named its riskiest requirement, deferred the one SPEC.md left open and said why, ended ⚠ built · 3/4 requirements have a test instead of ✓, and left a .leader/ folder with nothing of the project in it. Two details came out of it and are fixed here: the plan review no longer calls a test that uses a fake or a temporary directory a "stand-in", and the builder's own wiring check is recorded under its name.

Fixes

  • build: tests are not stand-ins, name the wiring check (1ff9203)

[1.14.0] - 2026-10-21

Highlights

  • A build no longer calls itself done when it only compiles: the closing line now says what is proven, e.g. ◐ built · 23 files · checks pass · 5/17 requirements have a test · core without one: FR-16 · 2 placeholders, and it is ✓ only when every requirement has a test and nothing is open. PROGRESS.md carries the same proof and what is open. A layer build (foundation, design, domain) reports the numbers but keeps ✓; kit.build.strict makes an unproven build a failure.
  • A repair cannot delete your code quietly: before a replan trashes files, the review lists what goes (lines, public types, tests, who still uses them) and asks, and the closing line counts the public declarations a build removed or the tests it made unproven.
  • Layers build on the code that is there: a whole-project build used to plan from the documents and the ADRs' text only, so it wrote the code earlier builds had left a second time under other names (two ports, two controllers, hundreds of analyzer errors). It now sees the project's files and their API. A free check fails when a public type is declared in two files, Flutter projects run flutter test and Rust ones cargo test, and plans name their risks and what they defer, with a warning for stand-ins of core requirements.
  • .leader/ in every project the builder touches: how Leader worked there (how builds end, which checks fail, what a model costs, whether you accept a proposal) as counts, sizes, durations, costs and outcomes, for measuring and improving Leader from real use. Offline: nothing is ever sent. It is never your work: a schema is the only gate, so unknown fields are dropped, file names are salted hashes, and there is no code, prompt, answer, name or text. The folder documents itself, ignores itself in git, <leader>aSl shows the report, LEADER_NO_DATA=1 turns it off, and deleting it clears everything.

Features

  • build: honest builds and a local .leader folder (1cb1c6e)

Fixes

  • build: skip examples in the duplicate check, hide custom models (bebfb1d)

Documentation

  • build: proof, loss gate, duplicates and the .leader folder (94a536e)

Chores

  • deps: update the plugin lockfile (3ab99c6)

[1.13.0] - 2026-10-21

Highlights

  • The agent is measured now, and a modest model gets help: make eval runs 31 cases (Python, Go, JavaScript, Lua; fix, docs, refactor, review) through the real pipeline against any model, judged by code (does it parse, does a script that imports it still pass, did anything outside the target move, did it find the planted bug), never by another model. On the committed baselines Claude Haiku passes 100% of the single tries on the cases it was tuned on at roughly a tenth of Sonnet's cost per usable answer. Every model gets a capability profile (small, medium, strong): a smaller target limit, a second try with the reason when an answer does not parse, server-shaped output for Ollama and OpenAI, a chain of single steps for an instruction that is several things, and an optional cascade to the main model.
  • You are told what is going to happen, and can take it back: the spinner carries the estimate, every proposal says whether anything checks it (checked on accept (LSP) or unverified), the statusline shows what the session has spent, <leader>au takes a whole run back across files (never what you edited since) and <leader>aSbu takes the last build back.
  • Less on the menu: DDD, the repository/service/lens menus, the doctor, building a whole project and multi-file plans are experimental and stay hidden until agent.experimental (Settings → Agent) is on. Nothing was removed.
  • Review stops crying wolf: it no longer reports a finding that says nothing is wrong, nor hypothetical misuse; on the eval, Haiku went from 82% to 100% on review without losing the real findings.

Features

  • agent: evals, capability profiles, trust layer, experimental flag (3777b62)

Documentation

  • agent: evals, profiles, trust layer and stability (4cef712)

[1.12.2] - 2026-10-21

Highlights

  • Your nvim now speaks Herdr: Herdr is the terminal workspace that runs the coding agents, and Leader talks to its CLI. <leader>Wa opens the breakdown of the workspace: one row per agent grouped by workspace (its state, the agent and where it is), the transcript of the one under the cursor and why Herdr thinks it is in that state (the rule that matched). It refreshes on its own, so what is on screen is the state now. <leader>Ws is the setup: it detects the binary, the version, the server and the agents' integrations, and installs what is missing (nothing without the button) — the installer does the same, and the start screen gets an Agents button when Herdr is there.
  • And Herdr sees what your nvim is doing: nvim registers itself as an agent of the workspace and says what it is doing (working while the inline agent runs, blocked while a proposal waits for you, idle otherwise), so the Agents panel of Herdr shows it. It never takes the pane from a real agent that already owns it, and a headless nvim (a script, a test) does not register at all.

Features

  • herdr: the workspace of agents under <leader>W (135c337)

[1.12.1] - 2026-10-21

Highlights

  • The code search index builds ~3x sooner and no longer freezes the editor: on a 4000-file TypeScript monorepo the first <leader>fs took 19-35 s with 12-22 s of blocked UI (stalls up to 1 s); it now takes 6-10 s with the UI blocked ~0.2 s. The files are read by up to 4 headless Neovims in parallel (settings.search.workers); the result is identical to reading them in the editor, which is still what happens for a few changed files or if a worker fails.
  • Typing is smoother: nvim-highlight-colors repainted the window on every key while the completion menu was open; it now waits 60 ms. In a TSX file the per-key cost went from p95 8.8-9.8 ms to 6.1-7.5 ms (the frame budget is 16.6 ms).
  • Less memory in big projects: Tailwind's server only starts where the project uses Tailwind (~210 MB less in the API of a monorepo), TypeScript is linted by the eslint server alone (eslint_d stays for light mode), and a server that outgrows settings.lsp.memory_limit_mb (vtsls: 2200 MB; it reached 2.9 GB in a long session) is restarted when you are idle. scripts/bench-project.lua measures all of this on any project.

Performance

  • lsp: fewer resident servers, and tsserver cannot grow forever (44a781a)
  • ui: highlight-colors repaints behind a 60 ms debounce (d1d39ba)
  • search: the code index is built by parallel workers (08ddc7e)

Other changes

  • chore(bench): a probe for a real project (5aa2719)
  • chore(packaging): point the PKGBUILDs at v1.12.0 (3995f15)

[1.12.0] - 2026-10-20

Highlights

  • Lua answers across files ~2.5x sooner: Lua now uses emmylua_ls (a Rust server) when it is installed, and lua_ls otherwise. On this repo's 670 files cross-file go-to-definition worked at ~0.7 s against ~3.8 s on the bare workspace, and ~2.5x sooner with the full config, with the same hover, completion and diagnostics. lua_ls keeps nothing between sessions and its maxPreload has no effect, so the way to speed it up was a faster server. Mason installs it with the rest; settings.lsp.lua_server forces either one.
  • <leader>dr runs and stops a Flutter app: pressed again while the app runs, it quits it and closes the debugger and every panel that opened, instead of only saying it is already running.
  • Your sessions are safe from tests: Neovim saves a session on every exit, and a headless test that opened temp files in a repo replaced that folder's real session with files that no longer existed, so restoring the project opened an empty buffer. Persistence now stays off without a UI, every test script runs against a throwaway state directory, and the whole suite leaves sessions, recent files, agent history and logs byte-identical.

Features

  • dart: leader dr toggles run/stop (fffd15c)

Fixes

  • sessions: tests and headless runs never write your state (db55542)

Performance

  • lsp: Lua gets emmylua_ls when it is installed (~2.5x faster) (ccc1a54)

[1.11.0] - 2026-10-20

Highlights

  • Big files stay fast: past 1.5 MB (or a minified bundle) a file opens as plain text, with no treesitter, LSP, folds, lint or formatter. A 4 MB Lua file opens in ~135 ms instead of ~1.9 s, and its first frame in the window matches a one-line file's.
  • Saving never freezes: a buffer over 3000 lines is written at once and formatted in the background. A 15k-line save goes from ~1.2 s to ~20 ms. Linting is debounced, so a burst of Esc and saves lints once.
  • Stop a language server that takes too long: <leader>L stops the servers of a buffer (also in the middle of "Loading workspace", in ~13 ms), stops all of them, restarts them, pauses everything, or says what is loading. A server that has not answered yet is found too. The editor cannot cache a server's workspace load, so this is the way out.
  • Code search remembers: the index is kept on disk, so only the very first search parses every file (~5 s here); every later session loads it in ~120 ms. The first <leader>ff of a session is ~2x faster too.
  • A cleaner, faster start: the window opens on the themed editor, never on a black frame, and the real editor reaches the screen ~45 ms sooner (graphics driver and Neovim start in parallel with the window, the first frame is warmed).
  • leader --reuse file opens files in the window that is already running in ~6 ms, without a new window or Neovim. Opt-in; a plain leader is unchanged.
  • Measure it yourself: make bench (startup, first frame, big files, picker, search index) and make bench-edit (big paste, undo, save, diagnostics, typing latency), with the findings and limits in AGENTS.md.

Features

  • lsp: stop, restart and pause language servers (<leader>L) (ecaf792)
  • gui: a full-screen theme picker (0770236)

Performance

  • app: a faster, cleaner start and a window you can reuse (21d5427)
  • editor: big files, saves, lint, search index, picker preload (c185df4)

Other changes

  • chore(bench): make bench and make bench-edit, and what they found (859c6b9)
  • chore(dev): a Makefile and a real-time loop for the app (09ebf01)
  • chore(packaging): point the PKGBUILDs at v1.10.0 (5b5cf42)

[1.10.0] - 2026-10-20

Highlights

  • Install it the way your system expects: a .deb for Debian, Ubuntu, Mint and Pop!_OS, PKGBUILDs for Arch and CachyOS (prebuilt and from source), the one-command installer for any Linux and WSL, and ./install.sh --app from a clone. They add the menu entry, the icons, the manual (man leader) and leader-setup, and uninstalling removes exactly what was installed.
  • Signed releases: every file has a SHA-256 and a detached signature by the Leader Releases key (fingerprint in packaging/README.md). The installer requires a signature made by exactly that key when gpg is installed, and refuses a forged or missing one.
  • The logo, clear: the N is the window icon, the menu icon at every size (a solid N when small, the dotted one when large) and the art of the About dialog. The app id is io.github.sazardev.Leader, so launchers pair the window with its icon and menu entry.
  • First launch that explains itself: no Neovim says so in a notification; no Neovim config runs leader-setup in the window's own terminal. AppStream metadata, a New Window action, a license (MIT) and a site with Open Graph cards, structured data, a sitemap and real screenshots.

Features

  • site: SEO, screenshots, install options for every system (eab5dbc)
  • packaging: signed releases, a .deb and Arch PKGBUILDs (3ebec37)
  • app: the logo everywhere, an app id, first-launch setup (035cbea)

[1.9.0] - 2026-10-20

Highlights

  • Projects first, like an IDE: Leader asks which project to open when it starts (or reopens the last one, or shows the start screen: your choice), leader <folder> opens that folder, and starting inside a repo opens that repo. The picker keeps its facts and the list it found on disk, reads git in the background one folder at a time, pins projects (p), and a Yazi-like browser (b: h up, l in, Enter opens) walks your folders.
  • Updates, connected: a ↑ 1.9.0 badge in the bar and a line at the top of the logo's menu when a newer version exists; a page shows what changed version by version and updates on request (the config with git pull --ff-only, the app through its installer). One tiny request, never more often than every 12 hours, never updates by itself.
  • Recent files that belong to the project you are in, not everything you ever opened (Ctrl+A widens it).
  • Esc closes whatever is extra: help, quickfix, panels, floats, pickers (from insert too), lone tabs; never the last window. The hidden cursor has one owner list now, so it can no longer stay invisible after pages overlap.
  • Install progress in numbers: every language tool with its state, time, bytes and speed, a bar, and a warning when data stops arriving. The window remembers its size, place and maximized state; the frame is yours to pick.
  • Speed: hovering never scrolls a list, hovers are coalesced, Telescope no longer leaks a buffer per open, streaming answers redraw at most every 40 ms, and a list that fails to open leaves nothing behind.

Features

  • gui: projects first, a folder browser, install progress, Esc (e9ccc65)
  • app: keep the window's size, place and maximized state (fd876ef)
  • ui: one owner list for the hidden cursor, Esc closes extra windows (0157e10)
  • update: detect new versions, show the changelog, update on request (3eac257)
  • recent: recent files that belong to the project you are in (9ac6d49)

Nothing runs until you press Install; sudo asks in a terminal and each item shows its status. A review with the install checks closes it.
