[1.7.0] - 2026-10-19

Highlights

  • Leader for the desktop (Linux and WSL): leader, a GPU window for the editor written in Rust. The same config, drawn in one GPU draw call per frame, nothing running between keystrokes, ~170 ms to the first frame. Its own bar in the theme's colors: Zed-like menus (File, Edit, Selection, View, Go, Run, Git, Agent, Window, Help), a command palette (Ctrl+Shift+P), the projects you work in (switched with their sessions) and a full-screen project picker (Ctrl+Shift+O), the current file as a clickable path, live activity (LSP indexing, installs, saves). Full mouse: menus, pickers, modals, Telescope, the tree and the start screen follow the pointer and answer a click. A welcome screen checks the install and fixes what is missing. Install it in one command (curl -fsSL https://github.com/sazardev/leader/releases/latest/download/leader-installer.sh | bash: the app, plus Neovim and the config when they are missing; Linux x86_64 with glibc 2.31+, WSL with WSLg) or from a clone with ./install.sh --app.
  • Doctor (<leader>aX): a troubleshooting agent for builds that fail, apps that will not start or crash. Free checks first (dependencies, lockfile, .env, toolchain versions, a merge in progress), then one confirmed request.
  • Merge conflicts (<leader>Gx): trivial ones resolved for free, the rest one proposal per conflict.
  • Language packs: Python, TypeScript, Rust, SQL, Swift, JVM and more as packs, with tests in the editor, project setup presets, debugging and a SQL runner. The installer asks once which packs you want.
  • A full-screen marketplace for SPEC.md modules and STACK.md / DESIGN.md add-ons, and a bigger library of each.
  • Leader is the new name (repo sazardev/leader), with a brand kit, wallpapers and a scripted product video.

[1.6.0] - 2026-10-19

Highlights

  • Build by layer (<leader>aSb then t, s or d): the foundation from STACK.md, the domain from SPEC.md or the screens from DESIGN.md, each built on top of the files the project already has. bb still builds everything at once.
  • Build one feature (<leader>aSbf): pick a requirement of SPEC.md, or describe a new one in your words; it is added to SPEC.md as the next FR-n (after a confirmation) and built end to end, following the patterns the code already uses.
  • Recipes (<leader>aSr): twelve prefab tasks for your stack (a Flutter screen with its state, a data feature or a form; a Go endpoint, entity with its repository, CLI command or Bubble Tea screen; web components and pages; TypeScript, Python and Rust endpoints). Each is a plain Markdown file you can copy and adapt.
  • Coverage (<leader>aSv, 0 tokens): which requirements have a test, what STACK.md names that the manifest lacks, DESIGN.md tokens no code uses, folders no document mentions.
  • Decisions and progress (0 tokens): every accepted plan records its new decisions as ADRs in docs/adr/, which later builds follow (reversing one is explicit: Supersedes ADR NNNN); PROGRESS.md says what was done, what is open and what is next. <leader>aSP lists them.
  • Release kit (<leader>GR): this changelog and the next version from the Conventional Commits (c), a pull request description (p, one light request, copied to the clipboard) and a release checklist from STACK.md (l). It never tags, pushes or posts.
  • Flutter: run on a chosen device, profile mode, quieter runs. opencode 2.x CLI supported.

Features

  • agent: layer and feature builds, recipes, ADRs, release kit (b2644fd)
  • agent: support the opencode 2.x CLI (790cf02)
  • dart: run on a chosen device, profile mode, quiet runs (b109513)

Fixes

  • release: write other changes as the commit is written (8e93451)

Features · docs/cheatsheets/agent.md

AI agent

One-shot actions on what you are looking at: a function, a class or a selection. No chat, no sidebar, no conversation — except the Chat (<leader>aC, agent/chat/): the one persistent, backend-agnostic chat under Leader (see Chat). Every one-shot action has a closed prompt, a bounded context and an output with a fixed format, so it is predictable and cheap. Keys: CHEATSHEET.md (Agent menu).

Chat (<leader>aC, agent/chat/)

A right half-screen partition with a read-only transcript and an input box, like Opencode/Claude Code but orchestrated by Leader: pick any backend/model on the machine (claude, opencode, ollama, openai, custom). The picker lists every model the backend knows (opencode: all opencode models, curated fast ones first), not just the one-shot shortlist. Pick effort, get streaming answers with thinking. Everything is shown: the blue header (profile · model · turns · tokens · cost), what was injected per turn (rules, buffer, diagnostics, git, skills, mcp, @files) and the turn's tokens/cost/time. Hiding the panel (Ctrl-Q, or <leader>aC while it has the focus) keeps the session — a single statusline chip (Write · deepseek-v4.1-flash) keeps the progress from any buffer. The panel is a real right split (the editor shrinks beside it, like a terminal opened with Alt-V); Esc only leaves insert and q does nothing, so it stays while you work. The transcript is icon-free and padded: a blank fold column on the left, a wrap margin on the right and a row of air above the brand bar; the composer starts at three rows and grows up to input_height. The user turn is a filled bubble with its own top/bottom air, and the sections use faint uppercase labels and the flat yellow key color. An empty chat and the history home carry a plain-text reference of the commands, mentions and keys, so the panel explains itself without opening /help. Sessions persist per project (stdpath("state")/agent-chat.json); every turn also lands in the agent history, so the spend/budget guards apply unchanged.

  • Profiles (Zed-style, Ctrl-P or /profile): Ask answers about the code and never proposes edits; Write is the full coding partner (plans and code changes); Minimal skips the automatic context and the token/thinking chrome for the fastest, terse answer. The profile is saved with the session and shown in the header and the statusline chip.
  • History on open: the first <leader>aC of a project with chats lands on its home: New chat first, then that repo's sessions (title, age, messages, model), newest first. ↑/↓ move, ⏎ opens, n starts a new one, D deletes the selected chat after a confirmation. Typing in the input starts a new chat without leaving the home. Only sessions of the current repository are listed; opening the chat in another repo shows that repo's history.
  • Send: ⏎ sends at once with the current model (session pick > <leader>am > the backend's default). It never stops to ask for a model: a backend that needs one and has none resolves it silently (the CLI's first) and says Chat: using <model>. Shift-⏎/Ctrl-J newline.
  • Input triggers (a filtered selector under the cursor; ⏎/Tab accepts, ↑/↓ moves, Esc closes it): / at the start of the line → commands (/new, /history, /model, /effort, /profile, /clear, /adr, /export, /full, /mini, /collapse, /help, run as soon as you pick one); @ → project files (tracked + untracked, gitignore respected; it inserts @path) plus @buf, @sel, @diag; # → skills; $ → MCP servers. ↑/↓ with no selector recalls the previous user messages, ↓ past the newest returns to your draft. The input grows with its content (input_height is the maximum), Esc leaves insert (or closes the selector) without hiding the panel, and Ctrl-Q hides it.
  • View: the default is the real half split: a dedicated right column, full usable height, the editor reflowed to the left (like Alt-V), the input as its own surface at the bottom. Ctrl-F toggles half ↔ full screen (:Chat full/:Chat mini, /full, /mini); full is borderless and edge to edge, with the single statusline still free. settings.agent.chat.dock = false (also in <leader>Sp) goes back to the old floating partition. Ctrl-T collapses to the right rail. Keys: Ctrl-N new, Ctrl-H history, Ctrl-M model, Ctrl-E effort, Ctrl-P profile, Ctrl-A ADR, Ctrl-X stop, Ctrl-Q hide, Ctrl-Y copy last answer, Alt-Y copy your last message, Alt-Q answer the last question. Esc/q never close the panel.
  • Markdown: answers are rendered with treesitter and render-markdown as soon as the turn settles (streaming stays raw and cheap): accent heading bands, real code blocks with their language name on a deeper surface, inline code, quotes, links, tables, checkboxes and bullets. No icons: headings, list bullets and task boxes keep their source text (-, [ ], the heading text) instead of glyphs, and code blocks show the language name without a language icon. Code blocks are highlighted by treesitter: an apply fence (language: path) is shown as language in the transcript, and an unlabeled block whose first line starts with a common command is shown as bash; copy and apply keep the original text. The transcript keeps the source verbatim, so copying is byte-exact; CRLF, ANSI escapes and control characters are cleaned for display AND copy, tabs and wide characters are preserved.
  • Transcript: y copies the message under the cursor, c the fenced code block under it (the clipboard gets the code, not the fences), Y the whole transcript, o/⏎ opens that message full size (markdown; y copies it there too), h history, i back to the input. Copy without aiming: Ctrl-Y copies the last answer and Alt-Y the last message you sent, from the transcript or straight from the composer in insert mode (where the focus stays after sending), so no walking to the message is needed.
  • Questions with options: when the model needs a decision it ends with a fenced ```ask block (the question, then - option lines); the transcript shows it as a quote with an Alt-Q hint. Alt-Q opens a selector (it opens by itself only when the composer has the focus and is empty), the chosen option lands in the composer without being sent so it can be edited, and "Write my own answer…" just focuses the input. If the panel is not in front of you when the turn settles, one note says the answer (or the question) is ready (settings.agent.chat.notify).
  • Context the model asks for: an answer can request files with @need: path/one, path/two (project-relative). Leader reads those files (inside the project, capped, at most 5) and injects them in the next turn, shown in that turn's injected footer like any @file; the request is used once. settings.agent.chat.auto_need = false disables it.
  • Queue: typing while an answer streams no longer gets refused: Enter queues the message (the placeholder counts what waits) and it is sent as soon as the turn settles, in order. Ctrl-X stops the turn and the queue still goes.
  • Long chats say what was left out: only the last max_history_chars (60 000; Minimal 12 000) reach the model. When older turns are dropped the turn footer says history: oldest N chars not sent and the first time a note names it.
  • Write mode writes files: a Write answer returns every changed file as a complete fenced block with its path (the info string is go: main.go); when the turn settles Leader applies those blocks to the project (existing files only, loaded buffers must be saved, a closed file is backed up to the trash first) and the turn footer says applied main.go. A on any assistant message applies its blocks again (useful for an old answer or after saving a buffer); settings.agent.chat.apply = false turns it off.
  • ADR: /adr (or Ctrl-A) drafts an ADR from the chat into docs/adr/ (same shape as the build's ADRs).
  • Settings (settings.agent.chat): view (mini = real half split / full), dock (true = real split, false = the old floating partition), apply (true = Write answers write their full-file blocks), width (fraction of columns, 0.5), profile (default write), history_on_open, rail_width, input_height (max rows of the composer, which starts at 3), gap, show_context, show_thinking, show_tokens, max_history_chars, max_context_chars, max_mention_chars, notify, ask_picker, auto_need, timeout_ms, autosave.

Clear file moved from <leader>aC to <leader>aW (:AgentClear file still works).

Agent skills (<leader>aK): the SKILL.md files the agents load on demand (marketplace, 0-config install by model/stack/cloud, tolerance for legacy/already-worked repos) — see skills.md.

Flow

  1. Put the cursor inside a function (or select lines in visual mode) and press <leader>a + the action.
  2. The code being sent is highlighted and a spinner turns on the right: ⠋ implement · claude/haiku · 1.2s. Requests with no code to paint (the project documents of <leader>aS, including the screens of <leader>aSw, the commit draft, the staged review, plans) show a badge in the statusline from any buffer: ⠹ drawing screens, then ✓ drawing screens (or a red ✗) for a few seconds after it ends. <leader>ax cancels them too.
  3. The result shows up in the code, depending on the action:
ActionInputResult
i implementfunction (its name, signature, doc or TODO are the spec) + optional instructioninline diff: deleted lines in red, added lines in green below. ⏎ accepts, ⎋ rejects
r reviewfunction or selectionreal findings as diagnostics on their line (◆); <leader>xd shows the detail
e explainfunction or selectionwindow next to the code, streaming; closes by itself when the cursor moves, or with Esc from the code (while it is still streaming, Esc also stops the call)
a askyour question (text field)window, same as explain
f fix inlinethe diagnostic (LSP, linter or agent finding) on the cursor line + optional hintdiff with the minimal change on only those lines; the surrounding code and the objects it points to (the variable's type, the signature of what is called) go along as read-only context; with no diagnostic on the line, it does nothing
F fix blockall the diagnostics of the function (or selection) + optional hintdiff with the minimal change that leaves the block coherent (several diagnostics with the same cause are fixed together)
d docsfunction + optional focusdiff that only adds/updates the documentation comment; the model returns only the comment (agent/docplace.lua puts it above the function, or inside it for Python), so the code cannot change and the output is a few lines
t convertselection (or the paragraph under the cursor) holding a JSON sample, a SQL CREATE TABLE or a curl command, in a code file + optional root type namediff that replaces it with ready code in the file's language: JSON → Go structs with json tags and nested types (TS interfaces, JSDoc typedefs in JavaScript, Python dataclasses, Rust serde structs, Dart classes with fromJson/toJson…), a table → a struct with db tags and pointers for nullable columns, curl → a function that sends the request with the standard client (secrets become parameters). What each source becomes is fixed per language (agent/convert.lua), so the prompt is explicit; light model, only rules as context. A one-word instruction names the root type (Invoice); in a non-code file it refuses (paste the JSON where the types should go). In Dart it follows pubspec.yaml: JSON → freezed classes (or @JsonSerializable()), a table → a drift Table; the import and part lines are added on accept (convert_header.lua on proposal.accepted, rules in utils/dartheader.lua) and a snake_case key left without @JsonKey(name: …) gets it by rule (Dart / Flutter)
n next editthe 30 lines around the cursor + what you just changed in this filediff with the ONE edit that follows from your recent changes (the rename you are following through, the field added to one constructor and not the others); with nothing changed in the file it asks nothing (Next edit)
R refactoryour instruction (text field)diff that applies exactly that instruction
g handoffyour instructionsends file:range + instruction to an agent already running in Herdr
aA + a fix allnone (the file's findings and diagnostics)one proposal per function that holds any, asked once with the total estimate; they wait in a queue (Several proposals at once)
aA + o / O fix from outputnone / a commandthe errors a build printed (last terminal, quickfix, or a command you run), one proposal per function they point into
aA + p plan across filesyour instruction (text field) on a type or functionan editable plan of steps, then one proposal per step (Multi-file plans)

The instruction field is inline (like the inline chat in Zed or VS Code): it opens right ABOVE the target code and pushes the code down instead of covering it, with an accent bar, a header (✦ implement · claude/sonnet) and a placeholder. It grows as you type (up to 6 rows), ⏎ sends, ⎋ cancels (also if you switch windows), ⌃J / Alt+Enter insert a line break and ↑/↓ (or ⌃P/⌃N) walk through the previous instructions of that action, which are remembered across sessions. In narrow windows the key hint shortens itself. There is no autocompletion or auto-closing of quotes inside.

Instruction: i, d, t and f open the field and Enter with an empty field runs without an instruction; if you type something, it is appended to the prompt and takes precedence over the default behaviour. Useful for an empty func main() {} ("read a CSV and sum column 3"), for an unclear function ("this validates orders before charging; the coupon case is missing") or to steer a fix. a ask and R refactor require the text; r review and e explain run instantly.

Short menu: i implement, d docs, t convert, n next edit, r review, e explain, a ask, f fix inline, F fix block, R refactor, g hand off (only inside Herdr), C chat (the persistent Chat), h history, m model (main, light and effort in a single selector), c clear (discards the finding on the line, or those in the selection in visual mode), W clear file (those in this file), x stop (cancels what is running and discards the pending proposals; it does not touch findings) and l last answer (reopens the last answer or rejected proposal). Everything that works on many pieces at once lives in its own Advanced menu, <leader>aA, so the everyday one stays short: a fix all, o / O fix from output / from a command, p plan across files, and for the proposals they leave waiting ] / [ next / previous, y accept all, l list. Accepting and rejecting a proposal have no menu entry: they are ⏎ and ⎋ on the proposal (and Tab refines it).

Accepting is a single change: u undoes it entirely. If you edit the code while the agent is working, the proposal is discarded instead of overwriting your change. Several actions can run at once (not on the same block).

Which target it takes

Priority: visual selection (always whole lines) > function under the cursor (with the contiguous comments above it, which are the spec) > class > paragraph of lines around the cursor (useful for a half-written signature: type func ParseConfig(path string) (*Config, error) and press <leader>ai). "Function/class" is the definition of af/ac (treesitter textobjects), for any language with a parser. More than context.max_target_lines (400) is rejected: trimming it would break the replacement.

Context (and why it is cheap)

Each action asks for providers; together they are capped at context.max_chars (8000 characters), the target is counted separately:

ProviderWhat it contributesCost
rulessettings.agent.rules + .agent.md at the project root (your conventions)text
typesthe definition of the types the target uses (fields of a struct, members of a class/interface/enum/trait), so the model does not guess Note.Donelocal + LSP
contractsif the target is an adapter (PostgresOrderRepository): the port it must satisfy (with path), which methods already exist and which are missing, the entity the port names and, when implementing, another implementation in the project as a style example (constructor, errors, mapping)rg + treesitter
schemafor a persistence adapter: the entity's real table (SQL migrations with their later ALTERs/indexes, or schema.prisma) and the database in use: dialect, driver, parameter style ($1, ?, @p1)rg + manifests
diagnosticsLSP/linter diagnostics inside the target, each one with its code line (the target is not numbered)local
calleesREAL signature of what the target calls, requested from the LSP (hover); whatever does not arrive within 700 ms is omittedLSP
outlineimports + first line of each function (without repeating the types already included in types)local

types is language-independent (it uses the same treesitter queries as af/ac): Go (struct, interface, alias), TypeScript (interface, class, type, enum), Python, Rust (a method of impl Note brings struct Note, not the impl), Java... A type in the same file is read instantly; one in another file or package is located with the LSP (textDocument/definition) and extracted from the destination file (project only, not dependencies). Field types are followed one level (Tags []Tag), and if the target is a class method, the class is included without the method itself. Cap: context.types (4) definitions of context.type_lines (30) lines. Each definition is compacted first (compact in providers/types.lua): full-line comments and blank lines go (context.type_comments = true keeps comments), and runs of bare enum values are joined, at most 24 shown and the rest counted (… (188 more)). Measured on a Flutter widget whose build uses two enums with a doc comment per value: the <types> section went from 3,243 to 871 characters. Definitions are never taken from generated files (x.freezed.dart repeats the class ~5x longer; utils/generated.lua).

Each action asks for what it needs, in priority order (if the budget gets tight, the last ones are trimmed):

ActionContext
implementrules, contracts, schema, types, callees, outline, diagnostics
portrules, contracts, schema, types, diagnostics
reviewrules, diagnostics, contracts, schema, types, callees
fix (inline)rules, diagnostics, findings, contracts, around (surrounding code), types, callees
fix blockrules, diagnostics, findings, contracts, types, callees
refactorrules, types, callees, outline
askrules, types, callees, outline, diagnostics
explainrules, types, callees
docsrules, types (documents the real fields and parameters, without paying for outline or callees)
convertrules (the selection is the whole input)

Transparency (note:): after the code block the model may add note: ... (max. ~15 words) when it assumed or added something that was not in the context, and needs-import: .... Both show up in the proposal hint (note: Note has no Done field; assumed one) and are kept in the history. Measured in a test with SetDone without types: it guessed Done and Updated and said so; with types: the same code, with no warning, -70 % output tokens and -25 % cost.

Measured with Claude: a normal claude -p call drags ~46 000 input tokens (your plugins, skills, hooks and memory). With --setting-sources "", --tools "" and its own --system-prompt it drops to ~600 tokens (+ the context), about 2 s and fractions of a cent per action (haiku). The CLIs run in a neutral directory (stdpath("cache")/agent), so they do not load the AGENTS.md/CLAUDE.md of your repo either. That is why --bare is not used: it would break subscription login.

Cost audit (measured): every claude -p call was billed as a 1-hour cache write (up to 2× the input price) and never hit the cache, because the message changes on every call. With DISABLE_PROMPT_CACHING=1 (setting agent.claude.prompt_cache = false, the default) a Sonnet action went from $0.0065 to $0.0037 and a Haiku one from $0.0005 to $0.0003, with no change in latency. The system prompts were also shortened ~35 % (implement 1462 → 943 characters) and now state what is true (types, callees, diagnostics) and that whatever was assumed must be flagged with note:. In total, the same action costs ~50 % less than before the audit. About ~560 fixed input tokens from the CLI itself remain, which no safe flag trims (CLAUDE_CODE_SIMPLE and --bare break subscription login). agent.claude.max_cost_usd (0.25) cuts off a runaway call.

Order of the prompt (stable first, volatile last)

The sections reach the prompt arranged by each provider's stability: 1 the same on every call of the project (rules, testing), 2 changes only when the code does (types, outline, contracts, schema, ports, ddd, testfile, callees), 3 changes with the cursor or the editor (diagnostics, findings, around, callers, gitlog, changes, subject). The declared order still decides what survives when the budget is tight; only what is SENT is rearranged. What this buys depends on the backend:

  • Ollama / llama.cpp / vLLM keep the KV cache of the last prompt and reuse what is identical from the start: a second action on the same code re-reads only the tail. For that, Ollama's num_ctx is now sticky per model (a small action after a big one keeps the big window, otherwise Ollama reloads the model and drops that cache).
  • claude -p gets almost nothing from it: the CLI sends the whole message as ONE block and the fixed part is under the cache's minimum size. That is why prompt_cache stays off (measured: -40% per call); if you turn it on, <leader>ah shows cache_read/cache_write per run so you can check it pays. The answer cache is what makes repeated questions free there.
  • A new provider declares stability = 1|2|3 next to its cap (default 2).

Models per tier (cheap where it is enough)

Each action runs on the main model or the light one. docs, explain and convert are light (Claude: haiku); the rest use the main one (sonnet by default). Measured with Claude, a single-function action: ~$0.005 on sonnet vs ~$0.0003 on haiku, about 17 times less, and the spinner shows the model that is running. Light actions do not inherit the effort either: they are the cheap ones and must not become expensive because of a setting meant for others.

  • <leader>am opens a selector with the main model (and the backend), the light model and the effort, with their current value. Everything is remembered.
  • Change which actions are light: agent = { tiers = { review = "light", docs = "main" } } in local.lua.
  • agent.claude.light_model / agent.opencode.light_model set the default light model. In OpenCode both default to opencode-go/deepseek-v4.1-flash (ids depend on your provider: pick another in <leader>am).

Backend and model

<leader>am → Main model opens ONE fuzzy list with the models of all installed backends (Claude: haiku/sonnet/opus; OpenCode: the cheap, fast shortlist in agent.opencode.models, or whatever opencode models returns if that is nil). Choosing one also switches the backend and is remembered across sessions (stdpath("state")/agent.json). Effort (Claude only; --effort) is in the same selector. With backend = "auto" the first available one in agent.order is used.

  • Claude Code (claude -p): no tools, no session on disk, text streaming.
  • OpenCode (opencode run): each action travels as an ephemeral agent with no tools (OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT). Your global MCP servers are turned off for these runs (agent.opencode.mcp = true keeps them): their tool schemas were ~20k input tokens and ~2 s per action. With agent.opencode.serve (default) a private opencode serve (free loopback port, random password, no MCP, stopped on exit, off in light mode) boots in the background and actions talk to its HTTP API with curl on a session created ahead: ~2 s per action instead of ~8 s. The first action (while it boots) is cold. The CLI line is detected (opencode --version): 2.x changes the inline schema (agents/system/permissions), takes the variant in -m provider/model#level, needs run --standalone (without it the background service, which never sees OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT, answers and the requested agent is "not found") and has no --pure equivalent. Its warm path is off until opencode serve's v2 HTTP API is driven, so every action pays the private-server boot (~3 s measured on 2.0.31) and the run's JSON stream carries no usage events in our tests (the report shows tokens as —). In 1.x, models matching agent.opencode.no_thinking (default deepseek, haiku, minimax-m3) run with thinking off: deepseek-v4.1-flash spent 300-1400 reasoning tokens (4-15 s) on small functions; it costs some quality, {} turns it back on. It is per model because glm/cat-jam reject the parameter. 2.x has no --variant flag and defaults to the model's max variant: measured on this account, deepseek-v4.1-flash spent 7.5M reasoning tokens on max against 7.5k on low — minutes per request and the 429s. A model in no_thinking now gets -m model#low and options = { thinking = { type = "disabled" } } unless an effort was chosen; effort in <leader>am still wins. opencode stats --models --cost shows the variant, reasoning and cost per model (and the console shows the 429s). The shortlist (deepseek-v4.1-flash, minimax-m3, claude-haiku-5-5, glm-5.3-flash, gpt-6-luna) was picked by benchmarking all opencode-go models on four graded tasks (two implement checked with go test, a review with a seeded bug, a review of clean code that must answer OK): 4/4, 1.4-3 s warm, ~$0.0002 per action. Haiku keeps requests 30 days; Gemini and Muse Spark train on prompts (left out). DeepSeek costs double at Peak (01-04 and 06-10 UTC, Mon-Fri). Only one run per task, so differences under ~0.5 s are noise. agent.opencode.attach points at a server of your own instead (--server on 2.x), but opencode run --attach printed no text in v1.19. agent.opencode.agent uses an agent of your own. If opencode does not find the requested agent it warns and falls back to the default one, which DOES have tools: here that is treated as an error.
  • Each opencode session stays in its list: titled nvim <action> in the cold path, nvim agent on the private server.

Local backends and models (automatic setup)

Besides Claude Code and OpenCode, the agent talks to local models (Ollama on CPU or CUDA, LM Studio, llama.cpp, vLLM...) and to any other agent CLI (Hermes Agent, Gemini CLI, Codex, Aider). A setup detects and connects them.

Quick start

  1. <leader>am → Setup (or scripts/agent-setup.sh from the terminal). It detects in parallel, without touching anything: the installed CLIs, Ollama and OpenAI-compatible servers (also the Windows one if you are on WSL: it tries 127.0.0.1, the nameserver and the gateway), their models, whether what is loaded runs on GPU or CPU, the VRAM/RAM, and it remembers the server it found.
  2. Use detected models applies the recommendation with what you already have. If there are no models, it offers to Download a couple according to your memory (VRAM, or half the RAM without a GPU): qwen2.5-coder 32b/14b/7b/3b/1.5b. They are suggestions, you decide.
  3. If Ollama is installed but stopped: Start Ollama server (ollama serve in the background). Starting and downloading are always requested explicitly: nothing is installed or downloaded by itself.

Terminal (scripts/agent-setup.sh; with no flags it only reports and exits with 0 if a backend is ready):

FlagDoes
--startstarts ollama serve if it does not respond
--pull MODELdownloads a model (repeatable)
--autoapplies the recommendation with the models already installed
--use backend/model[@light]sets backend and model (main, or light with @light)
--jsonthe full snapshot, for other tools

Example: scripts/agent-setup.sh --start --pull qwen2.5-coder:3b --auto.

The best of each tier: the main one stays on Claude and local models cover the light tier (docs, explain): light_backend (in <leader>am → Light model, or agent.light_backend) keeps those actions on a free local model without moving implement/fix/review. With no cloud CLI at all, the local one becomes the main one too.

Backends (all use curl, no dependencies):

  • ollama: agent.ollama = { host, model, light_model, models, num_ctx, max_ctx, keep_alive, temperature, think }. The host comes from host > OLLAMA_HOST > what the setup detected > 127.0.0.1:11434. num_ctx is automatic (power of two depending on the prompt, capped by max_ctx): Ollama SILENTLY truncates whatever does not fit in its window and would lose precisely the rules at the beginning. keep_alive leaves the model loaded (the first action is slow, the following ones are not); temperature is low (0.1) because the required format is respected better. Cost is always 0. The <think>…</think> blocks of qwen3/deepseek-r1 are discarded.
  • openai: agent.openai = { base_url, api_key_env, model, light_model, models, temperature } (LM Studio :1234, llama.cpp :8080, vLLM :8000, LiteLLM, OpenRouter...). The key is read from the api_key_env variable and travels through the process environment, not in the arguments (it does not show up in ps).
  • custom (Hermes Agent, Gemini CLI...): a declarative entry in agent.custom, without writing code:
-- local.lua
return { agent = { custom = {
  hermes = {
    label = "Hermes Agent",
    cmd = { "hermes", "chat", "-q", "{prompt}" },   -- {prompt} {system} {model}
    system_in_prompt = true,
    model_flag = { "-m", "{model}" },
    models = { "hermes3" },
  },
} } }

The flags in the Hermes Agent example are not verified (the CLI was not installed when this was written): check them with hermes --help. Hermes models do work today through Ollama (ollama pull hermes3).

Pitfalls

  • A small local model fails more often with the structured format (code block, L<n>|sev|msg): use it for docs/explain and, if you want more, move up to a 14b+ code model. Esc rejects malformed output as usual.
  • Without a GPU the model runs on CPU: fine for the light tier; the report says whether what is loaded is on GPU, GPU+CPU or CPU.
  • WSL2: to reach the Windows Ollama, set OLLAMA_HOST=0.0.0.0 there. In Docker with NVIDIA: docker run -d --gpus all -p 11434:11434 -v ollama:/root/.ollama ollama/ollama.
  • The setup (and the terminal report) stores the servers it finds in agent.json (endpoints).

Handoff to Herdr

<leader>ag (only inside Herdr): you type the instruction, pick an agent from herdr agent list (those in this folder first) and file:range + diagnostics are sent with herdr agent prompt. With unsaved changes it attaches the current code. Use it when the task no longer fits in a single shot.

What the other agents of the workspace are doing is in the Herdr panel (<leader>Wa), and this nvim can say what it is doing to Herdr (see herdr.md).

Hexagonal architecture levels (one menu per level)

<leader>a keeps the generic ones (implement, review, explain, ask, fix inline, fix block, docs, convert, refactor, hand off, model, history, clear, stop, last). Each architecture level has its own folder (lua/agent/<level>/), its own action registry and its own menu, with its context and, in each one, a local 0-token analysis. The actions of each level run through the same pipeline (inline diff, findings, history and cost), and in the history they appear under their own name (repo_port, service_review, ddd_fix...).

MenuLevelLLM actionsLocal analysis (0 tokens)Own context
<leader>aDDomain / DDD (agent/ddd/)implement i, review r, fix f, missing m, ask adiagnose d (layers, violations, aggregates), clear cddd (layers, naming, related pieces)
<leader>aPRepository (agent/repository/)port p, implement i, review rcheck c (port vs methods, entity vs table)contracts, schema
<leader>aUService (use cases) (agent/service/)implement i, review rdeps d (ports it uses, nonexistent calls, implementations, wiring)ports
<leader>aTTests (agent/tests/)write w, mock m, fix fgaps g (public functions in the folder that no test names)testing, subject, testfile
<leader>aLLenses (agent/lenses/)security s, performance p, concurrency c, errors e, api a— (each lens first discards what is not its concern)changes

The providers of each level (contracts, schema, ports, ddd) are silent if the code does not belong to that level, so implement/review/fix of the generic menu also receive them when applicable, at no cost when not. The shared code (searching with ripgrep, reading a type definition) lives in agent/discovery.lua, so levels do not depend on each other.

Repository (<leader>aP): persistence adapters

Implementing a repository (SQL, Postgres, MySQL, in-memory...) is long but mechanical, and the model needs what is not in the file you are looking at. The agent finds it by itself, without you mentioning files, and each piece arrives with its path:

  • Port: the interface it must satisfy. It is deduced from the name (PostgresOrderRepository, pgOrderRepository, OrderRepositoryImpl, CachedOrderRepository → OrderRepository) and from what is declared explicitly (implements X, class T(X), impl X for T, var _ X = (*T)(nil)); it is located with ripgrep in any folder structure and read with treesitter.
  • What is missing: it compares the port's methods with those the type already has (in Go, the functions with that receiver in the package) and also warns about the constructor. Only the missing ones are requested.
  • Entity and types the port names (Order, with its fields and constants) even if the empty struct does not mention them: without this the model invents the fields.
  • Sibling example: another implementation in the project (the one in the same folder is preferred) with the constructor and the equivalent method: this way it copies your structure, your error handling (fmt.Errorf("…: %w"), errors.Is(err, sql.ErrNoRows)) and your mapping.
  • Schema: the entity's real table with its later changes and the dialect (from the schema syntax and from go.mod/package.json/pyproject/Cargo.toml/pom.xml), the access library and the parameter style. It is agnostic: with the same prompt, a Postgres migration produces $1 and ON CONFLICT, and a MySQL one, ? and ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE.

<leader>aPp (repo port) uses the type under the cursor (or that of its receiver, if you are inside one of its methods) and writes the type, the constructor and ALL the missing methods, in the port's order. <leader>aPi (repo implement) writes a single method with that context; implement (i/aDi) receives it too if the code is an adapter, and <leader>aPr (repo review) reviews what is specific to persistence (columns that do not match, dialect parameters, concatenated SQL, unclosed rows, unwrapped errors, N+1) and <leader>aPc (repo check, 0 tokens) compares the port's methods with those of the type and the entity's fields with the columns of its table. Silent if it does not apply: a service, a handler or any other function does not pay even for the search (measured: 0 characters of extra context).

Tested with Claude on an empty PostgresOrderRepository with a 4-method port: it returned the constructor and the 4 methods with the real columns from the migration, $1…, the error style of the sibling implementation and no invented fields (what it assumed, such as order.ErrNotFound, it states in note:), for ~$0.0013 with Haiku. Limits: the port is found by name or explicit declaration (not by type inference); the sibling example is the first reasonable one it finds; the schema is only read from *.sql and *.prisma (not from ORM models or the live database).

Service (<leader>aU): application services / use cases

A service can only call the methods that its ports declare. The ports provider finds the interfaces the service receives (fields, constructor parameters, method parameters, in any language) and reads them with their real signatures, each with its path and the instruction to call only those methods; with examples it adds another service of the project as a style example.

  • <leader>aUi service implement: orchestrates the domain and calls ONLY the ports in <ports> with their exact names and signatures; business rules are called on the entity (not reimplemented); it propagates the context and translates the ports' errors.
  • <leader>aUr service review: calls to port methods that do not exist or with wrong arguments, business rules in the service, leaking infrastructure details, swallowed or untranslated errors, non-atomic steps or steps ordered in a risky way (effects before saving), N+1.
  • <leader>aUd service deps (0 tokens): for each port, which methods it declares, which ones this file calls and which ones the port does NOT declare (it would not compile), which implementations it has and whether any is wired (used outside its file); it lists what needs attention.

Tested with Claude: on a service that called orders.FindByID (the port declares ByID), deps detected it without an LLM and service review flagged it against the real port; service implement used only ByID, Save and Mailer.Send and called the entity's o.Confirm() instead of repeating the rule (what it assumed, such as a method the entity does not have, it stated in note:).

Findings that stay (and how to fix them)

The findings of review and ddd review, and the violations of ddd diagnose, are diagnostics pending attention: they are saved (stdpath("state")/agent-findings.json) and come back when you open the file in another session, even if you do not deal with them today.

  • Each one is saved with the text of the flagged line. If the code has moved since then (you edited above, another branch...), it is relocated by that text (up to ±40 lines); if the line no longer exists, it is dropped with a warning (Agent: 1 saved finding no longer matches the code and was dropped). Saving the file updates the positions.
  • Only the agent's are saved: LSP ones are not touched. If there is no findings file, opening a file costs nothing.
  • Clearing them (from memory and from disk; review and ddd alike): <leader>ac clear discards the one on the cursor line (in visual mode, those in the selection) → one at a time; <leader>aW clear file, all those in this file; :AgentClear [file|all] (with a range: :'<,'>AgentClear), all = all files. <leader>aDc ddd clear, the DDD diagnostic of the whole project. <leader>ax stop does not delete them: it only cancels what is running and discards pending proposals.
  • Fixing them: <leader>af (fix inline, the one on the line), <leader>aF (fix block, the whole function) and <leader>aDf (ddd fix) read the findings that fall in the function or selection, together with the LSP diagnostics, and propose the minimal change (in ddd fix, respecting the layers in <ddd>; if the fix needs a port, type or file that does not exist, it says so in note: and does not invent its content). When you accept the proposal, the findings in that range are considered resolved and also disappear from what is saved. review and ddd review do not read earlier findings: a new review must not start from what the previous one said.
  • Flow: <leader>aDr (review) → tomorrow, another session: the diagnostics are still there → <leader>aDf on the function → ⏎.

DDD (<leader>aD)

A feature separate from the normal agent, with its menu, its action registry (lua/agent/ddd/) and its context. Same pipeline and same experience (inline diff, findings as diagnostics, window, history and cost, where they are distinguished as ddd_implement, ddd_review...), but the actions follow the layers and naming of YOUR project.

KeyActionCosts
<leader>aDddiagnose: static analysis of the whole project0 tokens
<leader>aDiimplement: writes the code in the layer where it belongs, with your names and the language's idiomLLM
<leader>aDrreview: DDD and SOLID violations as diagnostics (without repeating those the local analysis already detected)LLM
<leader>aDffix: fixes what ddd review and the diagnostic left pending (minimal change, respecting the layers)LLM
<leader>aDmmissing: what is missing around what you see (ports, implementations, use cases, DTOs, wiring, errors, tests), with file names following your conventionLLM
<leader>aDaask: architecture question with the real layers and piecesLLM
<leader>aDcclear: discards the violations of the whole-project diagnostic (also from what is saved)0 tokens

What the local analysis does (free, no LLM): it walks the project once (asynchronously, in batches; it only reads files inside layer directories and that are not tests; cached by mtime) and deduces:

  • Layers by directory name (domain, application, infrastructure, interface and synonyms: usecases, adapters, persistence, api, handlers...; adjustable in settings.agent.ddd.layers). The dependency rule points inwards: domain ← application ← infrastructure / interface. Composition roots (main, cmd/, container, di...) may wire everything.
  • Violations (◈ diagnostics in the code + quickfix list + panel): outward dependencies (with the exact import), frameworks in domain/application (database/sql, net/http, gorm, @nestjs, sqlalchemy, org.springframework... per language: Go, TS/JS, Python, Java/Kotlin/Scala, Rust, C#, PHP, Dart) and badly named files (PaymentService in pricing.go). Only the project's own imports are assigned to layers (go.mod module, relative ones in TS/Python, crate:: in Rust): database/sql is a framework, not "the database layer".
  • Naming: file style (snake_case, kebab-case, order.entity.ts, PascalCase) and most used roles (repository, service, handler...), deduced from what already exists; suggestions for missing files respect it.
  • Aggregates, gaps and connections: for each domain entity (a type with an ID field) it looks for its repository port, its implementation, whether it is wired (its name is used in some other file, via rg) and its use cases; it lists what is missing.

What the model receives (<ddd> section, max. 1800 characters, only what is relevant to that target): its layer and what it may depend on, project layers, naming convention with examples, neighbouring files, the pieces surrounding the entities it touches (Order: port OrderRepository (...) · impl PostgresOrderRepository (...) wired ✓ · use cases PlaceOrder), the violations in that file and a line with the idiom of the current language (constructors, errors, injection). Plus the DDD rules (~600 characters).

Measured on a Go project with one complete entity (port + implementation + use case) and another without a port: implement of Order.Confirm() used Pending/Confirmed (the constants of the Status type travel with it) and respected the layer; review found the *sql.DB and the concrete repository stuffed into the entity, plus primitives without invariant; missing listed the vertical slice of Customer with paths in the project's style. Each action, under $0.001 with Haiku. On a repo with 7500 code files, the first analysis takes ~0.9 s (252 layer files) and the next one 35 ms.

Limits: layers are recognised by directory name (if yours are named differently, adjust layers); "entity" is a domain type with an ID field; "wired" means "its name appears in another file", not a dependency-graph analysis; imports are read with regex and types with treesitter, so it is not a compiler. Languages without a treesitter parser only contribute imports.

History and spending (<leader>ah)

Every action is stored in stdpath("state")/agent-history.jsonl (append-only): when, action, model, target, your instruction, the system prompt, the exact message sent, the response, tokens (with cache read/write), cost, time and what you did with the result (accepted, rejected, discarded, N findings...). This way you can see transparently what was asked and what was paid.

It opens on this project's runs (the git root the agent works from, so a run made from a subfolder belongs to the repo): settings.agent.history.scope = "all" (or <leader>Sp → Agent → History scope) shows every project. A project with no runs yet says so instead of showing the others.

  • <leader>ah opens a selector with one row per run (date, outcome, action, model, cost, target); the title adds up the total spent. The preview shows the full detail rendered as markdown, including the code sent (the target and the code around it, highlighted in its language); ⏎ opens it large and ⌫ (BS) comes back to the list with the same filters, search text and run selected.
  • ⌃F filters the history by agent (claude, opencode, ollama…), provider (who serves the model: anthropic, openai, local…), model, action, outcome or project (by folder; every project is offered, also while the history is scoped to this one): pick the dimension, then the value (each with its runs and cost). Filters stack and show in the title with the runs and spend that match; ⌃X clears them (back to this project, or to everything with scope = "all").
  • ⌃S opens the spending report (⌫ comes back to the list), always over all projects: by period (today, 7 days, total), by action, by model, by day, cost per applied change, what was spent on proposals you did not apply, errors, cache hits, where the prompt goes (system, context per provider, target) and the 5 most expensive runs. It is the basis for deciding what to cut.
  • ⌃Y copies EVERYTHING about the run as markdown (the same text you see in the detail): outcome and event line (accepted at 09:38:27...), target, time, tokens, cost, your instruction, the response, the system prompt and the exact message sent. ⌃P copies only the prompt (system + message) to reproduce or tweak it. In the detail panel and in the report panel, Y copies the whole panel. Everything goes to the clipboard (+) and the unnamed register.
  • Privacy: the history includes your code (it goes inside the prompt). agent.history.store_text = false stores only metrics; enabled = false turns it off. It compacts itself to the max_entries (1000) most recent.

Refine: change a proposal without starting over

A proposal is on screen and almost right? Tab (settings.agent.keys.refine) opens the instruction field again ("how should it change?") and replaces the proposal with a new one that starts from the previous code: the model sees the original target, the proposal and only what you ask to change. The old proposal never touched your buffer; the history links the new run to it. Cost: about one ordinary run per round (your instruction is ~30 tokens, the previous proposal 100-300), instead of rejecting and describing everything again.

The same key on an answer (explain / ask window) asks the next question about the same code with the earlier questions and answers as the conversation (the oldest turns are dropped past ~4k characters). The answer window never takes focus, so while it is on screen the key is also bound on the code buffer you are in (it goes away with the window; a proposal waiting in that buffer gets the key back). Pressing it needs no <leader>al first.

After accepting: verification

Accepting a proposal is no longer the end. agent/verify listens to the replace presenter's events and, for free:

  1. remembers the buffer's LSP and linter diagnostics before the code is written (the agent's own findings do not count: accepting resolves those);
  2. waits until the language server settles (verify.settle_ms, never more than verify.max_wait_ms) and compares: a diagnostic is identified by what it says (source, code, message), not by its line, so the same old warning one line lower is not new;
  3. nothing new → one quiet line ✓ verified · no new problems; something new → a question fix regression 1/2: it names the problems, the attempt, the model and the estimate. Yes launches a fix regression proposal over the function that holds them (same experience as any proposal, and verified again if you accept it); verify.max_retries (2) bounds the chain, verify.auto_retry = true skips the question (still bounded and still behind the spend guard);
  4. a build check (verify.command = "auto": go build ./..., cargo check, py_compile, dart analyze of the saved file in machine format (only its errors count, never warnings); or your own per language) runs when you save the accepted code, because a compiler reads the disk. Only the errors on or next to that code are blamed on it; the rest of the project's errors are counted, not guessed at.

Tokens: 0 unless you answer yes; then about 1.8 runs per attempt (the original prompt plus the errors). docs is excluded (verify = false: only comments change).

Several proposals at once

fix all, fix from output and multi-file plans share one engine (agent/batch):

  • One confirmation with the total (3 proposals · claude/sonnet · ~2.3k tokens · ~$0.0012, the files and how many problems each holds). The budget guard is not asked again per piece, but a hard cap still stops the rest.
  • The pieces run one by one; the statusline says ⠋ agent fix all 2/3. If you cancel (<leader>ax) or the budget blocks one, the rest does not go on.
  • Each piece is anchored (an extmark), so accepting something above it while it runs does not misplace it.
  • The proposals wait in a queue: <leader>aA] / <leader>aA[ jump between them across files, <leader>aAl lists them in the quickfix, <leader>aAy accepts them all after one question (each is its own u). Each proposal's hint ends with 2/4 · <leader>aA] next.

Fix all (<leader>aAa) groups the file's diagnostics by the function (else the type) that contains them: three findings in one function are one request. Sources and severity: settings.agent.fixall (agent findings, DDD violations, LSP/linters; warn and up by default). Tokens: about one fix block run per affected function (5 findings in 3 functions ≈ 3 runs).

Fix from output (<leader>aAo): the errors come from the last terminal buffer, else the quickfix list; <leader>aAO asks for a command (the project's build by default), runs it and uses what it prints. They are parsed by Neovim's own errorformat (go, tsc, cargo, python, javac, flutter analyze, dart analyze (human and --format=machine) and the generic file:line:col: message), 0 tokens; each piece sends only the errors of its function (<build_errors>), not the whole output. At most settings.agent.errors.max_errors (20) errors are read.

Multi-file plans

<leader>aAp on a type or function, then what should change ("add a Done flag to notes, through every layer"):

  1. Free: where it reaches. Language-server references (completed with git grep when the server never leaves the starting file), collapsed to the declarations that hold the symbol (function or type, with its first line as the signature), capped by settings.agent.plan (max_files, max_sites).
  2. One request: the instruction and only those signatures (never bodies; ~40 tokens per site) go to the model, which answers ordered steps n|path|symbol|what. Steps that name a file or symbol that is not in the list are dropped (the model inventing them).
  3. You: the plan panel (☑ 1. path · Symbol — what). Space unchecks a step, a all/none, Enter runs the checked ones, q cancels. Its first line keeps the estimate up to date as you toggle.
  4. One request per step, in order: each is a proposal for its declaration, with the overall plan (this step marked) and what the steps before it proposed as context, so step 3 uses the field that step 1 adds. They land in the queue above and are verified as you accept.

A snapshot (git stash create, nothing in your work tree changes) is taken first: git diff <ref> shows everything the plan changed and git checkout <ref> -- <file> restores a file. Renames are better done with LSP rename (deterministic, 0 tokens); the plan is for semantic changes. Tokens: plan ≈ 1-2.5k in, 300 out; each step ≈ 1 run; a 5-step plan is about 6 runs in total.

Spend guard

Before every request (actions, batches, the staged review, plans) the budget guard (agent/guards/budget.lua, pure core in agent/budget.lua) checks the size of what is about to be sent (characters ÷ 3.5 ≈ tokens) against settings.agent.budget and what the history says you already spent. The price of a token is learned from your own history with that model (no price table). Above confirm_tokens / confirm_usd, or near a cap, it asks (modal with the reasons); a used-up day_usd / session_usd blocks. Every one-shot call (commit draft, staged review, plan) is recorded in the history too, so the spend is complete. The usage report (<leader>ah → ⌃S) gains a Budget line and Suggestions: actions you accept rarely (and what they cost), the share of the spend that went to proposals nobody applied, and how often the verifier had to retry.

Review before committing

<leader>Gv (or ⌃R in the commit panel) reviews what is staged:

  1. Free checks on the added lines: secrets (AWS/GitHub/Slack/OpenAI-style keys, private keys, password = "…" literals), merge-conflict markers, debugger/print leftovers per language (not in tests) and sensitive files (settings.git.commit.sensitive).
  2. If there is real code to read (not only lockfiles, generated files, deletions or binaries) one request goes out with the staged diff numbered by its lines in the new file (+L13 added, L12 context), within git.commit.review_max_chars. A finding must point at one of those lines or it is dropped as invented.
  3. Findings land as diagnostics on their lines (open files at once; the others when you open them: they persist like any finding) with source staged review, and as a quickfix list. The panel's agent line says review: 2 findings (quickfix).

Tokens: a typical commit (40-200 changed lines) is ~1.5-6k in and 100-600 out; a docs-only or lockfile-only commit costs nothing.

Context for refactors and explanations

Two providers that are opt-in per action (they cost tokens): callers (refactor, the api lens): who calls the target, from the LSP references, one short line each (path:line code), at most context.callers (6) and two per file, so a refactor sees how the function is really used before it changes a signature (~60 tokens each). gitlog (explain, review, refactor): the last context.gitlog (3) commits that touched those lines (git log -L), one line each (~40 tokens): the "why is it like this" and "this was fixed before".

Project conventions: .agent.md

An .agent.md at the root (or further up) is added to ALL prompts: style, preferred libraries, what to avoid. Max. 1500 useful characters. Example: Idiomatic Go; errors with %w; no logs in pure functions; tests with testify.

Next edit (on demand)

Not autocomplete and not ghost text: one key press (<leader>an) asks the light model (a small local one through light_backend is enough) for the edit you are about to make. What it sees: the lines around the cursor (settings.agent.next_edit.window, 30 each side) and <edits>, your latest changes in this file as -/+ hunks, oldest first.

  • The changes are tracked by agent/edits.lua: a baseline snapshot per file buffer, compared with the buffer when an insert ends or a normal-mode change settles (one vim.diff; no model, no LSP, files over next_edit.max_lines are not followed). It keeps the last keep hunks and sends at most max_chars of them (the newest win).
  • With nothing changed in the file it asks nothing (0 tokens, and says why). If nothing in the window clearly follows from your changes the model returns the window unchanged and you get "no changes".
  • It is a normal replace proposal: inline diff, ⏎/⎋, verification after accepting, history. Not cached (a proposal on live code).
  • Why on demand: a streaming ghost-text subscription spends tokens on every pause; this spends them only when you ask.

Answer cache (repeated questions are free)

A request is identified by everything the model would receive: system prompt, file, context sections, target, task, your instruction, plus backend, model and effort (agent/cache/, pure logic in cache/core.lua). If the same request was answered before, the stored answer comes back with 0 tokens, before the budget guard (nothing to ask about), and goes through the same presenter (the status line says cached · 0 tok · saved $…). A hit is not a run: it never appears in the history (<leader>ah) or the spend report, since no model was called. Any change to the function, to a type or diagnostic it reads, or to your instruction changes the hash, so a stale answer cannot come back.

  • Kept by default: text and findings outputs (explain, ask, review, lenses). A replace proposal is never cached: asking again on live code should give a fresh one. An action can opt in or out with cache = true|false.
  • Stored in stdpath("state")/agent-cache.json; the least recently used go past max_entries, and answers expire after ttl_days.
  • <leader>aAk shows what the cache holds and has saved you (answers kept, times it answered, tokens and money saved: counters the cache keeps itself) and has a Clear button. settings.agent.cache.enabled = false turns it off.
  • Tests must never share the real cache (a hit hides the call they count): K.fake() points it at a temp file.

Project documents (<leader>aS): AGENTS.md, STACK.md, DESIGN.md, SPEC.md

The documents every coding agent (Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, opencode, this one) reads before it changes code, written at the repository's root in the same sections in every project, so an agent always knows where to look. Lowercase keys use presets or the project's own facts; uppercase ones take what you describe in your words and the model writes the document.

KeyDocumentHow
bbthe projectbuild with agent, everything at once: reads the four documents (says which one is missing and its key) and builds the project in its own tab: one plan request, then one small request per file (only the documents it needs, the plan, the API of what is already written and the files it uses), then the manifest's own checks (Go: tidy, gofmt, build, vet, test) and up to 2 fix rounds for the files the errors name. One question before it starts (files, estimated tokens and cost, what it replaces, what the previous build left that this plan drops)
btthe foundationbuild one layer from STACK.md: manifest, layout, tooling, a minimal entry point that runs (below)
bsthe domainbuild one layer from SPEC.md: entities, rules, use cases and their tests, no interface (below)
bdthe designbuild one layer from DESIGN.md: theme, styles, components, screens, wired to the domain the project has (below)
mSPEC.mdmodules: what every app repeats (accounts, roles, lists, workspaces, notifications, audit log, uploads, import/export, offline, payments, subscriptions) merged into your SPEC.md, with edge cases, and questions where the answer is yours (below)
bfone featurebuild one requirement end to end: pick an FR of SPEC.md (○ no test yet, ● tested) or describe a new one, which becomes the next FR-n in SPEC.md (below)
ra recipeprefab task for your stack (a screen with its state, an endpoint, an entity with its repository, a form…): asks its values, then builds it on the project (below)
vcoverage0 tokens: which FR-n have a test, what STACK.md names that the manifest lacks, DESIGN.md tokens no code uses, folders no document mentions (below)
PrecordsPROGRESS.md and the decisions (ADRs) the builds wrote, with a preview
Bthe projectcheck again: the checks and fix rounds on the files the last build wrote, as they are now (after you edit them, or after a restart; its list is kept per project in the state dir)
/the libraryevery preset in one search (stacks, designs, their add-ons, principles) with its preview: ⏎ uses it (a stack or design opens its customize step; an add-on is merged into the STACK.md/DESIGN.md there; a principle is added), ^e embeds its text under your cursor, ^y copies it, ^o opens the preset file (to read it or make your own from it)
oall fourwhich are there (●) and which are missing; Enter opens one or starts it
aAGENTS.mdthe model reads the project (file tree, languages, manifests, Makefile targets, package.json scripts, README, the other documents) and writes how to work in it: real commands, layout, conventions, workflow, boundaries, definition of done. With one there it updates it, keeping what is still true
AAGENTS.mdthe same sections without a model (0 tokens): commands detected from the Makefile and manifests, top folders, general rules, TODO: lines for what only you know
cCLAUDE.mdClaude Code reads CLAUDE.md, not AGENTS.md: creates one that imports it (@AGENTS.md), or adds that line to yours
tSTACK.mdpreset: kind of project → stack (with a preview) → customize → principles → sections. Kinds: backend (Go stdlib / chi / Fiber / Huma / gRPC / workers, Bun + Hono / Elysia, Hono, NestJS, FastAPI, Django, Flask, Rails API, Axum, tonic, Spring, Ktor), web (Next.js, React + Vite, Vite vanilla, Solid, Preact, Alpine.js, Lit, SvelteKit, Astro, Nuxt), full stack (Go + templ + htmx, Bun full stack, Leptos, Next.js + Supabase, T3, Django + htmx, Laravel), mobile (Flutter with Riverpod, Bloc, Supabase, Firebase, offline-first, desktop or no package; Expo, SwiftUI, Compose), self-hosted (Go + SQLite + Litestream, PocketBase, Docker Compose stack, Bun + SQLite), monorepo, CLI / desktop / TUI (Bubble Tea, Wish over SSH, Ratatui, cobra, clap, Tauri)
TSTACK.mddescribed ("Go API for invoices, Postgres, self-hosted"), then customize (the add-ons that fit the words you used) and principles; the model writes it knowing the add-ons, and they are merged after
kSTACK.mdcustomize the one there: add deploy, CI, observability… (those already in it come checked)
pSTACK.mdchange the principles it carries: the section is rebuilt from the presets you check
dDESIGN.mdkind → design (with a preview) → customize. Design systems (Material 3, M3 Expressive, Cupertino, Ant Design, Fluent 2, Carbon, Primer, shadcn/ui, Notion, Linear), styles (neo-brutalism, Swiss, retro pixel, glass, mono minimal) and terminal / TUI (ASCII art, Braille, brutal, classic, retro CRT, modern Charm-style)
DDESIGN.mddescribed ("like Linear but warmer", "an ASCII art TUI with amber phosphor"), then customize; a design you name is sent as the base, a terminal description gets the terminal add-ons
wDESIGN.mdscreens: one request draws a wireframe of every main screen SPEC.md names (and the states that change the layout: empty, error, too small) in DESIGN.md's own style, character by character at the minimum size, with what each region uses and how it adapts; written as its ## Screens section (replaced when there is one, the old file to the trash). Needs SPEC.md and DESIGN.md. Models copy a picture far more faithfully than prose, so the build lays screens out as drawn
sSPEC.mdwrite what you are building in any shape; it comes back as a spec: requirements FR-n with Given/When/Then acceptance criteria, non-functional NFR-n, data, interfaces, edge cases, milestones, and every gap as an open question (never invented). With a SPEC.md there, the page starts from it: edit freely and it comes back tidy, ids kept
jthe documentsreconcile: contradictions and redundancies between the documents and the code, with a preview of every fix (0 tokens). Duplicate Stack rows (the same layer twice, one-of group), duplicate lines or headings, a package manager the lockfile contradicts, and the Read first links AGENTS.md misses; report-only: tools STACK names that the manifest lacks and folders no document mentions. Space picks, Enter previews, y applies
MMakefileretroactive, foundation-lite (one light-tier call, no plan, no STACK.md/SPEC.md, no checks): reads the repo as it is — languages, manifests, commands, tree, the Makefile there if any — and writes one Makefile with only the commands that input backs up (help, install/setup, build, test, lint, fmt, run, check, clean; .PHONY, ## per target, tabs). With one there it keeps its targets and adds the missing standard ones. The old file goes to the trash; <leader>Rr lists the new targets at once

Customize is one list in groups, merged into STACK.md without a model (rows replace the row with the same first word, rules and commands are added once, so applying twice changes nothing). It opens with the best picks for that stack already checked (an add-on's <!-- default: … -->: in a one-of group the most specific match wins, the stack itself over its language over its category), so Enter alone gives a complete setup; change or uncheck what you want. Round marks (●/○) are one-of groups, squares (■/□) take any number. Only the add-ons that fit the stack are listed (its main language is read from the Stack table, so a Go API is never offered TypeScript tools).

The customize step is a full-screen marketplace too (the same keys): the choices by group on the left (●/○ one-of groups, ■/□ any number), the add-on under the cursor on the right (what it is and what it writes: its Stack rows, conventions, commands, rules), and under both what the choice sets (sets the rows Database, Auth…). p shows exactly the text the document would gain, Space picks (in a one-of group the others go off), ⏎ goes on, Esc goes back, / filters, Tab folds a group, ? lists the keys. <leader>aSk opens it on an existing STACK.md and <leader>aSK on an existing DESIGN.md.

Sections (the last step of t/T/d/D): which ## to keep — all checked by default, uncheck to leave a section out (a small API needs no Screens, a library needs no Commands). The title and the principles (when you chose them) always stay.

Esc goes back one step (Sections → Principles → Customize → stack → kind of project; in T, Customize → the page with what you wrote), keeping what you chose; Enter moves on. Only the first step's Esc closes.

DESIGN.md customize works the same way (best picks checked, Esc back), with its own groups:

Checked from the project's STACK.md: Go → Charm, Rust → Ratatui, Python → Textual, a Tailwind stack → Tailwind

A terminal design is a real DESIGN.md for a TUI: colors as ANSI 16 / 256 / truecolor roles with fallbacks, typography as monospace cells and glyph sets, spacing in cells, borders as box drawing, motion as frames.

The build (b…) is an orchestrator you can watch, steer, leave and come back to. It builds the whole project (bb) or one layer from the document that describes it, with the same letter that document has in this menu:

KeyLayerReads (required first)BuildsSkips
btfoundationSTACK.md, AGENTS.mdmanifest, folder layout, formatter/linter/test config, Makefile or scripts, a minimal entry point that builds and runs, one smoke test, README (15 files at most)domain logic, screens; the wiring check and the use phase
bsdomainSTACK.md, SPEC.md, AGENTS.mdentities, value objects, rules, errors, use cases and ports where STACK.md's layout puts them, each with tests that prove its FR-n; in-memory adapters only when the tests need themthe interface and the entry point; the wiring check (nothing calls the domain yet, by design) and the use phase
bddesignSTACK.md, DESIGN.md, SPEC.md (when there), AGENTS.mdtheme or tokens, styles, components and screens (as ## Screens draws them), wired into the entry point; it calls the domain the project has, sample data when there is nonenothing: the wiring check, the use phase (with SPEC.md) and the look all run

A layer is built on top of what the project already has: the plan request gets the existing files as <project> (their paths, the manifests whole, the API the code declares), every file request sees that API and the existing files it uses, and an existing file the plan has to change (the domain adds a dependency to go.mod, the design makes main.go open the screens) is sent as it is and comes back whole. The review shows those as replaced. Usual order: bt → bs → bd, each reviewed before anything is written; any of them alone works too (a domain on an empty folder plans its own manifest). Each layer keeps its own saved run and its own list of the files it created: a replan of the domain only drops the domain's old files, X sends only the files that layer created to the trash (the ones it only changed stay), and the foundation's run is never touched by the domain's. The tab's top line and the statusline name the layer (domain · from SPEC.md, ⠹ build domain 3/9).

One feature at a time (bf): after the first build, most work is a new feature. The picker lists the requirements of SPEC.md with whether a test proves them yet (the preview opens SPEC.md at that requirement), and ＋ new first:

  • An existing one opens the feature build with that requirement's text: its line to the next requirement, plus every other line of SPEC.md that names it (its edge cases, its open questions).
  • A new one: you describe it on the draft page in your words; one request (main tier, SPEC.md as context) turns it into a requirement (title, "The system must…", 2-6 Given/When/Then criteria, edge cases, the questions only you can answer). A confirmation shows it; Add and build writes it into SPEC.md in the document's own style (heading ### FR-n: or bullet - **FR-n**, the next free id), its edge cases under ## Edge cases and its questions under ## Open questions (numbered on from the last Qn); the old SPEC.md goes to the trash.

The feature build plans the smallest set of files that makes the requirement work for the user: the domain it needs, the interface that reaches it (when there is one), the tests of its acceptance criteria, following the patterns the code already uses (same folders, naming, state management, error handling), on top of the project as it is (<project>, like the layers). Existing files it changes come back whole and keep what the rest uses. The wiring check and the use phase run, with scenarios from that requirement alone. Features are additive: one feature's replan or clean never touches another's files. One feature run is kept at a time; picking another requirement starts afresh.

SPEC.md modules (m, 0 tokens): your SPEC.md stays yours (you write the core of your business); the modules add the well-known parts you would otherwise forget. It opens as a small marketplace on the whole screen: the modules by group on the left (□ not picked, ■ picked, + comes with what you picked, ✓ already in SPEC.md), the one under the cursor on the right (what it is, its requirements with their criteria, edge cases, and the questions that are yours), and under both what SPEC.md would get from your choice (the modules, the FR range, the counts, what came in because another needs it). Nothing is written until you add, and the confirmation sums it up first (the old SPEC.md goes to the trash; cancelling it brings you back to the marketplace as you left it).

  • List: j/k move, Space picks, ⏎ reads and customizes, p shows what SPEC.md gets, / filters by name, description, key or requirement title, {/} jump between groups, Tab folds a group (on a folded title it unfolds it), h hides what SPEC.md already has, t / d open the STACK.md add-ons / DESIGN.md patterns it goes with (below), A adds, ? lists every key, q closes. ★ marks a module your SPEC.md already talks about without having it (a word of its name or one of its requirement titles appears in the text: 0 tokens), and its pane says which word.
  • Read and customize (⏎): j/k scroll, Space keeps or leaves out the requirement under the cursor (its edge cases and questions go with it; leaving every one out drops the module), Esc/h back to the list.
  • What SPEC.md gets (p): exactly the text that would be added, as it would read, with what you left out missing; Esc goes back.
GroupModules
Every appaccounts and sign-in (sign up, sign in, reset, verification, lockout) · roles and permissions (ownership, changing roles) · lists (pagination, empty states, search and filters, delete with undo and a trash) · profile and preferences · first run and onboarding (empty states) · loading, errors and feedback (waiting, failures, undo) · privacy and personal data (consent, export, deletion) · languages, time zones and currencies · reviews and ratings · favorites and wishlists · multi-step flows · bulk actions
Teamsworkspaces and invitations (data kept apart, members, leaving) · notifications (in-app, email/push, preferences, unsubscribe) · comments and mentions · sharing and links (people, links, expiry) · activity feed · chat and direct messages · tasks and boards · calendar and booking · contacts and CRM · support tickets · newsletter
Dataaudit log · file uploads · import and export · offline and sync (queued changes, conflicts) · version history (compare, restore) · dashboards and reports · tags and categories · background and scheduled jobs (retries, never twice) · public API and webhooks (keys, signed events, limits) · forms and surveys · content publishing · media gallery · maps and location
Money and commercepayments (checkout, receipts, refunds, idempotency, webhooks) · subscriptions (plans, trials, renewals, grace period, upgrades, cancelling) · cart and checkout (orders, stock) · invoices and receipts (numbering, credit notes) · coupons and promotions · product catalog · inventory · seats and billing
Securitytwo-step sign-in and sessions (second factor, devices, recovery) · limits and abuse protection (rate limits, guessing, reports, blocking)
Platformback office (finding records, acting with care) · feature flags and settings (gradual release) · single sign-on
Qualityaccessibility · performance · reliability and backups · observability

How a module goes in:

  • Each requirement gets the next FR-n in SPEC.md's own style (heading or bullet) with its Given/When/Then criteria; its edge cases go under ## Edge cases and its questions under ## Open questions (numbered on from your last Qn), tagged with its id.
  • Questions, not invented rules: lockout limits, roles, taxes, grace periods… come as questions; the build's plan decides them explicitly until you answer.
  • A requirement you already wrote (same title, any case or punctuation) is kept as you wrote it, and the module's edge cases and questions for it are tagged with your id.
  • needs: pulls what a module builds on (subscriptions → payments → accounts, needs first) and the confirmation says so.
  • <!-- module: accounts --> marks where a module went in: applying it again adds nothing. Nothing is ever removed.
  • Then each new requirement is a ○ in bf (build it) and in the coverage (v).

Modules are plain files in agent/kit/presets/spec/<group>/ (## Requirements with ### Title, "The system must…" and criteria; - [Title] … edge cases and questions; <!-- needs: … -->). They are also in the library (/), where the search looks at their requirement titles too (refund finds payments).

Starter kits open the list of the marketplace: a product type as a set of modules (SaaS for teams, marketplace, online store, internal tool, blog, booking, community, support desk, API product, consumer mobile app, project tool, forms and surveys). Space on a kit picks all its modules (◧ = some picked), ⏎ goes to the first one, and its pane says who it is for, what it adds and the decisions to take first. They are files too: agent/kit/presets/spec-kits/<id>.md with <!-- modules: key key … -->.

Narrow terminals (under 100 columns): the list and the pane take turns on the whole screen: ⏎/l opens the pane, Esc/h gives the list back.

What a module goes with: every module (and kit) names the STACK.md add-on axes and the DESIGN.md screen patterns it pairs with (<!-- tech: auth email -->, <!-- design: forms auth-screens -->), shown in the pane under "Goes with": accounts → Auth, Database, Email · Forms, Auth screens. The names are the library's own (<leader>aSt / <leader>aSd customize steps), and a test checks every name exists. In the marketplace t opens the STACK.md add-ons over it (the groups that go with what you picked marked ★, the cursor on the first; with nothing picked it uses the module or kit under the cursor) and d the DESIGN.md add-ons with those screen patterns already checked; closing them puts you back in the marketplace with your picks. They need a STACK.md / DESIGN.md in the project. DESIGN.md has a new group, Screen patterns (forms, data tables, empty states, onboarding, settings, navigation shell, toasts, dialogs, command palette, search and filters, dashboards, auth screens, pricing and checkout, comments, file upload, notification center).

Recipes (r): prefab tasks, plain Markdown files in agent/kit/presets/recipes/<group>/ (add your own: the header of the presets plus one <!-- input: key | question --> line per value, and a body with ## Make, ## Rules, ## Tests using {{key}}, {{key_snake}}, {{key_camel}}, {{key_pascal}}, {{key_kebab}}). Only the recipes whose for: fits STACK.md are listed (go+server = both, go+!server = a Go project that is not a server).
