Picking one asks its values, fills it and opens the recipe build: the recipe says what to make, STACK.md and the existing code say where and how (its paths are only a hint when the project already does it differently). Recipes skip the wiring check and the use phase. They are also in the library (/).

GroupRecipes
flutterscreen with its state (Riverpod Notifier or Bloc/Cubit), route and widget test · data feature (model, repository interface + implementation, provider) · form with validation · list and detail
goHTTP endpoint (handler, service, route, httptest) · entity with its repository (port, in-memory + SQL adapter, contract tests) · CLI subcommand · Bubble Tea screen (teatest golden)
webUI component (states, tokens, accessibility) · page with its route and data · data table · validated form · settings page
general (typical features)CRUD resource · auth flow · search endpoint · background job · webhook receiver · file upload · transactional email · feature flag
typescript, python, rustAPI route / HTTP endpoint with validation, service and tests
generalmodule with its tests (any stack)

Coverage (v, 0 tokens): a report in a split, read from the documents and the code:

SectionHow it is checked
Requirementsevery FR-n of SPEC.md's Requirements; proved when a test file names it (FR-3, not FR-30) or a build plan's verifies does and that file exists. ⏎ on an untested one opens its feature build
STACK.md names the manifest lackspackage.json: the tools the build knows; pubspec.yaml, go.mod, Cargo.toml, pyproject.toml: the package names the Stack table writes in backticks
DESIGN.md tokens no code usesthe first column of the Color tables and every --css-variable, searched in every spelling (text-muted, text_muted, textMuted, TextMuted)
Folders no document mentionstop-level folders and those one level under lib/, src/, internal/, cmd/, app/, pkg/…: the layout drifted from STACK.md

⏎ opens the place a line is about, r reads again, q closes. Ask tests to name the requirement they prove (// FR-3: …): the builds' tests do.

Records (0 tokens, settings.agent.kit.build.adr / .progress):

  • Decisions (docs/adr/NNNN-title.md): when a review is accepted, the plan's decisions that no earlier ADR records are written as one ADR (Status, Context, Decision, Consequences: the files created and changed) and indexed in docs/adr/README.md. A build that decides nothing new writes nothing. Every later plan follows them: the decisions still in force go to the plan request as <decisions> (oldest first; the newest kept past ~4000 characters), and the plan lists only new choices. When a build truly must reverse one, the plan says so as a decision of its own (Supersedes ADR 0002: Bloc for state because …): its ADR's Status says supersedes ADR 0002, the index marks 0002 superseded by 0005, and 0002 is no longer sent to plans. An ADR is never edited. The feed's plan line says how many it followed (follows 3 ADRs).
  • PROGRESS.md at the root, rewritten whenever a run settles: Now (the last build, how it ended, how to resume it), Done (a log, one line per finished build, kept across rewrites), Open (files not written, failing checks, issues the use phase found, SPEC.md's open questions), Next (the requirements no test proves yet). Any agent reads it to go on where the last one stopped.

Both are linked from AGENTS.md's "Read first" the first time, and neither is ever read as code by a layer build.

What a build may claim, and what it may throw away (agent/kit/build/proof.lua, loss.lua, kit/decls.lua; all free, no model). Checks are compilers and unit tests: they say the files agree, not that SPEC.md is delivered. So the closing line says what is proven. A build of the whole project, a feature or a recipe ends ✓ built only when every requirement has a test that names it and nothing is open; otherwise ◐ built · 23 files · checks pass · 5/17 requirements have a test · core without one: FR-16 · 2 placeholders. A requirement is core when SPEC.md's Goals cite it (FR-1 to FR-15 ranges included) or its line says (core). A placeholder is code that says it is not real yet (TODO, UnimplementedError, todo!(), "placeholder", "coming soon"; an input's own placeholder= attribute, tests and generated files are not). A layer build (foundation, design, domain) reports the same numbers but keeps ✓, because one layer cannot meet most requirements. Also in the line: a requirement that had a test before the build and has none now, and the public declarations of the files the build rewrote or deleted that are gone. PROGRESS.md gets a Proof line under Now and every item under Open. settings.agent.kit.build.strict = true makes such a build end ✗ incomplete instead.

  • Deleting code asks. The files a replan drops are inventoried before anything goes to the trash (lines, public types, tests and which requirements they name, who still uses them). The review screen says so (⚠ deletes 2,591 lines and 9 public types), and when what goes holds a real declaration or 60+ lines, ⏎ opens a confirmation with the list first. <leader>Fu still brings them back.
  • A whole-project build sees the code that is there. <leader>aSbb over a project that earlier builds left code in used to plan from the documents and the ADRs' text only, and wrote the code a second time under other names (two ProjectRepository ports, two timeline_view.dart: 364 analyzer issues). Now the plan request carries the project's files and the API they declare, and is told to use exactly those names and to merge duplicates instead of adding a third.
  • A type is declared once. Every project gets a free check, no type is declared twice: a public type (class, interface, struct, trait, enum) in two source files of the same language fails it and goes to a diagnosis (Go: per package; tests, generated code and tool scaffolds do not count). Flutter/Dart projects also run flutter test (a project with a test/ folder), and Rust projects with tests/ run cargo test: "it analyzes" was all that was checked.
  • Plans name the risk and defer honestly. The plan lists risks (the hardest requirements, riskiest first; the real path through the first is built right after the manifest, before the layers that only present or store) and deferred (what this build cannot deliver, and exactly what is missing). The review shows ⚠ for a requirement the plan neither builds, wires nor defers, for a file that stands in for a core requirement, and for a plan that names no risk. These are warnings for you to read, not a reason to ask for a second plan. (Building the risky path first as a verified skeleton before the rest is not enforced: it is a rule in the plan prompt that the warnings check.)
PhaseWhat happensYour keys
readypre-flight: the four documents and their size, the model of each phase (plan · files · fixes · use, each on the main or light tier of <leader>am), fix rounds, what the plan request will cost⏎ plan · 1 2 3 4 tier of each phase · + - fix rounds
planone request writes the build's contract: how it works, the architecture and dependency direction, the key decisions (and the SPEC questions it had to assume), then every file in order with what it does, what it exports and promises (NewKeypad() — the highlight starts on =), what a test verifies (FR-n), the documents it needs and the files it uses, and the wiring: one row per requirement from what the user does to what runs to what they see and in which file (FR-2: presses + → ui.Update → ChooseOperator → view.go shows "12 +"), so a feature never stops at the domain. The contract goes to every file request (cached), so files written apart agree. Shown as plan.md in the code pane while it is made: first what the model read and its thinking steps (the CLI streams the thinking without its text on subscription accounts), then each section and file as it arrives. Long lines wrap: nothing scrolls sidewaysx stop
reviewthe plan with an estimate per file and in total (with and without the cache), what it replaces, leftovers of the last build. This is the confirmation: nothing is written before ⏎ (an Enter that arrives as the review appears, typed ahead or pressed twice, is ignored)⏎ build · x skip a file · 2 3 tiers · n new plan · p the plan request
writeone request per file; the code pane types each file out, the feed says what went in and what it costx stop · q hide (it goes on) · p the request of the file under the cursor
checkthe manifest's own tools plus free consistency checks (below), then the interface uses what the plan exports: a function the plan promised to other files that only its own file or a test calls (Calculator.Expression() implemented and tested, never drawn) fails the check and goes to a diagnosis like any error (0 tokens to find; values nobody uses, a style or a color token, are only context for the use phase)
usewhen every check passes, the app is used like its user would. One request (the use model) writes scenarios from SPEC.md (flows, Given/When/Then, edge cases, the state the user needs mid-flow) and DESIGN.md (its smallest size): steps and what each must show, the expectations from the documents, never from the code. A driver performs them: terminal UIs in a private tmux at the scenario's size, keys one at a time like a person types; command lines run once per step (output, errors, exit code); web apps (dev server, static page, the project's own server) and Electron in Playwright, read as a screen reader would plus the layout problems measured in the page (sideways scroll, controls out of the window, overlaps, targets under 24 px, cut text) and the JavaScript errors; Android over adb on the device or emulator you started (uiautomator hierarchy + screenshot). Every step is checked for free (texts that must and must not show, exit codes, crashes, actions with no visible effect); one verdict request reads the scenarios as the user lived them and names what to fix (blocker, major, minor). Fixes go like a diagnosis' files, the golden snapshots are written again, and the same scenarios are replayed: use_rounds fixes at most (2), the last play only reports. The frames and screenshots stay in the state dir (the feed says where). An app that cannot be driven here (no tmux, no Playwright, no device) skips to the look at 0 tokens; off with settings.agent.kit.build.use = false
lookthen the screens as they render (Go teatest golden files: exactly what the terminal shows, colors stripped) go to the model with DESIGN.md (its Screens wireframes first when it has them; never invented) in one request (cached system prompt, ~$0.006). What differs (not centered, cut off, a missing frame) is fixed in the code like a diagnosis' files, the golden files go to the trash, the checks write them again and they are looked at again: look_rounds looks at most (2: one fix, verified). r stays at 0 tokens; off with settings.agent.kit.build.look = false
fixrounds go on while they make progress (the failure changes), up to 8; they stop after max_fixes rounds in a row that change nothing (the ± keys), and a round that changed nothing escalates the next one to the main tier with more effort. Each round: first a diagnosis: one request sees the tool's output, the API (with each declaration's doc comment) and the files involved, and names the root cause and every file to change, including files the errors do not name (a test fails, the bug is in the code it tests). Then each of those files is fixed with the diagnosis and its own instruction, the code before the tests. A fix answers with edit blocks (<<<<<<< SEARCH the exact current lines ======= the new ones >>>>>>> REPLACE, applied here; UNCHANGED when the file is right): a few hundred output tokens instead of the whole file. A block that does not match the file, an answer much shorter than the file (a fragment taken for it), leftover block markers, prose or tool-call markup, or "unchanged" for a file the diagnosis said how to change are caught before anything is written and asked again once (after blocks that do not apply: for the whole file); still wrong, the file stays as it was. A fix that weakens a test (fewer assertions or waits, a new skip) is refused and the test stays as it was; a check whose tool does not run sends the manifest. A file the plan missed (an asset go:embed reads) can be created by the diagnosis ("new": true, a safe path, 2 at most). Every repair (the checks, each use or look fix) has its own rounds; after a use fix the diagnosis gets what the user met (<user>): a test that expects the opposite locks the bug in and its expectation changes, not the code back
done✓ or ✗, totals, every file with its lines and cost⏎ opens a file · r checks again · n new plan
  • The tab: on the left the phases as chips (done ones green, the current one with a spinner, the next ones faint), the progress bar, then the files (the one being written on a full-width band, like the agent's inline target) and the checks; models and documents only before it writes. The code (c shows/hides it) and the feed (f) on the right; with both hidden only the list advancing is left (settings.agent.kit.build.view sets which panes it opens with). The statusline of the tab is its own (a lualine extension): the phase as the mode chip in the color of how it stands (red failed, yellow stopped, green done), the keys of the moment (as many as fit, most useful first), then progress · money (and cache · time when the screen is wide).
  • Reading and moving: z puts the code pane alone on the screen with the cursor in it (the plan, a file, a request) and z again brings the panes back; l hides or shows the files, c the code, f the feed; Tab / Shift-Tab move the cursor between the panes; P shows the plan (plan.md, rendered) at any moment.
  • Start over: n plans again right away (the files of the old plan stay; the new review says which it replaces and which leftovers go to the trash). X cleans the build after a confirmation that lists them: every file it wrote goes to the trash (<leader>Fu brings them back), empty folders it made are removed, the saved run is forgotten; the four documents are never touched.
  • Nothing is lost: the run is saved after every step. Closing Neovim with a plan in review and running <leader>aSbb (or the layer's key) again brings the review back with the plan on screen; a run stopped mid-write continues from the first file not written.
  • Leave it running: q closes the tab, the build goes on; the statusline shows ⠹ build 7/25 · $0.031 from any buffer, a notice says how it ended, its key (<leader>aSbb, or the layer's) brings the view back exactly where it is.
  • Resilient: the run is saved after every step (state dir, per project). Closing Neovim mid-build and opening it again offers to continue from the first file not written; the spend so far is kept.
  • Transparent: money, tokens in/out, the share read from the cache and the time, live; per file its lines and cost; p shows the exact request (system prompt and message) a file was written from; every request is also in <leader>ah.
  • Clean answers: a model that wraps the file (a fence with a sentence around it, the prompt's<file>tags echoed, tool-call markup leaking from the CLI, "I'll make the file…" before the code) is cleaned before anything is written; when it is still not a file (Go withoutpackage`, JSON that does not parse, markup left) the request is asked again once with the reason. A broken fix never replaces a file that was a file. Each of these used to cost a full fix round.
  • Cheap: every file and fix request shares ONE system prompt (all four documents + the plan), so the backend caches it once and every later request reads it at a tenth of the price (measured with claude/haiku: ~20k of ~25k tokens per request from the cache); the message carries only the file's focus, the API of what exists and the files it uses.

Checks (never commands written by the model): Go go mod tidy · gofmt (fixes itself) · build · vet · test; Node/Bun package.json has STACK.md's tools (Vite, Vitest, TypeScript, Biome… named in the Stack table) · install (the package manager STACK.md names when there is no lockfile yet; pnpm's blocked build scripts are approved in pnpm-workspace.yaml from the names pnpm gives (onlyBuiltDependencies on pnpm 10, allowBuilds on 11+, setting the placeholders pnpm 12 scaffolds to true), 0 tokens: agent.kit.build.auto_build_scripts = false leaves it to the model; Playwright's browser is a missing tool: a small modal asks Install / Don't install (the command is shown). If you decline, an ADR in docs/adr/ records it (<!-- waived: playwright -->), the check is marked waived and the build goes on; later builds do not ask again (delete the ADR or supersede it to lift the waiver). agent.kit.build.install_browsers = false does not ask) · every id the code looks up exists (HTML vs getElementById, querySelector('#…'), *_ID constants) · every control the page declares is handled (a data-action/data-key/data-op name such as equals that no script handles: the button does nothing while every unit test passes) · every stylesheet and module is used · tsc --noEmit · the lint (Biome/Prettier fix format themselves), build and test scripts; Python uv sync · compileall · ruff · pytest when declared; Rust, Dart and Flutter their build/analyze. A Go module version the model invented (invalid version: unknown revision) is dropped with go mod edit -droprequire and go mod tidy writes the real one (0 tokens; go.mod is asked without a require block, and the plan never lists files tools generate: goldens, lockfiles, build output). Formatters (gofmt, Biome, Prettier) run again after every fix round. Missing golden files (snapshot tests run for the first time) are created by running those packages' tests with -update (no tokens; review testdata/*.golden), then the checks run again. A build is done only when every check passes; otherwise it ends failed with the fix rounds it tried.

Transient failures: a request that never reached the model (ECONNRESET, a refused socket, a 502/503/504) and failed within a minute is sent again twice, with the streamed answer reset. A 429 / inference_failed / [overloaded] (the provider's rate limit or an overloaded server) waits 10 s and 20 s before its two retries even after a long hold (OpenCode can hold one for minutes before cancelling: the model never saw it, so it is not the "already ran" case); a smaller prompt and the low variant reduce them. A request that already ran longer is not repeated (it would repeat the same wait): the build says so and Enter retries. A plan on a slow model is the long request (the feed shows its chars and 1200s max; after 60 s it says it is still waiting); files and fixes get 600 s instead of the 120 s of a one-shot action — a stronger main model or a smaller scope is the way out. The plan gets a brief of the documents (SPEC requirements/interfaces/edge cases, STACK stack/layout/conventions, AGENTS conventions; DESIGN whole), not the full text the file requests get, and it can run on its own backend/model (agent.kit.build.plan_backend/plan_model, e.g. claude/sonnet) while files and fixes keep the main one. A fix cut off leaves the file as it was and Enter re-runs the check (the diagnosis sees the failure again), not a blind rewrite of the file from the plan.

Measured fix (claude/haiku): ascii-calc's keypad opened on "7" so Enter typed a 7 and three UI tests failed; the diagnosis named keypad.go (not in the errors), one fix there, tests untouched, every check green: 2 requests, $0.008.

Measured on two projects (claude/haiku): a 25-file Go TUI and a 23-file Vite + TS 7 + Bun web app each cost about $0.08–0.10 in total, documents, plan, files, checks and fix rounds included, in 7–8 minutes. Known limits: the use phase reads screens as text (terminal cells, the accessibility tree and measured layout, the Android hierarchy): colors and pixel-level looks still need your eye (the screenshots are kept for that); native desktop toolkits (GTK, Qt, Flutter desktop) and iOS have no driver; library APIs the model knows badly can be invented (a fix round usually repairs them).

Principles are presets too, appended to STACK.md as ## Engineering principles (one ### each): SOLID, Clean Code, one file one responsibility, hexagonal, testing, error handling and security are checked by default; Clean Architecture, DDD, Twelve-Factor, observability, API design, performance, functional core, accessibility, git workflow and documentation are one Space away. The model writing a STACK.md only gets their names (to keep the layout consistent); their text is the preset's.

  • Never in silence: a document that is there is replaced only after a yes, and the old one goes to the trash (<leader>Fu brings it back). A file with unsaved changes in a buffer is never written under.
  • The page (s, D, T) is a Markdown float: :w or Ctrl-s sends it, q closes it; what you wrote is kept for the session, so closing it or a failed request loses nothing.
  • Linked: creating STACK.md, DESIGN.md or SPEC.md adds its line to AGENTS.md's "Read first".
  • Language: the documents are written in settings.agent.kit.language (English by default) whatever language you write the description in: the model translates it and keeps names and domain terms. Measured with Haiku: Spanish drafts came back fully in English (they came back mixed before the rule was moved to the end of the request).
  • Facts vs choices: AGENTS.md and SPEC.md never invent (a gap is a TODO: or an open question); STACK.md and DESIGN.md are choices, so the model makes them, marked (proposed) with the reason, and keeps TODO: for what only you know.
  • Commands come from what the project declares: the Makefile's targets first, then the lockfile's manager (npm/pnpm/yarn/bun, uv/poetry/pdm/pip, Go, Flutter/Dart, Cargo, Maven/Gradle wrappers, Swift, Deno, Composer, Bundler) and only the tools a manifest names (no pytest or ruff guessed).
  • Cost: the model ones run once on the main tier (kit_agents, kit_stack, kit_design, kit_spec, reassignable in settings.agent.tiers), through the spend guard and into the history (<leader>ah); presets and A/c/p cost 0 tokens.
  • Your own presets: drop a Markdown file in lua/agent/kit/presets/design/, stack/<category>/ (a new folder is a new category), addons/<group>/ (<!-- for: ts web --> says which stacks it fits: tags from the stack's words, presets.tags) or principles/, starting with <!-- name: … --> and <!-- desc: … --> (<!-- core: true --> checks a principle by default). scripts/test-kit.sh checks every preset has its document's sections.

Merge conflicts (<leader>Gx…, inside a repository)

git-conflict.nvim already chooses ours / theirs / both and jumps between conflicts (<leader>Go T B N n p l). The agent adds what it cannot do:

KeyWhat it does
xttrivial conflicts for free (0 tokens): both sides made the same change, or (with merge.conflictStyle = diff3) only one side changed anything; trailing whitespace does not count
xrthe conflict under the cursor (or the next one) to the agent
xRevery conflict of the file (the trivial ones first, for free): ONE confirmation with the estimate, one proposal per conflict, reviewed in the proposal queue (], [, Y)
xfthe files git lists as unmerged, with how many conflicts each holds
xsgit add of the current file once no marker is left (asks first)

Each conflict is sent with the story of the merge, read from git and never changed: which operation is in progress (merge, rebase, cherry-pick, revert) and which branches, what each side did (commit subjects touching that file), and what each side changed in the file against the common ancestor (stages 1, 2 and 3 of the index). The result has no markers; where both sides changed the same thing differently it combines them and says how in the note, and it never drops a side's change silently. Nothing is written until you accept the inline diff. Knobs: settings.agent.conflict (conflicts per batch, characters of each diff, commit subjects).

Doctor (<leader>aX): the app does not build, does not start, or dies

A troubleshooting agent for failures that are not one more squiggle in a function: a build that breaks, a server that will not start, a crash at runtime, a project that is off before you compile. Hand it the failure the easy way:

KeyEvidence
xxthe best at hand: the last terminal if it holds errors, else the quickfix list, else the clipboard, else it asks you to describe it
xpthe clipboard (copy the error, press this)
xca command it runs for you, captured: the project's own build/test/run commands, docker compose logs, or one you type (a command that passes says so and stops)
xdin your own words (a page to write on); the last terminal rides along when it looks like errors
xlthe end of a log file of the project (or any path)
xs(visual) the selected lines
xhbefore compiling: what the language servers already flag in the project plus every free check below
xrreopens the last case of this project

How it works, in this order, and what each step costs:

  1. Evidence, free. Colors off, repeated lines folded into a count, secrets blacked out (passwords, tokens, API keys, bearer headers, user:pass@host, AWS/GitHub/Slack keys, private keys), the long middle cut so the lines around the first errors and the end of the output survive, the stack frames that point into the project turned into code excerpts (Go, Node, Python, Rust, Java… file:line; a Python traceback puts the culprit first; node_modules, vendor, venvs and the standard library are skipped), the toolchain versions the project uses (Node, Go, Python, Rust, Java, Dart, Docker…) and what git says changed lately (status, last commits, the size of the uncommitted diff: "it worked before" starts there).
  2. Known causes, free. A table of the usual suspects matched on the output (a missing module, a port in use, the Docker daemon down, ECONNREFUSED to a well-known database port, a wrong Node/Go version, a missing go.sum entry, no C compiler…) plus checks of the project itself: dependencies not installed, a lockfile older than package.json, .env missing keys its example has, the Node/Go version the project pins against the one installed, Docker not running, a merge or rebase in progress. They are shown before any model with the command that fixes them.
  3. The model, once. ⏎ on “Ask the model” shows what is sent (sizes, the model, about how many tokens) and asks. One request reads the evidence, finds the root cause (the first error, not the cascade) and writes ordered steps. If it cannot conclude it may ask to read a file, search for a string, list a folder or run one read-only command, at most twice more; that is all it can do: no shell (a command is split into words and checked against an allowlist of diagnostics: versions, package lists, git status/log/diff, docker ps/logs, lsof -i :PORT…), no pipes or redirects, and secret files (.env, keys, .ssh, .aws, .npmrc…) are never readable; every refusal goes back to the model as the result. p shows the exact text that would be sent.
  4. Steps. A step is a command (⏎ runs it captured, in the project folder: its output and exit code come back into the case; t runs it in the integrated terminal for interactive things and servers), an edit (⏎: one proposal with the usual inline diff, accept/reject, the queue; the screen gives way to the code and the step is done when you accept) or a manual thing (Space marks it). Nothing runs without a yes that shows the exact command; a command that destroys or rewrites something (rm -rf, git push --force, git reset --hard, DROP DATABASE, curl | sh…) is marked ! and asked twice.
  5. Check again. r runs the failing command again (or reads the terminal) and says whether it still fails; if it does, a new case starts carrying what was tried, so the model never suggests it twice. a asks a follow-up (what you saw after a step).

The screen is the same full-screen layout as the other markets (list, reading pane, strip; ? lists the keys; narrow terminals show one pane at a time). A case is kept in memory: close it, run something in the terminal, <leader>aXr brings it back. When the model answers after you closed the screen you are told. Knobs: settings.agent.doctor (terminal lines, characters of output sent, stack frames, rounds, command timeout, characters per tool result). Runs are in the history (<leader>ah).

Evals: how good is each model? (make eval)

The claim of the agent is that the pipeline (curated context, strict output, free checks, one bounded retry) lets a modest model deliver work you can accept. evals/ is where that is measured: small projects with a question and a check answered by code (the new code parses, a script asserts its behaviour, nothing outside the target moved, a finding sits near the planted bug), run through the real pipeline against a list of models. It reports pass@1 (one try), pass@k (any of the samples), how often the output was usable, the number of tries and the cost, per action × model. SCAFFOLD=0 measures a model bare, so the table says what the pipeline adds. A free regression test of the harness itself (every case passes its golden answer and fails a do-nothing one) is part of scripts/test-agent.sh. Cases, cassettes and the rules for writing them: evals/README.md. Infrastructure errors (a server that answers 500, a gateway that refuses the account) are counted apart and never as the model failing, a model that errors three times in a row is given up on instead of asked thirty more times, flaky counts the cases whose samples disagree (how predictable the model is), and per pass is what one usable answer costs. The committed baselines (evals/baselines/) become evals/RESULTS.md with make eval-table; make eval BASELINE=… fails when something got worse.

Backend errors are completed with what to do about them (agent/backends/hints.lua: a gateway that blocks the account, an opaque server error, no credit, a rate limit, no login): the message is never replaced, only followed by one line of advice.

Capability profiles: small models get help

Each model belongs to a class (small, medium, strong; agent/profiles.lua), decided by settings.agent.capability.models, then by what make eval PROFILE_OUT=1 measured for it, then by its name (a 7b is small, haiku medium, sonnet strong; an unknown local model is small). What the class changes:

smallmediumstrong
target limit (declined before sending, with what to do)60 lines150the global limit
extra attempts when the answer is unusable211
extra context budget4000 chars6000context.max_chars
server-shaped output (Ollama format, OpenAI response_format)yesnono
an instruction that is several things runs as a chain of single stepsyesnono

An unusable answer is one the free checks reject: no code block, findings in the wrong format, or code whose syntax errors the file did not have before (a treesitter parse of the file as it would be, agent/syntax.lua, no server needed). The same job asks again with the model's answer and the reason in the prompt; the usage of every attempt is added up, so history and budget see the real bill. With settings.agent.cascade.enabled, a model that is down, still unusable after its repairs, or measured to fail the action hands the job to the main model (or cascade.to) and says so; it is off by default because it spends where you did not choose to. Measured advice per action: below 40% of the cases a model is declined for that action, below 90% it gets two repairs (3 cases or more are needed to say anything). settings.agent.capability = { enabled, measured, models, small, medium, strong } and cascade = { enabled, to }.

Chain of steps (agent/decompose.lua, for refactor and implement): an instruction the user typed as a list, as ;-separated clauses or with "then" is split for free (no model) into at most five steps, and the job asks for one edit at a time over the code the previous step produced, each checked like any answer (repairs included). The last step becomes the proposal, diffed against the ORIGINAL code, so accepting, <leader>au and verification behave as always; the budget guard and the preflight see the whole chain (2 steps). It costs the steps, so only the small class chains by default. Measured on Claude Haiku (a medium model) with make eval FILTER=three-edits DECOMPOSE=1|0 SAMPLES=5: 100% either way, 2.5 calls and about twice the cost with the chain, which is why it is not on for models that already do multi-part edits; whether it pays for a 1.5B-7B local model is what the same command is for.

Trust: what you are told, and taking it back

  • Before: the spinner carries the estimate (~1.8k tok); trust.preflight = "notify" prints the whole line first (claude/haiku · light · reads rules, types · ~1.8k tok · ~$0.0007); trust.preview = true shows the start of the message and asks before anything leaves.
  • After: the proposal's hint says whether anything checks it: checked on accept (LSP), checked on save (build), comment only · code untouched, or unverified · no language server. Findings and answers are labelled advice. When a server is attached but says nothing after you accept, the line is accepted · unverified, never "no problems".
  • Spend: the statusline shows what this session cost next to its cap ($0.031/$2.00, in the warning colour near the cap); it counts in memory, it never reads the history on a redraw.
  • Undo: <leader>au takes the last run back. Every accepted proposal is remembered with the lines before and after and where they are now, and the proposals of one batch (fix all, accept all, a plan) are one group across files. A range you edited after the agent wrote it is left alone and reported. The restore is an ordinary edit: u undoes the undo. The build has its own undo, <leader>aSbu: every file aSb writes is journaled (path, hash of what was written, and where the file it replaced went in the trash); undo brings the originals back, deletes what it created and the folders it made, and never touches a file you changed since (the hash no longer matches). Only the last build of a project is kept (agent/kit/build/journal.lua).

.leader/: how Leader worked on this project (offline, yours)

A build that ends, an agent action and your decision about its proposal leave one line each in <project>/.leader/events.jsonl, so Leader can be measured and improved from real use: how builds end, which checks fail and how often, what a model costs per file, which actions you accept. Nothing is ever sent anywhere (no module of the folder touches the network or starts a process; a test checks it). It records how Leader works, never what the person is working on:

  • Recorded: counts, sizes, durations, costs, models, outcomes; a file is a salted hash plus its extension and a size bucket (<50, <200, <500, 500+); a project's own script in a check is custom.
  • Never recorded: code, documents (SPEC/STACK/DESIGN), prompts, model answers, file or folder names, type or function names, error or diagnostic text, instructions you typed, environment, name or machine.
  • Enforced, not promised: every event goes through a schema (agent/leaderdata/schema.lua). A field it does not name is dropped, a value of the wrong type is dropped, a word outside the allowed list becomes other, an id is 8 hex digits. The README the folder generates for itself is built from that same schema, so the list of what is recorded cannot drift from what is. A test builds a project full of marker strings (names, documents, code, answers) and checks none appears anywhere in the folder.
  • Files: README.md (what is and is not recorded, how to read, switch off and delete it), events.jsonl, salt (random: the hashes are useless without it and it is never exported: share events.jsonl alone), .gitignore (*: never committed by accident).
  • When: the builder creates the folder; agent actions write to it only if it already exists, so a keypress never gives a project a folder.
  • Read it: <leader>aSl opens the report: builds and outcomes, cost per build, how many needed fix rounds, which checks fail most, files by kind and model, accept rate and cost per agent action. Switch off: settings.agent.leader_data.enabled = false or LEADER_NO_DATA=1 (the folder is then never created or written). Delete: remove the folder; nothing depends on it. max_kb (2048) caps the file: past it the oldest half goes.

Stable and experimental

The core is what is tested on every release and measured by the evals: fix, docs, refactor, review, explain, ask, implement, tests, the commit panel, the project documents and the build from them (<leader>aSb, recipes). The rest still finds its shape and stays out of the menus until settings.agent.experimental = true (or <leader>Sp → Agent): DDD, repository, service and lens menus, the doctor and multi-file plans. They keep working and say · experimental when shown. The list and the reason for each is agent/stability.lua; a feature moves by changing that table. scripts/gen-cheatsheet.sh shows everything (NVIM_EXPERIMENTAL=1).

Settings (settings.agent, overridable in local.lua)

backend, order, light_backend, history, cache, experimental, capability, cascade, trust, language (of explanations, answers and findings; English by default), rules, timeout_ms, tiers, context.*, apply.format (formats the range with conform on accept), keys (accept/reject), claude.models/model/light_model/prompt_cache/max_cost_usd, opencode.*, ollama.*, openai.*, custom.

Extending

I want...Do
another backend (another CLI)lua/agent/backends/<n>.lua with the contract of backends/init.lua + NAMES; without code: an entry in agent.custom
another actionlua/agent/actions/<n>.lua with the contract of actions/init.lua + NAMES; the menu and the cheatsheet come out by themselves
an eval casea folder evals/<action>/<name>/ with case.json, the files and cassettes/<action>/<name>/golden.json (rules: evals/README.md)
another kind of outputlua/agent/output/<n>.lua (done and optional stream/release) + NAMES
another context providerlua/agent/context/providers/<n>.lua (cap, collect(target, done)) and use it in the context of an action

The format the model must return (code block, L<n>|sev|msg) lives in lua/agent/protocol.lua next to its parser: the prompt rules and how they are read always change together.

Pitfalls

  • Generated files (*.g.dart, *.freezed.dart, *.gr.dart, *.mocks.dart, protobuf; list in utils/generated.lua) are rewritten on the next build: actions that edit (replace output) refuse to run there, explain/ask still work. They are also left out of types, code search and the architecture levels' searches.
  • If the model disobeys and sends several code blocks (draft + corrected version, 2 out of 6 times in a test with Rust), the last one that starts like the target is used and the hint warns: model sent 2 blocks, check the diff.
  • The accept/reject hint shortens in narrow windows (it drops tokens/cost first) and the spinner switches to a compact version.
  • The colors of the diff, the spinner, the hint and the window are derived from the active theme's palette with guaranteed minimum contrast (4.5:1 for text) and are recomputed when the theme changes (<leader>S); tested with the 97 base46 themes and the terminal-inherited theme. Findings use the diagnostic colors of your theme.
  • A visual selection is taken as whole lines.
  • Esc rejects the proposal while one is visible in the buffer (only then; settings.agent.keys changes it).
  • A model may return an empty block: it means deleting that range (e.g. fix removing unused imports).
  • needs-import: after the block is shown in the hint; imports are not added by themselves.
  • scripts/test-agent.sh runs the tests for parsers, target, local backends and setup (no network or tokens: the Ollama/OpenAI servers are fake, scripts/fake-llm.py).

Fix: two levels

  • <leader>af fix inline: put the cursor on the line with the diagnostic (the underline/block from LSP, linter or review). The target is only the diagnostic's lines (if it spans more than agent.context.diagnostic_lines, only what starts there). It carries as context: the full diagnostic, the agent.context.around lines before and after within its function (<around>, read-only), the signature of what it calls (<callees>) and the definitions of the objects it points to: for each identifier in the diagnostic (and the object of an x.Field access) the LSP is asked for its typeDefinition, so the model sees type Note struct even if the code does not name the type (n := load(); n.Titl). If the real fix lives elsewhere (an import, a missing declaration) it says so in needs-import: / note: instead of editing outside the lines. In visual mode, the selection is the target.
  • <leader>aF fix block: the target is the whole function (or the selection) and it fixes all its diagnostics at once, with the same type definitions. Use it when several diagnostics share a cause or the fix touches several lines.

Tests (<leader>aT) and lenses (<leader>aL)

Two menus of their own so <leader>a does not fill up. Both run through the same pipeline (instruction field, inline diff, ⏎/⎋, history and cost) and are designed to spend the minimum: whatever can be decided without a model is decided beforehand.

Tests (agent/tests/): they do not replace the function, they append to the end of the test file.

KeyWhat it doesHow it saves
w writetests for the function under the cursor (or the selection), only what is missing: main case, boundaries and each error branch, max. 6 cases, in the file's stylethe target sent is one line (the end of the test file), the model returns only what is new; from the test file only the header, names, the tests that already cover that function and, if there are none, ONE example travel; a function of <4 lines is rejected before calling
m mocka hand-written fake for the interface/class under the cursor (records calls, returns what the test sets)light model
f fixfixes a failing testruns the test first (go test -run, vitest/jest -t, pytest -k, cargo test) and only if it fails sends its real output; if it passes, 0 tokens. If the code is at fault, it leaves the test as is and says so in note:
g gapsquickfix with the public functions in the folder that no test nameslocal, 0 tokens (approximate map: a short name can give a false "covered")
  • The test file comes from the language's convention (agent/tests/convention.lua): Go x_test.go; TS/JS x.test.ts (or .spec. if the folder already uses it; vitest/jest/mocha according to package.json); Python test_x.py (in tests/ if it exists); Lua x_spec.lua; Rust, a mod x_tests in the same file; Dart lib/ → test/; Java/Kotlin src/main → src/test.
  • When done, the test file opens to the side with the proposal (⏎ accepts, ⎋ rejects); accepting only changes the buffer, you save. A new file is not created (nor its folder) until you save it.
  • fix only knows how to run Go, TS/JS (vitest/jest), Python and Rust; in another language it warns without spending anything.
  • Change the model: agent.tiers.test_write = "light" (or test_mock, test_fix).

Lenses (agent/lenses/): reviews with a single focus, which come out as diagnostics with their origin ([security]) on the exact line. af/aF fix them, and repeating a lens only replaces the findings of that lens (not those of another one or of review).

KeyLooks forModel
s securityuntrusted input reaching a sink (SQL/command/template injection, traversal, SSRF, XSS), authentication/authorization, secrets, cryptography, deserializationmain
p performanceN+1, repeated work, quadratic algorithms, copies and allocations in hot paths, unbounded memorylight
c concurrencyraces and unsynchronized shared state, deadlocks, leaking goroutines/tasks, unawaited promises, double closesmain
e errorsignored or context-less errors, swallowed in catch, resources not released on the error path, half-done statelight
a apiuncommitted changes that break whoever uses it (signatures, return, errors, nil, tags)light
  • Each lens has a gate: if the code contains nothing of its topic (a function without threads for c, without I/O for s, without changes for a) it warns skipped (0 tokens) and does not call the model.
  • Minimal context per lens (rules and signatures of what it calls; c adds types; a adds the diff): the output is ≤ 8 lines.
  • Adding a lens = one file in agent/lenses/actions/ (key, title, focus, gate) and its name in NAMES.
  • Change the model of one: agent.tiers.lens_security = "light".

Features · docs/cheatsheets/skills.md

Agent skills

SKILL.md files the agents load on demand (OpenCode skill tool in .agents/skills; tool-less backends receive ONE body as a context section). Leader manages them for you: no manual copying, no question about what to install. Keys: CHEATSHEET.md (Skills menu).

Marketplace

<leader>aK + o (or <leader>Sp → Skills → Marketplace) opens the full-screen marketplace (same widget as the SPEC.md modules):

  • Left: skills by group: Installed, Suggested for your model, Catalog (skills.sh + offline list). ■ on, □ off, + suggested, ○ catalog.
  • Right: what it is and its instructions, plus where it lives (~/.config/opencode/skills/... or .agents/skills/...).
  • Bottom: counts (N ON · M OFF · model small/medium/strong) and what A will do.
KeyWhat
Space / xInstall a suggested/catalog skill (writes .agents/skills) or toggle an installed one on/off
eEdit its SKILL.md in a split over the marketplace (coming back, the market is still there)
nNew skill: id → trigger line → instructions
dDelete to Leader's trash (30 days, <leader>Fu brings the last one back)
AApply every suggestion for your model (0-config, no question)
/Search the catalog (npx skills find when Node is there, offline curated list otherwise)
⏎ / lRead it (Esc/h goes back), ? keys, Esc/q close

You never leave the view to add, edit or delete: after each action the list refreshes where you were.

0-configuración (infiera, no pregunte)

skills/infer.lua (pure, tested) decides from what you already have:

  1. Your model (agent.selection + agent.profiles): a small model gets the token-savers first (caveman, caveman-commit); a medium gets caveman; a strong gets only stack skills.
  2. Your stack + environment (project markers): go.mod → go-pro, test-master; *.sln/*.csproj → dotnet-pro (C#/.NET); pubspec.yaml → flutter-expert; package.json → typescript-pro, react-expert; pyproject.toml → python-pro; Dockerfile → docker-pro, devops-engineer; *.tf → terraform-pro; wrangler.toml → cloudflare-expert; serverless.yml/cdk.json → aws-pro; firebase.json → gcp-pro; netlify.toml → netlify-expert; vercel.json → vercel-expert; azure.yaml → azure-pro; Chart.yaml → kubernetes-specialist. C# and cloud/deploy are first-class: the environment with no inferred skill is exactly where the agent invents flags and commands.
  3. What is already there: installed+on is keep, installed+off is enable, missing is install, disabled by you (skills.disabled) is skip.

A (or <leader>aKs) applies it with npx skills add <repo> --skill <id> -a opencode -y (global adds -g); without npx it clones the repo with git and copies the dir in. Nothing is installed without you pressing A/install: never a package manager in silence (same rule as the welcome screen).

Sources (marketplaces)

  • skills.sh CLI (npx skills find/add/list/check/update/remove): live search and install for 75+ agents, project by default, -g global.
  • HTTP catalogs (OpenCode 2 skills array): https://opencodeskills.dev/catalog/ and https://skills.sh/ are added in settings.skills.sources when you give one.
  • Offline curated (skills/store.CURATED): languages (typescript-pro, python-pro, go-pro, flutter-expert, dotnet-pro, php-pro, ruby-pro, elixir-pro, rust-pro-adjacent via stack), environments/deploys (docker-pro, kubernetes-specialist, terraform-pro, aws-pro, gcp-pro, cloudflare-expert, netlify-expert, vercel-expert, azure-pro, devops-engineer) and the always-on (caveman, code-reviewer, test-master, postgres-pro, api-designer, security-reviewer) — always searchable with no network.

How each backend uses them

  • OpenCode (agent/backends/opencode.lua): skill tool is on when skills.enabled (it lists only id+description per step, so small models are not drowned; the body loads on demand).
  • Claude / Ollama / OpenAI (tool-less): agent/context/providers/skills.lua injects ONE relevant body (skills.max_chars, default 1200) matched by language + file + action words. The provider is silent with no match or when off.

On/off per skill lives in its frontmatter (metadata: { opencode/autoinvoke: false } = dormant), where the model will not see it. skills-lock.json (project root) records where each one came from.

Settings

skills = { enabled, auto, scope (project|global), max_chars, disabled, sources } in core/settings.lua (or <leader>Sp → Skills). auto = false turns suggestions off (the marketplace still works).

Tolerance (repos oldie / legacy / ya trabajados)

Leader detects and adjusts, never overwrites (skills/adopt.lua, pure + collect):

  • Ya hay skills: se conservan y no se duplican (store.list deduplica: proyecto gana sobre global; create se niega si existe). Las inválidas sin description se listan con ⚠ y no se anuncian al modelo; editarlas con e las arregla.
  • Ya hay agents: AGENTS.md/CLAUDE.md/.agent.md/.agents/.claude/.opencode se detectan: CLAUDE.md sin AGENTS.md sugiere <leader>aSc (enlaza, no crea de cero); AGENTS.md existente nunca se sobrescribe (kit confirma + papelera).
  • Ya hay docs: STACK.md/DESIGN.md/SPEC.md/PROGRESS.md se detectan: Stack/Design/Spec ya documentados se ajustan con <leader>aS, no se recrean.
  • Legacy sin manifests modernos: solo token-savers (caveman) + keep, sin forzar skills de stack (adopt.adjust). Un repo con Makefile/muchos archivos y ningún go.mod/package.json/… se marca legacy, no se le propone infra nueva.
  • Verlo: la franja del marketplace muestra Detected: ... (hints del repo) y <leader>aKd abre el doctor con todo lo detectado y un consejo por cosa.

Tests

scripts/test-skills.sh (pure parsing/inference, discovery+CRUD on temp dirs, tolerance, market windows/keys/back navigation, menu and provider shape). No network.

Features · docs/cheatsheets/herdr.md

Herdr

Herdr is the terminal workspace that runs the coding agents: every agent lives in a real pane with its shell, its logs and its process. Leader talks to it through its CLI, so you can see what every agent in the workspace is doing without leaving the editor, and Herdr can see what your nvim is doing.

Keys: CHEATSHEET.md (section Herdr). Nothing here costs tokens: it only reads.

The panel (<leader>Wa)

The breakdown of the workspace:

  • One row per agent, grouped by workspace: its state (● working, ○ idle, ! blocked, ✓ done), the agent (opencode, claude, codex…) and where it is (~/Work/leader).
  • The pane on the right is the agent under the cursor: its own words (the transcript of its terminal) and why Herdr thinks it is in that state (the detection rule that matched, e.g. progress_bar_working).
  • It refreshes on its own every herdr.observe_ms (2 s), so the state you read is the state now.
KeyDoes
j kmove
Enterread the agent under the cursor
fbring its pane to the front in Herdr
ssend it a prompt (the text you type is submitted to it)
xthe report of how Herdr detected its state
rrefresh now
qclose

An agent that is working can only be read as it is drawn on screen (its scrollback is only captured when it goes idle), so the panel shows the visible screen then and the transcript once it settles.

Setup (<leader>Ws)

What Herdr is on this machine, and what to do about it:

  • The binary: not installed? The setup offers to install it (curl -fsSL https://herdr.dev/install.sh | sh, the PowerShell one on Windows) in a floating terminal. Nothing is installed without pressing the button.
  • The server: running or not, its version, its socket.
  • This nvim: whether it is inside a Herdr pane, and whether it registers itself as an agent (see below).
  • The integrations: herdr integration install <agent> makes an agent (Claude, OpenCode, Codex…) report its own state to Herdr and come back after a server restart. The setup lists the ones that are missing or need repair.

Settings (<leader>Sp → Herdr)

SettingWhat it does
Herdrauto (the binary decides), on, off
Register as an agentauto (inside Herdr), on, off
Setupthe menu above

In lua/local.lua:

return { herdr = { enabled = "auto", register = "auto", observe_ms = 2000, lines = 150 } }

The old knob still works: session = { herdr = true } (or false) in local.lua decides the registration when herdr.register is left at auto.

nvim as an agent of the workspace

When Leader registers itself in Herdr (register), the pane shows up in Herdr's Agents panel and says what it is doing: working while the inline agent runs (with the action's name), blocked while a proposal waits for you to accept it, and idle when nothing runs. It never takes the pane from a real agent that already occupies it.

With herdr >= 0.9.5 the registration also carries a resume command (nvim +HerdrResume, which restores the folder's session), so Herdr brings the pane back with your session after the server restarts. Older versions (0.9.x) do not know that option: the registration is made without it, so Herdr cannot bring the pane back — the panel says so.

Handing work to another agent

<leader>ag (the agent menu) sends file:range + your instruction to an agent that is already running in a Herdr pane, with the diagnostics of the range and the current code when the file has unsaved changes. It only appears inside Herdr. See agent.md.

Features · docs/cheatsheets/editing.md

Undo and clipboard

Undo and paste behave closer to VS Code without changing how Vim works underneath. Nothing runs in the background, and nothing is loaded until you use it. Keys: CHEATSHEET.md (sections History and Clipboard).

Undo word by word

In plain Vim, u undoes a whole insert at once: type a paragraph, press u, and all of it is gone. Here one insert becomes several undo steps. A new step starts:

  • after each space or tab (one u = one word),
  • after a new line (Enter, a snippet that adds lines, a paste),
  • after a pause in typing (settings.history.pause_ms, 1 s by default).

. still repeats the whole insert, and a macro's insert is still a single step. To get Vim's behaviour back, turn off Undo word by word in the settings panel (<leader>Sp) or set history.word_undo = false.

KeyWhat it does
u, Ctrl+Zundo; the lines that changed glow for a moment
Ctrl+R, U, Ctrl+Yredo
Ctrl+Z in insert modeundo without leaving insert mode

Ctrl+Z never suspends nvim. U replaces Vim's rarely used "undo line", and Ctrl+Y replaces "scroll one line up". history.redo_keys = false keeps Vim's meanings.

Going back in time

  • <leader>uu opens the undo tree (Neovim's built-in :Undotree) in a side panel. Moving the cursor in it moves the file through its history, branches included (what you undid and then overwrote is still there). q closes it.
  • <leader>u[ / <leader>u] take the file to how it was at the previous / next save (:earlier 1f / :later 1f). This is VS Code's Timeline, and it is undoable too: <leader>u[ then u gets your edits back.

Undo history is saved with the file (undofile), so all of this works after you restart nvim.

The clipboard keeps what you copied

With settings.clipboard.keep (on by default), only y (copy) and d (cut) fill the clipboard:

  • x, X, c, C delete without copying (changing a word does not lose what you copied);
  • pasting over a selection (viw then p) keeps the clipboard, so you can paste the same text again over the next one.

An explicit register still works as usual: "ax puts the character in register a. <leader>d deletes without copying with any motion. Turn it off with x / c keep the clipboard in the settings panel.

Yank history

Every copy and cut is kept, newest first, without duplicates. The list survives restarts (stdpath("state")/yanks.json).

  • Right after p / P, [y swaps what you pasted for the previous copy, and ]y goes back to a newer one. The count and p/P are kept, and your registers are not touched.
  • <leader>uy lists them all with a preview. Enter pastes the copy after the cursor and makes it the clipboard again, so the next p repeats it. <C-x> forgets one entry, and <leader>ux clears the whole list.

Settings: history.yanks.max (50 entries), max_chars (longer texts are not kept), persist (false = memory only).

Pitfalls

  • WSL / SSH: text copied outside nvim. Copying from nvim reaches the system clipboard (OSC 52). Pasting from the system clipboard is Ctrl+Shift+V in the terminal, because the terminal does not let nvim read it (p pastes nvim's own clipboard).
  • [y only works right after a paste. Once anything else changes the buffer it tells you to paste first.

Features · docs/cheatsheets/panes.md

Panes

A pane is a real Neovim window, not a plugin. Alt+V opens one to the right and Alt+H one below with the start screen in it; ff / fo from that screen load the chosen file in that pane. The window you were in keeps its file, so you can work with two, three or more files at once and still have a dashboard in any of them.

The keys are in CHEATSHEET.md (the Panes group). This page is the workflow.

The mental model

  • Every split is a pane. No extra layer, no tabs: the same windows, buffers and LSP clients as always; sessions, diagnostics, the agent and the terminal work inside them without knowing they are in one.
  • The dashboard is per pane. The start screen keeps one selection, one layout and one set of window options per window, so a dashboard in a narrow split shrinks itself and the one in the big split stays as it was. Several can be open at once; opening a file replaces only the dashboard of the pane it was opened from.
  • Search from the pane, land in the pane. Find File, Recent Files, Find Word and the rest run in the window that owns the dashboard. Telescope opens its picker over the editor; choosing a file closes the picker and puts the file where the dashboard was.
  • The dashboard is not a mode. Once a file is loaded the pane is a normal window: Ctrl+h/j/k/l moves, :split works, the file tree and the agent use it like any other.

Keys and mouse

  • Alt+V / Alt+H: new pane right / below with the start screen. They also work from terminal mode: they leave it and open the pane, so nothing gets typed into the running shell.
  • Alt+T: a quick terminal pane below the current one (one shell per tab). Esc closes it and leaves you in the file in normal mode; Alt+T brings the same shell back. See terminal.md.
  • Ctrl+h/j/k/l (NvChad's window keys): move the focus. It also works from a terminal in Normal mode (Ctrl+x).
  • Alt+Left/Right/Up/Down: make the current pane narrower / wider / shorter / taller. The same border can be dragged with the mouse when mouse is on. The step is a setting (<leader>Sp → Panes → Resize step).
  • Alt+Z: zoom the pane (a tab of its own, keeping the real layout in the other tab); the same key comes back.
  • Alt+Q: close the current pane. The last one stays; a buffer with unsaved changes is not thrown away.
  • <leader>VX: close every other pane of the tab and keep the current one (buffers stay open).
  • <leader>V: the menu with the same actions plus list (jump to a pane), equal sizes, rotate and swap with next. The desktop app's View › Panes has the same items.
  • Layout presets (<leader>V): 2 two columns, 3 three columns, m a wide main pane with two stacked on the right, 4 a 2x2 grid. The file you are editing stays in the first pane and the new ones open on the start screen. On a tab that already has several panes it asks before replacing the layout; the buffers stay open, so unsaved work is never lost.
  • Terminals: Alt+I is still the floating terminal; Alt+V/Alt+H no longer toggle terminals. Use <leader>Tv / <leader>Th for a vertical / horizontal terminal split, and <leader>Tt / <leader>Tn for the command terminal and a new shell.

Workflows

  • Two files side by side: Alt+V, then ff and pick the second file; the first pane keeps the file you were editing. Alt+V again from either pane adds a third. For a typical layout without opening panes one by one, use <leader>V2, <leader>V3, <leader>Vm or <leader>V4.
  • A dashboard that stays: open the dashboard with Alt+V and use the other pane to edit. When you want another file, go back to the dashboard pane and pick it; the pane you were editing is untouched.
  • A full-screen look: Alt+Z on the pane you are reading, Alt+Z again to return to the split.
  • A pane that got too narrow: Alt+Right a few times, or <leader>V= to make them all equal.
  • Find a pane: <leader>Vl lists every pane with its file, size and whether it has unsaved changes; Enter focuses it.
  • The pane list also shows terminals and the tree: they are windows too; entering one just moves the focus.

Pitfalls

  • A pane is not a tab. Tabs are not shown (showtabline = 0) and are only used by Alt+Z; :tabnew is still Neovim's own thing and is not what the pane keys use.
  • A terminal pane is not killed by closing its window. Alt+Q/Esc close the window and the shell keeps running; <leader>Tl lists it and brings it back.
  • The start screen does not survive a session restore as a dashboard. It is an unlisted scratch buffer; sessions save the windows and the files, and a dashboard pane comes back empty (open it again with Alt+V or <leader>Vd).
  • A terminal pane comes back as a fresh shell. Sessions include terminals (terminal in sessionoptions): the window, its place and its working directory return, but the scrollback and whatever was running do not. The session is saved on exit and every minute (<leader>Sp → Editing → Session autosave), and a bare leader inside a project (or leader <folder>) restores it automatically; <leader>Px skips saving that run.
  • Resizing is per window, not per column. Alt+Left/Alt+Right change the current window's width; the neighbor gets the difference. If the pane is already at the minimum, nothing moves.

Features · docs/cheatsheets/tasks.md

Project tasks

The targets of the project's Makefile in one picker, run in the integrated terminal. It asks make, so it costs 0 tokens and nothing stays running. Keys: CHEATSHEET.md (section Run).

It works with a Makefile and only with a Makefile. Without one, every key says so and does nothing, except <leader>Rc, which creates one.

Which Makefile

The nearest GNUmakefile, makefile or Makefile from the current file's folder up to the git root, never above it (the ~/Makefile of the machine is not your project's). In a monorepo, a Makefile in the subfolder wins over the root's.

What is a task

A target is listed when it is .PHONY or has a description. Descriptions come from the file's text:

.PHONY: build test

build: ## Build everything        <- inline description
test: build
	go test ./...

# Remove the build output            <- a comment right above works too
clean:
	rm -rf out

.PHONY targets declared in included files are found too (the list comes from make -qp, which prints make's database and runs nothing). Without GNU make (Windows, BSD) only the file's text is parsed. A Makefile with neither .PHONY nor descriptions lists every plain target.

Picker

KeyDoes
Enterrun the target
Ctrl-aask for arguments, then run (PKG=./x, -j8)
Ctrl-pdry run: make -n, prints what it would do
Ctrl-rread the Makefile again (the list is cached until it changes)

The dot marks the default goal; <leader>Rd runs a bare make.

Create a Makefile (<leader>Rc)

Only where there is none. Pick a language (the one the project looks like comes first: go.mod, Cargo.toml, package.json, pubspec.yaml…) and then a preset, with the file as preview. It is written to the project root (the git root) and opened. It never overwrites a Makefile.

LanguagePresets
Gobasic, service (live reload, race, coverage, image), cli (cross-compile), library (bench, coverage report)
Pythonvenv, uv, fastapi, django
Rustbasic, full (watch, audit, coverage, bench)
TypeScript / Nodenpm, pnpm, nextjs
Dart / Flutterflutter, dart
Javamaven, gradle
Kotlingradle (ktlint, detekt)
C / C++c, cpp (sources in src/, objects in obj/), cmake
Dockercompose, image
Shellbasic (shellcheck, shfmt, bats)

Every preset has a help target (make help prints each target and what it does) and descriptions on all of them, so the picker shows them at once.

Add your own: drop presets/<language>/<name>.mk in nvim/lua/tasks/ (a new folder is a new language). The first line is a # description comment (it stays in the created Makefile), every target has ## text, recipes use tabs. scripts/test-tasks.sh runs a real make over every preset.

To update one instead (or to fit it to the repo as it is, with a model), use <leader>aSM (Project documents → Makefile): one retroactive call that reads languages, manifests and commands and rewrites it, keeping its targets.

Editing a Makefile

Makefiles get their own treesitter parser and checkmake (pack infra) for the usual mistakes (a target with no .PHONY, no all/clean/test); Neovim keeps real tabs in them. A target documented with ## text on its line is what the picker shows next to its name.

Good to know

  • Targets run in the same terminal as <leader>ao reads, so a failing build can go straight to the agent's "fix from output". <leader>Rq puts the file:line errors of that output in the quickfix for free.
  • If a target is still running, the next one asks before it is typed into it (Stop it and run). <leader>Rs sends Ctrl-C.
  • <leader>Rl repeats the last target of the project (it survives a restart); <leader>Ra edits its arguments first.

Features · docs/cheatsheets/gitignore.md

.gitignore

Writes a .gitignore from what the project is made of. 0 tokens, nothing resident. Keys: CHEATSHEET.md (section Git, ignore rules).

What it looks at

Marker files and extensions under the git root (3 levels deep; settings.git.ignore), never inside dependency or build folders (node_modules/, vendor/, target/… count by name only). go.mod or *.go → Go, package.json or *.ts → Node, pubspec.yaml → Dart/Flutter, Dockerfile → Docker, *.tf → Terraform… Nothing is read from file contents.

The checklist opens with what it found checked and says why (found go.mod +14). Secrets (.env*, keys, certificates, credentials, password databases) and Editor and system (OS files, IDE state, logs) are recommended for every project; Secrets goes first in the file, Editor and system last. Space or x toggles, a toggles all, Enter writes.

With a .gitignore already there

It adds only what is missing and never removes or reorders anything: a rule is not written twice (not even across topics: dist/ is in Node, Python and Go but lands once), a block with nothing new disappears with its comment, and a topic the file already covers is listed as already in .gitignore, unchecked. Each row says how many new rules it would add. It refuses to run while .gitignore has unsaved changes in a buffer.

What git already tracks

Ignoring a file does not untrack it. After writing, it asks git which tracked files the rules now match (git ls-files -ci --exclude-standard) and warns, e.g. a .env that was committed long ago: git rm --cached <file> stops tracking it (the secret stays in the history: rotate it).

Topics

Plain files in lua/gitignore/presets/<name>.gitignore; drop one in and it is discovered:

# Go: compiled binaries, test and profiling output     <- line 1: `Title: what it ignores`, written as the section header
# detect: go.mod go.work *.go                          <- line 2: what says it applies (not written)
# Binaries and build output                            <- rules in blocks with a comment each
/bin/

detect takes file names (go.mod), globs on a whole name (*.go, .env.*), folders with a slash (node_modules/) and always. Rules that people usually commit on purpose are left out or commented (Cargo.lock, pubspec.lock, google-services.json, .vscode/settings.json is kept); the tests check with git check-ignore that none of a long list of files a project needs is ignored by all topics together, and that secrets and junk are.

Tests: scripts/test-gitignore.sh.

Features · docs/cheatsheets/release.md

Release

The steps around a release that are always the same, inside a git repository. Changelog and checklist cost 0 tokens; the PR description is one light-tier request. Nothing is ever tagged, pushed, posted or published from here: those lines are shown for you to run. Keys: CHEATSHEET.md (section Git, release …).

Changelog (c)

Reads the commits since the last tag (all of them before the first tag), in Conventional Commits:

Commits since the tagNext version
a breaking change (feat!:, refactor(api)!:, or BREAKING CHANGE: in the body)major (minor before 1.0)
a featminor
anything elsepatch

The current version is read from VERSION, then package.json, pubspec.yaml, Cargo.toml, pyproject.toml, then the last tag. The confirmation shows the section; Write puts it at the top of CHANGELOG.md (the file is created with a header when missing; a section of the same version is replaced, never duplicated). Groups: Breaking changes, Features, Fixes, Performance, Other changes (each commit once, in the first group it fits); scopes in bold, short hashes at the end. Release commits (chore(release): …) and merges are left out.

Then, by hand: bump the version file, commit, git tag vX.Y.Z, git push --tags.

PR description (p)

The branch against its base (origin/HEAD, else origin/main, origin/master, main, master): its commit subjects, the diff stat and the diff (cut at settings.git.release.pr_max_chars, 12000) go to the light model, which writes Summary · Changes · How to test · Risks from what they show, never inventing tickets or behaviour. The body opens in a split and is copied to the clipboard: paste it in the PR (gh pr create --body "$(…)" or the web form).

Checklist (l)

A buffer of what must be true before releasing, checked where it can be:

  • Repository: working tree clean, ahead/behind the upstream.
  • Quality gates: the commands in STACK.md's ## Quality gates (or the test/lint/format/build ones of ## Commands); ⏎ runs one in the integrated terminal. A command that leaves the machine (push, deploy, publish, release, upload, tag) is listed "by hand" and never runs from here.
  • Version and changelog: whether CHANGELOG.md has the next version (⏎ writes it), what the version file says against the next version (⏎ opens it), the tag line to run.
  • Shipping: the CI configuration found (GitHub, GitLab, Forgejo/Gitea, CircleCI) and STACK.md's Deploy / Release / Hosting / Distribution / CI rows.

r reads it again, q closes.

Pitfalls

  • Subjects that are not Conventional Commits still appear, under Other changes, and never bump more than a patch: keep using the commit panel (<leader>Gc), which validates them.
  • The changelog follows tags: tag every release, or the next changelog includes the previous one's commits.

Features · docs/cheatsheets/github.md

GitHub

A menu for the gh CLI inside a repository (like <leader>G): the TUIs that visualize your work and octo.nvim for editing PRs and issues without leaving Neovim. The keys are in CHEATSHEET.md.

TUIs (gh extensions)

KeyExtensionWhat it shows
dgh-dashPRs and issues in one dashboard: Vim keys, preview, comments, merge
ogh-observerthe checks of the current PR, live, with queue and run times
egh-enhanceActions runs, jobs and logs; rerun the failed ones
ngh-newsnotifications as a TUI (read, mute, snooze, open)

Each one lives in its own terminal (utils/terminal.tui): pressing the key again while it runs only brings it back to the front (the command is never typed into the TUI); Esc closes the window and the TUI keeps running in the background. Reopening is instant. They are Nerd Font TUIs: the icons need a patched font in the terminal.

The first time, if the extension is not installed, Leader shows the exact gh extension install … line and, after you confirm, installs and launches it in one step. <leader>Ghs opens the checklist of every extension in the menu (missing ones checked, installed ones listed): Enter installs the checked ones in the shared terminal; nothing runs before that. By hand:

gh extension install dlvhdr/gh-dash
gh extension install fini-net/gh-observer
gh extension install dlvhdr/gh-enhance
gh extension install chmouel/gh-news

Configuration lives where each tool expects it: gh-dash reads ~/.config/gh-dash/config.yml (and a repo-level .gh-dash.yml); gh-observer auto-detects the PR of the current branch.

octo.nvim (PRs and issues inside Neovim)

p lists PRs, i issues and r starts a review of the current branch's PR. The plugin (octo.nvim) is lazy-loaded on :Octo and uses Telescope (the picker this config already has). Inside an Octo buffer its own keys apply: <CR> lists the actions, <localleader> has comments, labels, reviewers and reactions, and a review opens a tab with the changed files and their diff. Projects v2 needs the read:project scope (gh auth refresh -s read:project).

Core gh commands

KeyWhat it does
vopen the current PR in the browser
cchecks of the current PR (plain table)
bopen the repository in the browser
a / Agh auth status / gh auth login

These run in the shared command terminal, so errors (no PR for the branch, no remote, credentials) stay readable; Esc closes the window without stopping anything.

Pitfalls

  • Everything needs gh installed and logged in: A runs gh auth login in the terminal.
  • gh observer and gh enhance need a PR/branch with Actions; gh-dash and gh-news work from any folder.
  • octo.nvim is installed by lazy on the next start after pulling this config (or :Lazy sync); until then the menu says so.

Languages · docs/cheatsheets/go.md

Go

Tools

WhatWith
LSPgopls (gofumpt, staticcheck, placeholders, analyses: unusedparams, shadow, unusedwrite, useany; inlay hints)
Format (on save and <leader>fm)goimports then gofumpt
Lintgolangci-lint (in light mode: go vet); does not run when leaving insert
Extrago.nvim (GoAddTag, GoFillStruct, GoIfErr, GoAddTest...), treesitter, gomodifytags

<leader>g menu (only in *.go)

The keys are in CHEATSHEET.md (generated; not duplicated here).

  • Struct actions (tags, fill literal, if err, add test) act on the cursor. The tag ones (ga/gA/gD) save the file when done (that is how go.nvim works).
  • In light mode go.nvim does not load: those actions warn instead of failing.
  • run and build run the current file's package when it can run (a main package with func main, so each main.go variant — root, cmd/api, cmd/worker… — runs its own binary) and fall back to the module's main otherwise (the only one, or the preferred cmd/*); without a go.mod they run the single file. They always run from the module root (go run ./cmd/api), so relative files resolve the same way. The rest run from the module root. The terminal keeps the focus (in terminal mode) when the command starts, and Esc closes it without stopping the program.
  • test the package of this file (l) runs go test ./folder from the module root: a folder with main.go and its *_test.go files is tested on its own (a main package with tests works like any other), not the whole module. Without a go.mod it runs in the folder. m/R/c still test everything, with plain, -race and -cover.

Project setup (<leader>gS, lua/gosetup/)

The config files a Go project usually wants, one key each (0 tokens, nothing resident): golangci-lint (.golangci.yml: minimal, standard, strict, v2 format), Air (.air.toml: basic, api with graceful restarts), Dockerfile (alpine or distroless, multi-stage, non-root), .dockerignore, GitHub Actions CI, git hooks (lefthook) and .editorconfig. Sp is a checklist of all of them (what is missing and core is checked), Sm creates a Makefile (same as <leader>Rc) and Sg the .gitignore (<leader>Gi).

  • Filled in from the project, never asked: the module path (golangci's local import prefix), the Go version of go.mod (image tags), where main really is (./cmd/api or .) and the binary's name. The module is the nearest go.mod up from the current file.
  • Never overwrites. A file that is already there (also .golangci.yaml/.toml/.json for golangci-lint) is opened. A misnamed config such as .golangcli.yml (golangci-lint only reads .golangci.yml) is offered a rename with the buffers and servers following; the content is kept.
  • With several variants the picker previews the file exactly as it will be written.
  • golangci-lint v2 configs (version: "2"): a v1 binary refuses them, and the action warns when it finds one.
  • Add a preset: drop presets/<topic>/<variant>.<ext> in lua/gosetup/. Line 1 is # description (stays in the file), line 2 is # target: path/relative/to/the/module (not written). Placeholders: {{module}} {{go}} {{main}} {{binary}}. A new topic (a folder) also needs its entry in TOPICS (gosetup/init.lua): key, label, default variant.
  • Tests: scripts/test-gosetup.sh renders every preset and, where the tools exist, has them check it (golangci-lint v2 config verify, air's own keys from air init, lefthook validate, TOML/YAML parsing).

Struct tags while you type (utils/gotags.lua)

The tag value is derived from the field name: SentAt → sent_at, UserID → user_id, HTTPServer → http_server, Kind → kind.

You type (in a struct field)Suggests
` after the type | json:"kind", json:"kind,omitempty", json:"sentAt" (camelCase if it differs), the gorm:"…" variants for that field and its type (see below), yaml, db, mapstructure, form, toml, xml, json:"-", validate:"required"
json:"kind", "kind,omitempty", camelCase, "-"
json:"kind, kind,omitempty, camelCase, -
json:"kind,omitempty, omitzero, string (options change depending on the key)
gorm:"full values by field and type: primaryKey;type:text, type:text;not null…
gorm:"type:text;loose options you have not used yet: not null, index, size:255, column:title, default:……
type Envelope struct {
    Kind   Kind      `json:"kind"`
    ID     string    `json:"id,omitempty"`
    SentAt time.Time `json:"sent_at"`
}
  • Inside a tag the first item comes preselected: Enter accepts it.
  • The cursor ends up inside the quotes: type , for options, or Tab to leave.
  • Only keys you have not used yet in that tag are offered.
  • It does not trigger in raw strings (query := `SELECT…), return, assignments, etc. (treesitter checks that you are in a struct).

gorm

gorm separates its options with ; (not ,) and some take a value (size:255, column:sent_at). When you open the gorm:" tag, full values are offered, chosen by the field name and type; after a ;, loose options are offered (already used ones are not repeated, and the cursor lands on the value of size:, type:, column:… with Tab to jump).

FieldVariants offered (in this order)
ID stringprimaryKey;type:text, primaryKey, primaryKey;type:uuid
integer IDprimaryKey;autoIncrement, primaryKey
stringtype:text;not null, type:text, not null, type:text;not null;index, uniqueIndex, index, size:255
CreatedAt / UpdatedAt (time.Time)autoCreateTime / autoUpdateTime, not null;auto…, not null, index
other time.Time, gorm.DeletedAtnot null, index / index
boolnot null;default:false, default:false, index
integers and floatsnot null, not null;default:0, default:0, index
[]bytetype:blob, type:bytea
custom type or relation (Author User)foreignKey:AuthorID, embedded, many2many:author
(all)column:<name_in_snake>, -

With a pointer (*string) not null is not offered. gorm tags show up in any .go file.

type NoteModel struct {
    ID        string    `gorm:"primaryKey;type:text"`
    Title     string    `gorm:"type:text;not null;index"`
    Content   string    `gorm:"type:text"`
    CreatedAt time.Time `gorm:"not null"`
}

Model files (*_model.go / model.go, utils/gomodel.lua)
